```facts
Read: A forward set up at the goalmouth with their blade in the shooting lane makes every point shot a probable tip
Technique: Stay tall and patient as long as you can — a low tip hits you if you are big and set, a high one will not if you are already down
Key: Your best chance is the right place with a wide body and a still head, not a heroic reaction
Action: Shout to get the net-front player moved — that is your defenceman's job
```

A tip or deflection changes the puck's direction a few feet in front of you, and no reflex is fast enough to follow it. So you do not try.

- **Watch the sticks, not just the puck.** If a forward is set up at the **goalmouth** — the immediate area directly in front of your net, at the edge of the crease — with their blade in the shooting lane, treat every point shot as a probable tip.
- **Stay tall and patient for as long as you can.** Dropping early on a shot that gets tipped means it goes over you. A tip that stays low will hit you if you are big and set; a tip that goes up will not if you are already down.
- **Expect the puck to change direction and stay balanced.** Your best chance on a tipped puck is being in the right place with a wide body and a still head, not a heroic reaction.
- **Get the net-front player moved.** This is your defenceman's job, and it is the thing to shout about. See the skaters' section below.

---

## Playing the Puck

### The trapezoid — the rule that defines what you can do

```facts
Rule: Playing the puck behind the goal line outside the trapezoid is a two-minute delay-of-game minor (NHL Rules 27.8 and 63.2(viii); IIHF 27.7); the one exception — skate contact with your crease — excuses playing, never freezing: none for a teammate nearby and none for pressure
Rule: 🇬🇧 England Ice Hockey's Rules & Regulations 22.3 say the restricted area is "not currently enforced" in England and Wales — assume it binds and ask your league; 22.3 suspends that rule alone, so IIHF 63.2(I) still bars freezing outside your crease and 63.2(VI)'s Note still allows a save-then-cover
Rule: The determining factor is the position of the puck, not of your body, and the IIHF numbers the trapezoid 27.7 not 27.8 (NHL Rule 27.8; IIHF Rules 27.7 and 63.2(VIII)) — so a puck you stop on the goal line is not behind it, and being hit by one is not playing it (Handbook Situations 27.6 and 27.9)
Key: Whether the KHL has a trapezoid is unverified here — no KHL rulebook was available to check; and how much rec, beer-league and youth hockey has one is a question about leagues, not about books
Rule: Hockey Canada writes no trapezoid at all, and that is not freedom — but read both halves: Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a) clause v bars freezing once you leave the crease, a Minor penalty, no warning, while clause ii permits coming out to cut down the angle and covering after the save
Convention: Hockey Canada never says whether clause v of Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a), no freezing once out of the crease, or clause ii, a cover permitted after the save, governs a save-then-cover outside the crease; treating ii as the exception is a reading, not a ruling. Clause iv is racing a loose puck
Rule: USA Hockey has no trapezoid but restricts where you may freeze the puck, on the same two minutes — and Rule 614(c) has four triggers, not one, and no skate-contact exception; that exception belongs to the trapezoid rule, printed twice (NHL 27.8’s own text and the Note to 63.2(viii))
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 614(c) makes two freezes a minor in terms — with the puck behind the goal line and your body entirely outside the crease (614(c)(1)), and outside the "goalkeeper's privileged" area, which reaches back from the faceoff-dot line but excludes the corners (614(c)(3))
Rule: Crease contact is not the test — the Casebook excuses a cover made prone and fully outside the crease if the puck is in the privileged area in front of the goal line, and penalises one you had time to play with your stick unpressured (USA Hockey Casebook, Rule 614(c) Situations 8 to 12)
Rule: No trapezoid applies in front of the goal line; the bar on participating in the play past the centre red line still does, and NHL Rule 27.7 and IIHF Rule 27.6 judge it on the puck where USA Hockey Rule 407(c) and Hockey Canada Rule 4.13(c) judge it on your skates
Action: Find out which rules you are under before your first shift, and take the trapezoid dimensions from Rink Map and Glossary — NHL Rule 1.8, never NHL 27.8 (stale); IIHF 1.8 and 27.7 give 6.80 m / 8.60 m
```

> **A goalkeeper may not play the puck behind the goal line outside the marked trapezoid.** Doing so is a **two-minute minor for delay of game** (NHL Rules 27.8 and 63.2(viii)), and **the determining factor is the position of the puck, not of your body.**
>
> **There is exactly one exception: no penalty is assessed when you play the puck while maintaining skate contact with your goal crease.** It belongs to the restricted-area rule and to no other, and the NHL prints it **twice, in the same words**: inside **Rule 27.8** itself, and again as the Note to **Rule 63.2(viii)** — *"The minor penalty will not be assessed when a goalkeeper plays the puck while maintaining skate contact with his goal crease."* **IIHF Rule 27.7** carries it in the same words too. It excuses **playing** the puck, and it says nothing about freezing it. There is no exception for a teammate being nearby, none for being under pressure, and none for the puck coming to you rather than you going and getting it. ⚠️ **What the exception does not do is decide what counts as playing the puck, and the IIHF's Situation Handbook bounds that in your favour twice — in the same words in the 2025/26 (v1.1) and 2026/27 editions.** **A puck that comes off the glass and hits you while you are retreating is no penalty**, because *"the goalkeeper must play the puck to be penalized"* (Situation 27.9). And **a puck you stop on the goal line is not behind it**, so stopping it there is not the offence (Situation 27.6). Neither is a reason to go out there, and the Handbook ranks itself below the Rulebook — *"in case of any contradictions, the valid IIHF Rulebook is always the basis"* — but neither should you believe you have taken a minor because a puck bounced off you.
>
> **Where it applies:** the **NHL** and **IIHF play** — both read directly in the rulebooks, at NHL Rules 1.8 and 27.8 and IIHF Rules 1.8 and 27.7. **The dates are a separate question, and the rulebooks do not answer it.** The IIHF's own announcement of 5 June 2021 says it would *"introduce a new Rule Book for the 2021/22 season"* and lists among the rules to be integrated *"A restricted area for goalkeepers behind the net"*, which is what dates the IIHF's adoption; **the NHL's commonly given 2005-06 date is in no rulebook held here**, so treat it as the received date rather than a checked one. **The KHL is commonly said to have one as well, though that one is unverified here: no KHL rulebook was available to check it against.** **Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada writes a trapezoid at all** — the word appears nowhere in either book, neither marks out a zone behind the net that governs where a goalkeeper may go, and neither restricts *where you may play the puck* behind your net. **Two qualifications on that, and neither of them gives you a trapezoid.** Hockey Canada does print the phrase *"designated area"* once, at Rule 5.4(b), where it is the spot a Goal Judge stands in. And USA Hockey's *"goalkeeper's privileged area"* is a real marked-out zone — but it governs the **freeze**, not the play, and it is set out two paragraphs below. **How much rec, beer-league and youth hockey that adds up to is a question about leagues rather than about books, and this document has no count for it.** If you play under rules without a trapezoid, you are a genuine third puck-handler behind your net and your whole retrieval game — and your defencemen's — changes. ⚠️ **But no trapezoid is not no restriction: both of those books restrict the *freeze* instead.** USA Hockey's restriction is the next paragraph. **Hockey Canada's** is Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a), clause v, and it is the blunter of the two: *"When a goaltender leaves their crease, they will not be allowed to freeze the puck. If they do, they will be assessed a Minor penalty. No warning will be issued."* ⚠️ **Read that clause against clause ii of the same Interpretation, because Hockey Canada's own guideline permits the thing clause v appears to forbid:** *"The goaltender may come out of their crease to cut down the angle and catch the shot or, after stopping the shot, cover the puck."* Clause iv sits between them and reaches the narrower case in terms — *"When a goaltender comes out of their crease to beat an attacking player to the puck and simply jumps on the puck, causing a stoppage of play, a penalty will be assessed. No warning will be issued in this instance."* **Nothing in the book says which of ii and v governs a cover made after a save outside the crease**, so read the permission and the prohibition together rather than either on its own, and ask your league which way its referees take it. Find out which rules you are under before your first shift.
>
> **🇬🇧 In England and Wales, treat the trapezoid as unsettled — and play as though it binds everywhere in Britain.** The IIHF book is the base rule set for the NIHL, the English and Scottish recreational and junior game, university hockey and the GB sides — and for the Elite League too, though the League runs its own Casebook that nobody here has read ([UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md)), and the 2025-26 England Ice Hockey and SIHA In-House Rules do not amend it. **But England Ice Hockey's own *Rules & Regulations* 22.3 say the restricted area is *"not currently enforced to allow ice rinks the necessary time to make the changes to implement this rule."*** That edition is 2024-25 and the current In-House Rules are silent on the point, so the published documents do not settle whether the suspension still stands. Assume it binds, ask your league, and look behind the net to see whether the lines are painted at all. ⚠️ **And if the answer comes back "not enforced", read what 22.3 suspends before you act on it: the Goalkeepers' Restricted Area, and nothing else.** **IIHF Rule 63.2(I)** is a separate delay-of-game minor — nothing in the *Rules & Regulations* or in either edition of the In-House Rules searched for this document touches Rule 63 — and it reaches *"any Player, **including the goalkeeper**, who holds, 'freezes' or plays the puck… in such a manner as to deliberately cause a stoppage of play"*, its goalkeeper limb applying *"outside of their goal crease area"*. ⚠️ **That boundary is your crease, not the trapezoid**, so the whole designated area is outside it. **A suspended restricted area frees where you may *play* the puck. It never frees where you may *freeze* it** — which is the same split Rule 27.7's own skate-contact exception draws, and the IIHF prints the two as separate rows in its Appendix IV Table 13. ⚠️ **Take the carve-outs with it or you will stop covering pucks you should cover:** 63.2(VI)'s Note makes it legal to come out, *"cut down the angle"*, make the save and *then* cover, and 63.2(VII) excepts a goalkeeper *"actually being checked by an opponent"* inside the crease. [Freezing the puck — and the rules that govern it](#freezing-the-puck--and-the-rules-that-govern-it) above has all three limbs in full. **IIHF Rule 27.7** is the operative text and it says what the NHL's says: *"A goalkeeper shall not play the puck outside of the designated area behind the net. Should the goalkeeper play the puck outside of the designated area behind the goal line, a minor penalty for 'Delay of Game' shall be imposed. The determining factor shall be the position of the puck. The minor penalty will not be assessed when a goalkeeper plays the puck while maintaining skate contact with their goal crease."* The delay-of-game companion is **IIHF Rule 63.2(VIII)**, the same subsection number as the NHL's.
>
> **⚠️ The rule number is not the same, and this catches people out.** The NHL puts the restricted area at **27.8** and the centre-red-line rule at **27.7**. The **IIHF shifts both down one**: the restricted area is **27.7** and the centre-red-line rule is **27.6**. IIHF **27.8** is not a rule about you at all — it is a signpost to the appendix table of goalkeeper infractions: *"A list of the infractions that shall result in a penalty to the goalkeeper can be found in Table 13."* ⚠️ **That table number is the 2025/26 book's. The 2026/27 edition merges three earlier tables and renumbers this one Table 11**, with the same rows and the same rule references, the restricted-area row included. So quoting "Rule 27.8" at a British referee cites the index, not the offence. **And the IIHF's own two publications disagree with each other — in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions alike:** the Rule Book and its goalkeeper-penalties table both number the restricted area 27.7, while the Situation Handbook still answers every restricted-area question with "Rule 27.8" (Situations 27.6, 27.7, 27.9, 27.10 and 27.11) and cites 27.7 for the centre red line. The wording of IIHF 27.6 and 27.7 is identical in the two editions; only the table number moved. Cite the **Rule Book's** numbering; know that the Handbook's is one out.
>
> **⚠️ But "no trapezoid" is not "no restricted area" — USA Hockey restricts where you may *freeze* the puck, and it is the same two minutes.** **Rule 614(c)** assesses a minor for delay of game to a goalkeeper who falls on or gathers the puck and causes a stoppage in **four** separate cases, and the first is the one that catches goalies out: *"(1) The puck is behind the goal line and their body is entirely outside of the goal crease"*; *"(2) They fail to play the puck with their stick when provided the opportunity to do so prior to being pressured by an attacking player"*; *"(3) The puck is outside the boundaries of the 'goalkeeper's privileged' area"*; and *"(4) They hold or place the puck against any part of the goal frame or boards or intentionally drops the puck on the back of the netting."* **Trigger (1) is not limited by the privileged area at all** — coming out behind your own net to smother a rimmed puck, with your body clear of the crease, is a minor under USA Hockey wherever on that line you are standing. That area is defined in the rule's own Note as *"an area outlined by connecting the end zone face-off spots with an imaginary line and imaginary lines from each face-off spot running perpendicular to the end boards"* — so it is a rectangle running from the faceoff-dot line back to the end boards. It is far more generous than a trapezoid and it does cover the ice directly behind your net — the *Casebook* asks that question directly at **Rule 614(c), Situation 14**, and answers *"Yes"*, the area *"extends to the closer end boards"* — but **it excludes the corners**. **So "behind the goal line" and "outside the privileged area" are two separate bars, not one**; trigger (1) catches you behind your own net even though the privileged area reaches back there. So under USA Hockey you may skate into the corner and *play* the puck freely, and you may not smother it there. Play it; don't sit on it. **⚠️ And do not carry the skate-contact exception across to this rule.** That exception is the trapezoid rule's — written into NHL Rule 27.8 itself and repeated as the Note to NHL Rule 63.2(viii), and in IIHF Rule 27.7 — and it excuses *playing* the puck outside the designated area. **Rule 614(c) writes no exception into its own text**, so a skate on the crease paint licenses nothing here: trigger (4) is violated by a goalkeeper standing on the paint holding the puck against the goal frame, and trigger (2) is a test of whether you could have played it before being pressured, with no location component in it whatsoever. **The exceptions to 614(c) are published in the *Casebook*, and they are the next paragraph.**
>
> **The exceptions live in the *Casebook*, and they are not a geography test.** USA Hockey's *Casebook* answers Rule 614(c) through the Playing Rules' glossary definition of the **"act of playing goal"** — *"Any action by the goalkeeper that prevents the puck from entering the goal, or prevents an immediate scoring opportunity within the goalkeeper's privileged area"* — which is the same test **Rule 610(b)**'s own Note states for freezing generally: *"A goalkeeper may only cover the puck while in the act of playing goal … and any action that makes the puck unplayable without an immediate scoring opportunity must be penalized."* Fourteen worked situations sit under Rule 614 in the *Casebook*, ten of them about the goalkeeper, and they answer both ways. **Several of the ones answered *no penalty* have you completely out of your crease.** So the question is not where your skate is. It is **whether you were making a save or taking a stoppage you could have avoided.**
>
> **Under USA Hockey, crease contact decides exactly one of the ten goalkeeper situations.** **USA Hockey *Casebook*, Rule 614(c), Situation 7** puts a goalkeeper in the crease who dives on a puck outside it and whose momentum carries them out, and answers **no penalty**: *"As long as some part of the goalkeeper's body is in contact with the goal crease when initial contact with the puck is made, this is considered to be in the 'act of playing goal'."* **Note what that is.** It is **body** contact, tested at the moment you **first touch the puck** — not a skate held on the paint while you lie on it.
>
> **Away from the crease, three things decide it — and all three are USA Hockey's, not the IIHF's or the NHL's.**
>
> - **Where the puck is.** **USA Hockey *Casebook*, Rule 614(c), Situation 12** puts a goalkeeper *"completely outside of the goal crease and lying prone on the ice"* who reaches out to cover, and answers *"No, if the puck is within their privileged area in front of the goal line."* The same situation states its own inverse: *"if the puck is behind the goal line or outside of the privileged area, a minor penalty for delaying the game must be assessed."* Those are triggers (1) and (3), and note the *Casebook*'s own verb on them: the minor **must** be assessed.
> - **Whether you are being pressured.** **USA Hockey *Casebook*, Rule 614(c), Situation 11** — you outside the crease, a point shot stopped, the rebound three feet away, and you dive on it — is answered both ways in one sentence: *"No, if the attacking team is in a position to pressure the goalkeeper. Yes, if the goalkeeper is not being pressured and has an opportunity to play the puck with their stick."*
> - **Whether you had time to play it with your stick.** That is USA Hockey's trigger (2), and its *Casebook* applies it hard. **Rule 614(c), Situation 8** (you skate out to cut down the angle and catch the shot in the air) and **Situation 10** (you skate out to challenge and trap the puck between your pads) are both answered **no penalty** — **neither answer turns on crease contact** — and both attach the same limit: *"If the goalkeeper had sufficient time to drop the puck and play it with no attacking players in their immediate vicinity, however, a minor penalty shall be assessed."* **Situation 9** is Situation 8 except that you drop the puck to the ice first and then hold it against the boards, net or ice, and it is a **penalty**: *"If the goalkeeper had sufficient time to drop the puck to the ice they must be forced to play it."* And **Situation 6** — you leave the crease and beat a lone attacker to a loose puck inside your own privileged area, then fall on it — is a **penalty** too: *"The goalkeeper has an obligation to play the puck with their stick, just like any other player."*
>
> **So the practical version of USA Hockey's rule runs the other way from the caution above, and this is the part to carry onto the ice under that book — and under that book only: in front of your goal line, inside the privileged area, with attackers coming, you may smother the puck whether or not any part of you is touching the crease.** ⚠️ **Do not carry that sentence into an NHL or IIHF game**, and if you play in Britain your book is the IIHF's: there the trapezoid governs where you may play the puck behind the goal line, and **IIHF Rule 63.2(I)** — the clause set out earlier under *Freezing the puck* — makes a deliberate freeze **outside** your crease a minor wherever on the ice you are, which is why the same cover that is routine on your doorstep is two minutes out at the top of the circle. What you may not do is take a stoppage you did not need — freeze a puck you had the time and the space to play with your stick, skate out to fetch one and fall on it, or carry the freeze behind your goal line or out into a corner. Two last USA Hockey situations round the picture out. **Rule 614(c), Situation 13** answers **no penalty** where a puck is simply shot into a goalkeeper already lying prone outside the crease, because that *"was not done in a deliberate manner"*. And **Rule 614(c), Situation 5** answers **no penalty** for a goalkeeper who falls on the puck behind the goal line with the body entirely outside the crease — but **for a reason that has nothing to do with the act of playing goal, and one you cannot plan around**: it is a **delayed penalty**, so play stops the moment your team gains possession, and *"[t]he goalkeeper is allowed to hold the puck for three seconds before being assessed a penalty"* (Rule 614(c)(1) with the Note to Rule 409(a)).
>
> **In front of the goal line, no trapezoid applies to you.** What still does is the bar on **participating in the play beyond the centre red line**, and **all four books carry it in the same operative words: *"participates in the play in any manner"***. **NHL Rule 27.7** and **IIHF Rule 27.6** are that sentence, with *"(intentionally plays the puck or checks an opponent)"* set inside it as a parenthetical gloss rather than as a limit on it. **USA Hockey Rule 407(c)** assesses a minor where a goalkeeper *"deliberately participates in the play in any manner when they are beyond the center red line"*, and **Hockey Canada Rule 4.13(c)** reaches a goaltender who *"intentionally participates in the play in any manner when they are beyond the centre red line"* — carrying no penalty of its own, and sending the offence instead to **Rule 8.3(a) – Interference**, a minor. **So it is not only a stick on the puck**: the NHL's and the IIHF's own parenthetical names checking an opponent, and neither amateur book's own sentence limits the manner at all.
>
> **⚠️ And the test is the opposite way round in the two North American amateur books — this is the half almost nobody knows.** Under the **NHL and the IIHF** the rule says in terms that *"The position of the puck is the determining factor for the application of this rule"*, and the IIHF Situation Handbook takes the sharp version of it at Situation 27.5: a goalkeeper whose **skates are still in their own half** but who plays a puck that is over the centre red line takes the minor anyway. Under **USA Hockey and Hockey Canada it is your skates that decide.** USA Hockey's *Casebook*, at **Rule 407 Situation 13**, puts a goalkeeper *"whose skates are beyond the center red line"* playing a puck still on their own side, answers that it is not legal, and gives the reason: *"In this situation the determining factor is the position of the goalkeeper's skates and not the puck."* Hockey Canada's **Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.13(c)** puts the identical facts and rules the same way — *"The determining factor is the position of the goaltender's skates and not the position of the puck. Assess the goaltender a Minor penalty for Rule 8.3 (a) – Interference."* **And Hockey Canada does not leave the skates test to an interpretation**: Rule 8.3(a) itself assesses a minor to any goaltender who *"plays the puck with their skates over the centre red line"*, in operative rule text — worded narrower than 4.13(c)'s *"in any manner"*, though both routes end at the same minor.
>
> **So the two halves catch opposite mistakes, and one instruction satisfies all four: keep your skates and the puck both on your own side of the red line.** That is legal everywhere, and if you do not know which book you are under it is the version to play. USA Hockey publishes a worked case on accidental contact — *Casebook* **Rule 407 Situation 12**, a goalkeeper on the way to the bench who is *"accidentally struck by the puck"* over the line, is **no penalty**, because *"the goalkeeper is not considered to be participating in the play if the puck contact was accidental"* — and the other three books have not been searched here for one of their own.

*How big is it, exactly? **Take the geometry from [Rink Map and Glossary](../foundation/rink_map_and_glossary.md)**, which covers the rink's dimensions and gives the NHL figures, the depth and the IIHF metric equivalents in one place. The part to carry in your head, because it is your two minutes: **under the NHL book** the dimensions come from **Rule 1.8**, and **never from Rule 27.8**. NHL Rule 27.8 defines your penalty correctly but still describes the area as beginning "six feet from either goal post" — wording left over from before the trapezoid was widened by two feet on each side in 2014. That is **stale, not self-contradictory**: six feet each side gives eighteen feet at the goal line, diverging coherently to the twenty-eight feet at the boards that both rules quote. It is the old shape, accurately described — and it is two feet a side narrower than the lines you are actually looking at, which is exactly the margin you would be penalised in.*

*🇬🇧 **The IIHF book does not have this defect, so if you play in Britain you can stop worrying about it.** IIHF Rule 1.8 gives the marking as "6.80m" along the goal line and "8.60m" at the boards, and IIHF Rule 27.7 — the penalty rule — repeats exactly those figures rather than leaving a superseded one behind. The shape is the shape you already know: 6.80 m is 22 ft 4 in against the NHL's 22 ft, and 8.60 m is 28 ft 3 in against the NHL's 28 ft. What genuinely differs is the **depth**, because the IIHF goal line sits 4.0 m from the end boards where the NHL's sits 11 ft — so an IIHF trapezoid is about two feet deeper. See [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) for the penalty rules, and [Defender](defender.md) for the retrieval consequences.*

### Stopping pucks for your defence

```facts
Action: Kill a rimmed puck dead behind the net and leave it stationary — you have converted a race into a pickup
Action: Call "leave it" early, while your defenceman is still coming back, not as they arrive
Technique: Blocking a rim with your pads is legal in front of the goal line, or behind it inside the trapezoid
Never: Body-block a puck behind the goal line and outside the trapezoid. Get out of the way instead and let your defenceman have it
Rule: Blocking a rim or pass with your leg pads behind the goal line and outside the trapezoid is "playing" the puck under IIHF rules, and a minor — not a grey area (IIHF Situation Handbook, Situation 27.7)
Rule: The offence is playing the puck, so a puck that comes off the glass and hits you while you retreat is no penalty — "the goalkeeper must play the puck to be penalized" (IIHF Situation Handbook, Situation 27.9, 2025/26 v1.1 and 2026/27 alike)
Convention: Whether the goalie plays the puck at all is a coaching choice — ask, and then be consistent
```

Even with a trapezoid, you have real value behind the net. The most useful thing you do back there is often not a pass at all:

- **Stop a rimmed puck and leave it.** A puck rimming hard around the boards will end up in the far corner with a forechecker on it. If it comes through the trapezoid, kill it dead behind the net and leave it stationary for your defenceman. You have converted a race into a pickup. Call **"leave it"** so they know to come and get it, and say it early — while they are still coming, not as they arrive.
- **Get your body in the way — but mind where you are.** You do not have to make a play with the puck; killing a rim with your pads so it dies behind the net instead of reaching the corner is often the most useful thing you can do, and **in front of the goal line, or behind it but inside the trapezoid, it is completely legal.** Behind the goal line and *outside* the trapezoid, do not do it. NHL Rule 27.8 says a goalkeeper "shall not play the puck outside of the designated area behind the net" and does not define "play" narrowly, and the determining factor is where the puck is, not where you are. **Under the NHL book, whether a deliberate body block counts as playing the puck is still my reading rather than a quoted interpretation.**

  **⚠️ Under the IIHF book it is not a reading at all — it is answered, and answered against you.** The IIHF Situation Handbook 2025/26 puts exactly this case, at Situation 27.7: a goalkeeper retreating through the corner *"drops down on the knees and blocks the pass using the leg pads which is clearly inside the restricted area."* The answer: *"Since the goalkeeper 'plays' the puck in the restricted area, the goalkeeper must be assessed a minor penalty for delay of game."* No stick, no possession, no attempt to make a play with it — a pad in the way of a pass is playing the puck. **That is the operative interpretation in Britain**, so the conservative reading this document takes is not caution there, it is the rule. Two things the Handbook also settles, both in your favour, and in the same words in the 2025/26 (v1.1) and 2026/27 editions. **A puck you stop on the goal line is not behind it**, so stopping it there is not the offence — Situation 27.6 reasons that *"the puck must be over the goal line to be considered to be in the restricted area."* ⚠️ **Read "restricted area" carefully in that sentence, because the IIHF's two publications use the phrase in opposite senses.** The **Handbook** means the ice behind the goal line that you are barred from; the **Rulebook** means the trapezoid itself — Rule 1.8 is headed *"Goalkeeper's Restricted Area"* and describes the marked trapezoidal area, and the goalkeeper-penalties table lists the offence as *"Playing puck outside restricted area"*. The corpus and the rest of this section use the Rulebook's sense. And **a puck that comes off the glass and hits you while you are retreating is no penalty**, because *"the goalkeeper must play the puck to be penalized"* (Situation 27.9). Out there, get out of the way and let your defenceman have it.
- **Know your team's convention.** Whether your goalie plays the puck at all is a coaching choice. Some teams want the goalie handling everything they legally can; others want the goalie to touch nothing and stay in the net. Both are defensible. Ask, and then be consistent.

### The safe outlets

```facts
Priority: Rank your options by risk before you move the puck
Options: Leave it for a defenceman with time — lowest risk, and always first when it is available
Options: A short pass to a defenceman on the wall on your strong side, where an interception means less
Options: A rim around the boards to the far winger — concedes possession but relieves pressure
Options: Off the glass and out — but along the glass, never over it, because under NHL and IIHF rules a puck you shoot straight out of the rink from your own end is a minor; goalies are allowed to be boring
Never: Pass up the middle — a goalie's turnover there is a shot on an empty net, and you are standing in it
```

When you do move the puck, rank your options by risk:

1. **Leave it for a defenceman with time.** Lowest risk, and it starts a controlled breakout. Always first when it's available.
2. **A short pass to a defenceman on the wall on your strong side.** Simple, short, and if it's intercepted it's intercepted below the goal line where it means less.
3. **A rim around the boards to the far winger.** Concedes possession but relieves pressure. Aim it to arrive where a teammate can meet it.
4. **Off the glass and out.** Rarely wrong when you are in trouble, and goalies are allowed to be boring — but ***along*** the glass, never over it. A puck you shoot directly out of the rink from your own end is a two-minute minor **under NHL and IIHF rules** — USA Hockey's clause reaches only a *deliberate* clear, so an accidental one is no penalty there — and the next section is about the rules that make it one, in all four books.
5. **A pass up the middle.** Almost never. A goalie's turnover in the middle of the ice is a shot on an empty net, and you are the one standing in it.

### Putting the puck out of play — the minor that is yours too, and the tier above it after a whistle

```facts
Rule: Deliberately shooting or batting the puck out of the playing area, from anywhere on the ice, is a minor — and both books print it against goalkeepers by name (NHL Rule 63.2(ii), at Reference Table 11; IIHF Rule 63.2(II), at Table 13 in the 2025/26 book, Table 11 in the 2026/27 one)
Rule: Shooting or batting the puck directly and undeflected out of the playing surface from your defending zone is a second, separate minor, with no deliberateness element in it (NHL Rule 63.2(iii); IIHF Rule 63.2(III), whose own words are wider at the bench)
Rule: Rules 63.2(ii) and 63.2(iii) both say "any player", and NHL and IIHF Rule 5.1 define that word as "both skaters and goalkeepers"; where a limb means to leave you out it says so, as Rule 63.2(v) does
Key: The defending-zone clause turns on where the puck was, not where you were — and almost every puck you play is inside your own zone when you play it, so you are in its fact pattern nearly every time
Rule: The minor reaches your clear, not your save — a stick save deflected over the glass is no penalty because you did not "shoot" the puck out (IIHF Situation Handbook, Situation 63.29); its reasoning that "Rule 63.2. talks about deliberate" is absent from 63.2(iii), so it is no general defence
Rule: The rebound relief is USA Hockey's alone — batting a rebound out of the rink before you have control is no penalty there (Casebook, Rule 610 Situation 5, on Rule 610(c)'s "deliberately"), but NHL and IIHF Rule 63.2(iii) has no such word and reaches it
Rule: USA Hockey writes no defending-zone limb at all — Rule 610's puck-out-of-the-rink clause is 610(c), which turns on "deliberately" not on where the puck was, and names goalkeepers in terms; but Rule 610 has other limbs that reach you, 610(b) for manufacturing a stoppage among them
Rule: USA Hockey does not stop at a minor for a puck put out of the rink during a stoppage — 610(c) is a minor, 601(c)(10) a misconduct, and the book does not say which governs; done "in protest of an official's decision" 601(d)(7) is a game misconduct
Rule: Hockey Canada writes a goaltender limb of its own, with no zone restriction, for shooting or batting the puck directly out of the playing surface with your stick (Rule 10.1(v)) — but Interpretation 5 to Rule 10.1(a) excuses a puck that touches the glass or deflects off a player or official
Rule: You do not serve a goalkeeper's minor yourself — a teammate on the ice, designated by your coach, serves it (NHL and IIHF 27.1; USA Hockey 407(a) adds misconducts) — but a game misconduct takes your place in the net (USA Hockey 407(b))
Action: Clear along the glass rather than up it, and if the puck is not settled on your blade put it down the boards instead of lifting it
```

Two of the delay-of-game limbs in Rule 63.2 are about pucks that leave the ice, and neither of them is written for skaters only.

- **The deliberate one, from anywhere on the ice.** A minor is assessed *"on any player who deliberately shoots or bats (using his hand, leg, foot, arm, or stick, etc.) the puck outside the playing area (from anywhere on the ice surface) during the play or after a stoppage of play"* — NHL Rule 63.2(ii), and IIHF Rule 63.2(II) in the same words. **This one is not an inference: both books print it in their own goalkeeper tables.** The NHL's **Reference Table 11**, *Summary of Goalkeeper Penalties*, reads at its eighth row *"Deliberately shoots or bats puck out of play — Rule 63"*, and the IIHF's **Table 13** carries the same row, naming **63.2 (II)** outright. *(That table is renumbered **Table 11** in the IIHF's 2026/27 edition, with the same row and the same reference.)*
- **The one with no deliberateness in it, from your own end.** The NHL's Rule 63.2(iii) assesses a minor *"on any player who shoots or bats (using his hand or his stick) the puck directly (non-deflected) out of the playing surface from his defending zone, except where there is no glass"*, and *"[t]he determining factor shall be the position of the puck when it was shot or batted by the offending player."* **Read what is missing from that sentence: there is no "deliberately" in it.** Meaning to do it is not the test; where the puck was when you played it is, and the clause says so again in terms — *"[i]f contact with the puck occurs while the puck is inside the defending zone, and subsequently goes out of play, the minor penalty shall be assessed."* ⚠️ **And that determining factor is what makes this clause yours rather than mainly a defenceman's problem.** Almost every puck you play is sitting inside your own defending zone at the moment you play it, so where a defenceman meets this fact pattern occasionally, you are in it nearly every time you touch the puck. **The IIHF's Rule 63.2(III) is the same rule written in its own words, and two of them are not the NHL's** — it exempts a rink *"where there is no **protective** glass"*, and where the NHL's clause catches a puck shot *"over the glass 'behind' the players' bench"*, the IIHF's catches one shot *"over the protective glass **surrounding** the Players' bench."* **Glass that surrounds a bench is more of it than glass behind one, so on its words the British clause is the wider of the two** — that is a reading of the wording, and no published interpretation of it is held here. Both books then attach the same Note, and in each it cuts two ways: a puck shot *into* the players' bench carries no penalty, and neither does one that leaves the rink directly off a faceoff.
- **Why "any player" means you.** NHL and IIHF Rule 5.1: *"For the purposes of these playing rules, any reference to 'player' shall refer to both skaters and goalkeepers. Any reference to 'goalkeeper' shall mean that the section of the rule is specific to goalkeepers."* Rule 63.2 uses that machinery deliberately — where a limb means to leave you out it says so, at 63.2(v), *"On a player other than the goalkeeper"*, and where it means to name you it does that too, at 63.2(i), *"On any player, including the goalkeeper"*. Rules 63.2(ii) and 63.2(iii) do neither, so both reach you. ⚠️ **One honest difference in how well the two are evidenced.** The goalkeeper tables put Rule 63.2(ii) beyond argument. **Rule 63.2(iii) reaches you through Rule 5.1's definition alone** — no table, and no published situation held here, states it of a goalkeeper in terms.

**Two things sit alongside all of this, and a goalie who took the rule without them would start flinching at pucks they should be stopping. Read which book each one belongs to: the first is the IIHF's own, and the second is USA Hockey's alone.**

- **It reaches your clear, not your save.** The IIHF Situation Handbook puts exactly that case at **Situation 63.29**, identically in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions: a goalkeeper moving from post to post makes a stick save, and the puck comes off the stick and over the glass. The answer is *"No. The goalkeeper was simply making a save and did not 'shoot' the puck over the glass. Rule 63.2. talks about deliberate, the save cannot be seen as falling under this criteria."* ⚠️ **Read that second sentence, because it cuts against the reading above.** Rule 63.2(iii) carries no "deliberately" and the Handbook's reasoning does. The answer cites *"Rule 63.2"* without naming a limb, and the neighbouring limb 63.2(II) *does* carry the word, so it may be reasoning from that one — but nothing held here says which it means. **So take the answer and not the argument for it.** What the clause's own words give you is that a save is not a *"shoot"*; they do not give you a general deliberateness defence, and nothing else held here supplies one.
- **⚠️ USA Hockey excuses a rebound you bat away before you have control of it. The NHL and the IIHF do not.** USA Hockey's *Casebook*, **Rule 610, Situation 5**: a shot hits the goalkeeper in the chest, rebounds into the air, and the goalkeeper bats it out of the air and out of the rink. **No penalty** — but the answer names the line: *"Had the goalkeeper caught the puck first, thereby gaining control, and then deliberately batted the puck directly out of the playing area, a minor penalty for delay of game would be required."* **That turns on the word *"deliberately"* in USA Hockey Rule 610(c), which is the only rule the Situation cites.** Rule 63.2(iii) has no such word, and it reaches *"any player who shoots or bats (using his hand or his stick) the puck directly (non-deflected) out of the playing surface from his defending zone"* — so a goalkeeper who bats a rebound out of the rink from their own crease is inside it. Control or no control, under the NHL and the IIHF expect the minor.

**⚠️ USA Hockey's book and Hockey Canada's each write this differently, and if your league runs one of them the two bullets below are the ones that apply to you. How much North American rec and youth hockey runs on those two books is a question about leagues, and this document has no count for it.**

- **USA Hockey writes no defending-zone limb at all.** Rule 610(c) is the deliberate clause and nothing else — a minor *"to any player or goalkeeper who delays the game by deliberately shooting or batting the puck outside the playing area, including after a stoppage of play."* Note that it names you expressly, so the deliberate clear is squarely yours. But a clear that leaves the rink **by accident** from your own end is two minutes under NHL and IIHF rules and no penalty under USA Hockey's. ⚠️ **Rule 610 is not the whole of that book's answer for a puck put out *after* a whistle, though, and the rest of it is not two minutes.** Rule 601(c)(10) assesses a **misconduct** penalty to a player who *"[s]hoots or bats the puck outside of the playing area during a stoppage of play (when not done in protest of an official's decision or in the direction of a spectator)"* — the same act Rule 610(c) prices as a minor. **USA Hockey does not say which of the two governs**, and none of its Rule 601 casebook situations concerns a puck put out of the playing area, so treat the tier as unsettled rather than settled at two minutes. That is a reading of two clauses side by side, not a ruling. ⚠️ **What is not unsettled is the parenthesis, and it is the part that ends your night.** Do it *"in protest of an official's decision"* and Rule 601(d)(7) makes it a **game misconduct**; send the puck *"in the general direction of an on-ice official, or in the direction of a spectator during a stoppage of play"* and Rule 601(d)(9) does the same; and the Note to Rule 601(d) reaches a **match penalty** for a player who *"shoots the puck directly at an official or spectator in a manner that recklessly endangers the official or spectator."* Rule 601(a)(4) prices the plainer version — *"[s]hoots the puck after the whistle, if in the opinion of the Official such shot was avoidable"* — as an unsportsmanlike minor, and prints its own ladder underneath it: *"A misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who persists in such conduct and any further dispute by the same player shall result in a game misconduct penalty being assessed."*
- **Hockey Canada writes a goaltender limb of its own, drawn differently again.** Rule 10.1(v) lists among the delay-of-game actions *"A goaltender who shoots or bats the puck directly out of the playing surface, with their stick"* — no zone restriction at all, and confined to the stick. ⚠️ **Whether that limb needs to be deliberate is not settled by the book.** Rule 10.1 opens *"Delay of Game occurs when any player or team deliberately causes a stoppage of play or delays the restart of play"*, and Rules 10.1(i), 10.1(ii), 10.1(vi) and 10.1(xi) each repeat "deliberately" while 10.1(iv) writes "by any deliberate action" — where Rule 10.1(v) carries no deliberateness element at all. Nothing held here says whether the opening sentence governs the limb, so do not assume it saves you. ⚠️ **What Hockey Canada does publish is a narrowing, and it is worth more to you than the deliberateness question is.** **Interpretation 5 to Rule 10.1(a)** gives *"[c]larifications as it pertains to goaltenders shooting or batting the puck directly out of play"*, and its clause i takes three cases out of the limb: *"When the goaltender shoots the puck directly out of the playing surface where there is no glass, such as at the Players' Bench or Penalty Bench, or if the puck touches the glass or deflects off a player or official, no penalty will be assessed."* **So under Hockey Canada a clear that clips the glass on its way out is not this offence at all** — which is most of what *along* the glass rather than over it buys you. ⚠️ **Its clause ii is about the rink rather than about you, and it can switch the rule off entirely:** *"In order to assess a penalty to a goaltender under this provision, both ends of the rink must be similar. If there is glass at one end only, then the rule would not apply. However, if the rink only has glass on one side of the ice, then a goaltender may be penalized, as both ends of the rink are similar and no goaltender has an advantage over the other."* ⚠️ **Do not read the whole of that across to the NHL and the IIHF.** **A deflection they already excuse in their own words** — 63.2(iii) reaches only a puck put *"directly (non-deflected)"* out of the playing surface, so a clear that comes off a teammate or an official is outside the clause there too. **What those two books do not write is the rest of it:** they have **no glass-touch carve-out** — their only glass provision is the *"except where there is no glass"* exemption for a rink that has none — and **nothing about the two ends of a rink being similar.** So under the NHL and the IIHF a clear that merely glances the glass on its way out is not excused by anything in the text, and no interpretation of theirs saying otherwise is held here. ⚠️ **And do not read Rule 10.1 as the ceiling either — Hockey Canada writes the after-the-whistle escalation somewhere else on purpose.** Rule 10.1(d) says flatly *"No Misconduct penalty may be assessed for Delay of Game"*, and 10.1(e) *"No Game Misconduct penalty may be assessed for Delay of Game"*, so that rule could not carry one. The book instead lists *"Shooting the puck out of the playing area, after the whistle"* among the acts of **Unsportsmanlike Conduct at Rule 11.1, preamble item (v)** — the book runs two roman-numeral lists in that rule and they collide at (v), so the locator matters: 11.1(d)(v) is the separate Referee's-crease clause — a Minor or Bench Minor under 11.1(a), a **Misconduct** under 11.1(d)(i) for a player who *"[p]ersists in Unsportsmanlike Conduct, after receiving a Minor penalty under Rule 11.1 (a)"*, and a **Game Misconduct** under 11.1(e) for persisting after either. ⚠️ **Neither rung is one you have to be walked up to, because each of 11.1(d) and 11.1(e) carries its own Note 1 lifting the rung beneath it:** a referee *"is not required to assess a Minor penalty under this rule before assessing a Misconduct penalty, but may assess either penalty initially"*, and likewise *"not required to assess a Minor or Misconduct penalty under this rule before assessing a Game Misconduct penalty."* **You do not get one free minor first.** What the Notes lift is the requirement that the lower penalty have been *assessed*; 11.1(e)'s own requirement that the player *"persists"* stays. Clause (iv) of the same list reaches the neighbouring act, *"Shooting the puck at or near the goal after the whistle."*

**And know who pays, because it is not you.** **NHL and IIHF Rule 27.1**: *"A goalkeeper shall not be sent to the penalty bench for an offense which incurs a minor penalty, but instead, the minor penalty shall be served by another member of his team who was on the ice when play is stopped and the penalty assessed"* — designated by your coach through the captain, and *"[a] penalized player may not serve a goalkeeper's penalty."* (The IIHF writes *"penalty box"* where the NHL writes *"penalty bench"*; the rule is otherwise the same sentence.) So your two minutes are a teammate's two minutes and your team's kill, and you stay in the net to face the power play you created. **USA Hockey extends that one rung further up:** Rule 407(a), *"A goalkeeper shall not be sent to the penalty bench for an offense that incurs a minor **or misconduct** penalty"*, with the same teammate designated by the coach through the captain. ⚠️ **It stops there, and the rung above it is the one that takes you off the ice.** Rule 407(b): *"Should a goalkeeper incur a game misconduct or a match penalty, their place will be taken by a substitute goalkeeper or a temporary goalkeeper (see Glossary) or an additional skater"* — a teammate still serves the time, but you are not the one finishing the game. Under USA Hockey Rule 404(b) a game misconduct is *"the suspension of a player or Team Official for the balance of the game"*, and the player *"shall also be suspended for the team's next game."* ⚠️ **Do not convert a misconduct into a fixed ten minutes either** — Rule 404(a) reads *"10 minutes, or the designated misconduct penalty time"*, and the Note above it authorises affiliates and governing bodies *"(Except for Adults)"* to reduce that length for games under their jurisdiction, so what a misconduct costs in minutes depends on the classification you play in.

⚠️ **None of that ladder exists in the two books this section opened with.** The NHL's and the IIHF's offence for putting the puck out of play is Rule 63.2, and neither writes a tier above it for that act — Rule 63.9 lists *"Deliberately shooting the puck out of play"* among the acts producing *"a penalty (minor, bench minor, penalty shot or awarded goal)"*, with no misconduct in the list, and NHL and IIHF Rule 75, Unsportsmanlike Conduct, was read in full for a puck limb at all four of its tiers and contains none. **So one of them after the whistle is two minutes in a British or NHL-rules game, where the same act can end your night in a USA Hockey or Hockey Canada one.** ⚠️ **What those two books do write is a misconduct for a different puck offence, and a way up by repetition.** **Rule 39.4(ii)**, and in the IIHF book **39.4(II)**, is a misconduct for a player who *"intentionally knocks or shoots the puck out of the reach of an official who is retrieving it"* — that one is about the official, not about the glass. And **IIHF Rule 75.4(I)** makes a misconduct of *"Any Player who persists in any course of conduct for which they were previously assessed a Minor Penalty"*, with **75.5(I)** a game misconduct above it, while the NHL's **75.4(v)** states the same progression in general terms. **Neither book says whether a repeated puck-over-the-glass minor is such a course of conduct**, so take that as what the text leaves open rather than as a decided point. **Find out which book your league runs before you decide that lifting it out is cheap.**

**What to do about it is small.** Clear **along** the glass rather than up it. If the puck is not settled on your blade, put it down the boards instead of lifting it — a rim that dies in the corner is a worse outcome than a clean exit and a far better one than a minor. And never lift a puck out of the rink to buy yourself a whistle: that is Rule 63.2(ii), deliberately, in front of a referee who can see exactly what you did — and if the whistle has already gone and you do it in temper, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada both have a rung above the minor for exactly that, ending in a game misconduct.

### The discipline of not playing it

```facts
Key: The best puck-handling decision a goalie makes is usually to leave the puck alone
Never: Leave your net for a 50-50 puck — if you lose the race the net is empty with you behind the play
Rule: Racing out and jumping on a loose puck instead of playing it is a minor — but coming out to cut down the angle, making the save and then covering the puck is legal (NHL and IIHF Rule 63.2(vi))
Never: Handle it because you can — every touch is a chance to make a turnover you are out of position to save, because playing the puck takes you out of your net
Action: If you commit, commit — call "I've got it" and mean it
Mindset: A goalie who handles the puck well is an asset; one who believes they handle it well is a liability
```

The best puck-handling decision a goalie makes is usually to leave the puck alone.

- **Don't leave your net for a 50-50 puck.** If you lose the race, the net is empty and you are behind the play. The rulebook even anticipates this: racing out and jumping on a loose puck instead of playing it is a minor (NHL and IIHF Rule 63.2(vi)). ⚠️ **That is not a reason to hesitate over a puck you have already stopped.** The NHL and IIHF books both attach a Note to that same clause saying so in terms — coming out to cut down the angle, making the save and *then* covering the puck is legal — and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a) says the same thing in its own words. What the clause penalises is racing an attacker to a loose puck and smothering it instead of playing it.
- **Don't handle it because you can.** Every touch is a chance to make a turnover you are out of position to save, because playing the puck takes you out of your net.
- **If you commit, commit.** The worst outcome is starting to play the puck, changing your mind, and leaving both you and your defenceman out of position. Call **"I've got it"** and mean it.
- **Watch the clock and the pressure, not your ego.** A goalie who handles the puck well is a real asset. A goalie who *believes* they handle the puck well is a liability.

### Icing — you are the one who can wave it off

```facts
Rule: Skating toward the puck, feigning at it or attempting to play it waves off an icing; your defencemen cannot (NHL Rule 81.3)
Action: Usually you want the whistle, so do nothing, deliberately — drifting toward the corner hands them a live puck in your end
Rule: Under NHL and IIHF Rule 81.4 the team that iced the puck cannot change — the exceptions are an injured player, a pulled goalie returning and a strength-changing penalty, plus a fourth for a broken skate in IIHF 81.4 alone; under USA Hockey Rule 624 they can change anyway
Rule: Do not plan a change around that fourth one — the same IIHF book states the bar again at Rule 82.1, whose trigger names Rule 63.8 and Rule 81, and that copy lists only the NHL's three. Neither rule says which governs, in either edition
Read: Wave the icing off only when your defenceman is arriving with time and their forwards have already turned for the bench
Action: Decide whether to wave the icing off before the puck arrives, not halfway through it — two strides is the whole action
Action: Talk either way — "leave it" or "I've got it"; silence produces two players and one puck
Rule: Going to the bench for an extra attacker does not wave off an icing, provided you make no attempt on the puck (NHL Rule 81.3)
Rule: Under USA Hockey only an actual touch waves an icing off — Rule 624(b) does not wave off for goalie movement
Rule: You can cause an icing too — Rule 81.1 stops play when "any player" shoots the puck from their own half beyond the far goal line, and Rule 5.1 makes that word include you (NHL and IIHF)
Rule: Knocking your own net off locks your bench too — a goal post accidentally displaced by a defending skater or goalkeeper, you included, bars your team from substituting before the draw (NHL and IIHF Rule 63.8)
```

**The rule itself is in [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md).** What is specific to you is that under NHL and IIHF rules **you can kill an icing without touching anything.** Merely skating in the direction of the puck, feigning at it, or attempting to play it waves the icing off (Rule 81.3 in both books, same number), and so of course does any actual touch. Your defencemen cannot do that; only you can. The IIHF wording is *"if … the goalkeeper feigns playing the puck, attempts to play the puck, or skates in the direction of the puck on an icing at any time, the 'potential icing' shall not be called"* — and the IIHF Situation Handbook shows how little it takes: at Situation 81.5, a goalkeeper who **extends the stick towards the puck with both feet still in the crease, then pulls it back**, has already waved the icing off.

So the decision behind your net on every dumped puck is a real one, and it has to be made before it arrives:

- **Usually you want the whistle, so stay put.** An icing against them means a draw in *their* end, and **under NHL and IIHF rules (Rule 81.4) they cannot change** — you have converted a dump-in into an offensive-zone faceoff for your team against five players who are stuck out there. **Both books allow the same three exceptions**, and **IIHF Rule 81.4 prints a fourth**, permitting a substitution to replace *"a Player with a broken skate"*, which the NHL's three do not have. ⚠️ **Do not plan a change around that fourth one, in either direction.** The same IIHF book states the post-icing substitution bar a second time, at **Rule 82.1** — a rule whose own trigger names Rule 63.8, Delaying the Game, and Rule 81, Icing — and that second copy lists only the NHL's three, in the 2025/26 (v1.1) and 2026/27 editions alike. **Neither rule says which governs**, so the book says both things and this document does not resolve it. [Switching Positions](switching_positions.md) carries the same pair. **⚠️ Under USA Hockey they can change:** Rule 624 restricts no substitution and Rule 204(a) gives each bench the ordinary five seconds to do it — after which *"no additional player substitutions shall be permitted until the face-off has been completed legally and play has resumed, except when a penalty or penalties are imposed that affect the on-ice strength of either or both teams"* — so in North American rec and youth hockey you win the zone but not the tired line. ⚠️ **Under Hockey Canada the rule is scoped, and most of its players never meet it.** **Rule 6.7(d)** opens *"[i]n categories of U18AAA, Junior, and at the option of the Member in Senior, the following 'no-change on icing' rule will apply"* — the same scope appears again at **Rule 6.1(f)** — and 6.7(a), (b) and (c) carry no substitution bar at all, so below U18AAA, and in Hockey Canada minor and female hockey generally, **an icing costs the faceoff and nothing else and you may change freely.** ⚠️ **Where 6.7(d) does apply, do not read its exceptions as the NHL's: its third one is wider.** **Rule 6.7(d)**'s third exception permits a change *"[w]hen a penalty or penalties have been assessed to either team"* — **with no on-ice-strength requirement**, where NHL and IIHF Rule 81.4 both require the penalty to affect strength. ⚠️ **And one more thing to expect to have to argue.** **Rule 81.4 grants the injury exception and it governs** — but **IIHF Situation 81.7 is worded as though it does not.** Asked whether a *lengthy delay* for broken glass lets the icing team change, it answers no, and then reaches far wider than the question, stating that the *"[o]nly time a line change is permitted is when a penalty is assessed which affects the on-ice strength of either team"* and citing Rule 81.4 for it. **Read literally, that withdraws the very injury exception the rule it cites plainly grants.** The Situation Handbook's own Foreword ranks it below the Rulebook, so **81.4 wins and an injured player may still be replaced** — but a linesperson quoting 81.7 at you is quoting the book, and you should expect to have to say which one governs. [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) covers this. Drifting toward the corner "just in case" throws all of that away and hands them a live puck in your own end with a forechecker arriving. Do nothing, deliberately.
- **Wave it off only when the counterattack beats the whistle.** If your defenceman is arriving with time and their forwards have already turned for the bench, a live puck with numbers is worth more than a stoppage. Two strides toward the puck is the whole action — but decide before it arrives, not halfway through it.
- **Talk either way.** Your defenceman is skating back with his head down and has no idea which you have chosen. **"Leave it"** or **"I've got it"** settles it; silence produces two players and one puck.
- **Going to the bench for an extra attacker does not wave it off**, provided you make no attempt on the puck (Rule 81.3). Nor does simply retreating to your crease if you were already out of it. You can leave without giving them a live puck.
- **Freezing a dumped puck can lock your own team on the ice.** See Rule 63.8 under *Rebound Control* above — on a puck shot in from their own side of the centre red line, your freeze means **your** five cannot change.
- **And so does knocking your own net off — the other limb of the same rule, and the limb that names you.** Under **NHL Rule 63.8** a goal post *"displaced accidentally by a defending skater or goalkeeper"* gives the defending team a draw *"at one of the end zone face-off spots in the defending zone"*, at the dot the attacking team picks, and *"[t]he offending team shall not be permitted to make any player substitutions prior to the face-off."* **IIHF Rule 63.8 writes *"a defending Player"*** instead, which its Rule 5.1 defines as covering goalkeepers, so the rule reaches you under either book. ⚠️ **The two limbs do not carry the same number of exceptions.** This one has three — a pulled goalkeeper going back in, an injured player, and a penalty that changes on-ice strength — where the freeze limb above has **two**, with no pulled-goalkeeper exception at all. Backing into your own net in a scramble is a whistle nobody gets to rest on.
- **And you can cause an icing yourself.** Rule 81.1 stops play when *"any player of a team, equal or superior in numerical strength (power-play) to the opposing team"* shoots, bats or deflects *"the puck from his own half of the ice beyond the goal line of the opposing team"* — the same *"any player"* that Rule 5.1 defines as covering goalkeepers, and the same sentence in the IIHF book. **Rule 81.5's list of what is *not* icing does not rescue you.** It has three goalkeeper clauses and all three are about somebody else: a touch by the **opposing** goalkeeper, a goalkeeper dislodging the puck from the back of their own net, and the *"any player (other than the goalkeeper) of the opposing team"* who could have played it. None of them exempts the goalkeeper who shot it. This is a much rarer play than the delay-of-game minor above — it takes a hard rim or a stretch attempt from the trapezoid that carries the length of the ice untouched — but the cost is exactly the one you spend the rest of this section trying to impose on them: under NHL and IIHF Rule 81.4 it is **your** five who are stuck on the ice for the draw.

**Under USA Hockey rules none of the movement part applies:** Rule 624(b) does not wave off icing for a goalie moving toward or feigning at the puck. Only an actual touch does. Find out which rule set you are under, because the same two strides mean opposite things.

---

## Communicating with Your Defence

**This is the most valuable thing you do that is not a save.** Read this section twice.

You see everything. You are stationary, and you are facing the play while your defenceman is not — and it is not only a turnover you are preventing. A player facing the end boards with a forechecker arriving behind them is the situation the checking-from-behind rules exist for — so your call is not just saving a possession, it is the warning that keeps a teammate off the boards. Your defenceman retrieving a puck in your corner is facing the end boards with a forechecker arriving from behind them — the exact view you have and they do not. If that information doesn't travel from you to them, they guess, and a guess in the defensive zone is a turnover in the highest-danger area on the ice.

**A call converts a blind decision into an informed one, and it costs you nothing.** The information was already sitting in your eyes, useless.

### The core calls

```facts
Convention: These goalie calls are the standard vocabulary, but words vary by team — agree yours in the room, not on the ice
Options: Pressure — "time" means lift your head and make the good play; "man on" means get rid of it now
Options: Direction — "over", "reverse", "up", "wheel". Over means behind the net, never through the slot
Options: Ownership — "leave it" means I will stop it and leave it for you; "I've got it" means go to your breakout position
Key: "Screen" means move, or move the man in front of me — make it your first call, because nobody else can know you cannot see; that it is also the most ignored is observation, not a count
Key: "Back door" means an unmarked opponent at the far post, and the nearest player takes them right now
Key: "Shot" means you have the shooter, so your defenceman takes away the pass — not a warning that a shot is coming
Action: If your team already uses "shot" as a warning, pick a different word for the warning
```

These are the standard vocabulary. Words vary by team — agree yours in the room, not on the ice.

**Pressure information — the category to get right first:**

- **"Time"** or **"You've got time"** — nobody is near you. Lift your head and make the good play instead of the safe one. This call is exactly as valuable as its opposite, because a defenceman who fears pressure that isn't there throws the puck away.
- **"Man on"** — someone is on you right now, get rid of it. Some goalies use **"one on"** / **"two on"** to give the count, which is more useful still: one forechecker can be beaten with a reverse, two means you are being sealed and the puck has to leave.

**Direction:**

- **"Over"** — move it to the other side, behind the net, below the goal line. Never through the slot.
- **"Reverse"** — send it back the way it came, against the flow. The call for a forechecker who has over-committed and skated past your defenceman. Distinct from a **rim**, which goes onward around the boards and beats a forechecker who is sealing rather than over-running.
- **"Up"** — to the winger on the boards on the strong side.
- **"Wheel"** — skate it yourself, around behind the net and up the far side. You are telling them the route is clear, so they should commit.

**Puck ownership:**

- **"Leave it"** — I will stop it and leave it behind the net for you. Two syllables, distinguishable from "up" and "over" in a loud rink at speed, which is why it beats one-word alternatives.
- **"I've got it"** — I am playing this puck myself. Get to your breakout position, not to the puck.

**Danger:**

- **"Screen"** — I cannot see. Move, or move the man in front of me. **Make it your first call: it is the only one that reports something nobody else on the ice can know, which is that you cannot see the puck. That it is also the one most often left unmade is an observation, not a count.**
- **"Back door"** — there is an unmarked opponent at the far post. The nearest player takes them, right now. That is a tap-in from the slot, the most dangerous ice on the rink — [Shooting](../technique/shooting.md) owns the shot-location values behind that and the limits on them. How it ranks against every other unmarked position is not something anyone has counted; what makes the call necessary is that the person who can see it is almost never the person who can cover it.
- **"Shot"** — **not a warning that a shot is coming. It means you have the shooter, so your defenceman should take away the pass.** This is documented word for word in published goaltending material: *"When the puck is at the point, or when the opposing team has a two-on-one, it can be difficult for your defense to know what you want them to do. If you'd prefer that they take away the pass or guard the open man, call for 'shot!'... If you want the shot, call for it!"* ([Sportmentary — Ice Hockey Goalie: Communication Tips](https://sportmentary.com/ice-hockey/ice-hockey-basics/ice-hockey-goalie-communication-tips/)). Use it in that sense, because "a shot is coming" tells a skater nothing they can act on while "I have the shooter" tells them exactly where to put their stick. If your team already uses "shot" as a warning, pick a different word for the warning — the two meanings are incompatible and a defenceman has half a second to decode it. [On-Ice Communication](../foundation/on_ice_communication.md) covers the same split from the defenceman's side.

### How to call well

```facts
Action: Early beats loud — call while the play is still developing, not as the puck arrives
Action: Short beats descriptive — one or two syllables; nobody parses a sentence at full speed
Never: Call sometimes — a goalie who calls intermittently trains their defencemen to filter them out
Action: Say the same thing every time; consistency is what makes a call fast to act on
Action: Get answered — tell your defencemen you need an acknowledgement
```

- **Early beats loud.** A call that arrives as the puck does is decoration. Call while the play is still developing.
- **Short beats descriptive.** One or two syllables. Nobody parses a sentence at full speed.
- **Every time, or not at all.** A goalie who calls sometimes trains their defencemen to ignore them, which is worse than silence, because the one time you shout something urgent it gets filtered out with the rest.
- **Say the same thing every time.** Consistency is what makes a call fast to act on.
- **Get answered.** A call with no response gets made quieter next time and then not at all. Tell your defencemen you need an acknowledgement.

Full treatment, including the whole team's vocabulary and who owns which call: [On-Ice Communication](../foundation/on_ice_communication.md).

---

## Situational Goaltending

### Breakaways

```facts
Key: One attacker, you, and a lot of time to think — which is the actual difficulty
Technique: Meet them well out, then retreat on C-cuts, matching their speed so the angle stays closed the whole way
Technique: Keep a little backward flow so you can react either way; flat-footed means you can react to nothing
Never: Move first — their whole plan is to make you commit, and the last one to move wins
Convention: What to watch is a genuine coaching split — the puck, or the shooter's chest and hips. Ask your goalie coach and be consistent
Read: Puck out in front and winding up, respect the shot and stay big; pulled to the forehand with the head up, respect the deke
Risk: The poke check works when the puck gets too far in front of them — miss and you have neither reach nor balance
Rule: Deliberately removing your mask to force a stoppage is a delay-of-game minor in all four books (NHL and IIHF Rule 9.6, USA Hockey Rule 304(e), Hockey Canada Rule 3.6(d)) — do it on a breakaway, penalty shot or shootout and the NHL and the IIHF award the goal outright
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 610(e) and Hockey Canada Rule 4.11(a)(iii) price that same act as a penalty shot instead, and both add a last-two-minutes-and-overtime limb that neither the NHL nor the IIHF writes
```

One attacker, you, and a lot of time to think — which is the actual difficulty.

> **The shooter's half of this is in [Shooting](../technique/shooting.md), "Breakaways and Shootouts — the Shooter's Side"** — approach speed, deception, the classic moves, where they are aiming, and how they decide between shooting and deking. Read it: everything they are coached to do is a way of making you commit first, and knowing the plan is most of the defence against it. The penalty-shot and shootout rules below are stated identically in both documents.

- **Come out and retreat with them.** Meet them well out, then back in on C-cuts, matching their speed so the angle stays closed the whole way. Standing still at the top of the crease lets them pick a corner; charging out lets them go around you.
- **Keep flow.** A little backward motion means you can react either way. Flat-footed means you can react to nothing.
- **Stay on your feet as long as you possibly can.** The shooter's whole plan is to make you commit. The last one to move wins.
- **What to watch is a genuine coaching split.** One school says **watch the puck, not the body** — everything above the puck is theatre, and a body fake exists only to move you. The other says the puck is exactly what the shooter controls and fakes with, so read the part of them that cannot lie: the BVHS goalie manual's breakaway checklist step 3 is *"Read the play: Watch the shooter's chest and hips, not their stick or eyes. Hold your ground and wait for them to make the first move."* Note that the two schools agree on what they are *against* — the stick, the eyes and the shoulders — and disagree only on whether the puck or the torso is the honest signal. **Ask your goalie coach which they teach, and be consistent about it**, because switching mid-play is how you end up reading neither.
- **Take away what they are showing you.** If they have the puck out in front and are winding up, respect the shot and stay big. If they're pulling it to their forehand with their head up, respect the deke and stay patient.
- **The poke check is available but expensive.** It works when the puck gets too far in front of them. If you miss, you have neither reach nor balance.

> **⚠️ Do not knock your own mask off to kill the play. Do it against a breakaway and under two of the four books it is not a penalty at all — it is a goal.** Deliberately removing your helmet or face mask *"to secure a stoppage of play"* is a **minor for delay of game** under **NHL Rule 9.6** and **IIHF Rule 9.6**, which write that limb in the same words, and under **USA Hockey Rule 304(e)**, which extends it to skaters as well: *"A minor penalty for delay of game shall be assessed to a goalkeeper or skater who causes a stoppage of play by deliberately removing their helmet/facemask during play"* — and under **Hockey Canada Rule 3.6(d)**, which routes it the same way: *"If the goaltender deliberately removes their protective equipment to gain a stoppage of play, they will be penalized under Rule 10.1 (a) – Delay of Game."* **All four books write that floor. It is above it that they stop agreeing.**
> 
> **Do it while the other team is on a breakaway, or during a penalty shot or a shootout attempt, and the NHL and the IIHF do not give you a penalty at all — they give the goal.** NHL Rule 9.6: *"If the goalkeeper deliberately removes his helmet and/or face mask when the opposing team is on a breakaway, during the course of a penalty shot or shootout attempt, the Referee shall stop play and award a goal to the non-offending team."* IIHF Rule 9.6 says the same, with the breakaway limited to one where the penalty-shot criteria are met *"except for a foul from behind"*, and its **Rule 25.4** repeats the penalty-shot half: a goal is awarded where a goalkeeper tries to stop a penalty shot by dislodging the goal *"or removes their face mask to force an interruption."* ⚠️ **The NHL's own 25.4 omits that clause**, so a reader who checks only the awarded-goal rule will not find it there — NHL 9.6 reaches the same facts and the outcome is identical. **There is no shot to save at the end of this one.**
> 
> **The two amateur books price it as a penalty shot instead, and both reach further than the NHL and the IIHF do.** **USA Hockey Rule 610(e):** *"If a goalkeeper (all classifications) or player (excluding Adults) deliberately removes their helmet/facemask during a breakaway (see Glossary), a penalty shot/optional minor penalty shall be awarded"* — with a second, separate limb for *"the last two minutes of the game or any time during overtime"*, **no breakaway required.** ⚠️ **Read that age scope carefully, because it runs the opposite way to most: goalkeepers in every classification, skaters in every classification except Adults.** **Hockey Canada Rule 4.11(a)(iii)** awards a penalty shot *"[w]hen a goaltender or any other defending player deliberately removes their helmet or facial protector during the course of a breakaway in the neutral or attacking zone"*, and separately *"[w]hen a goaltender in their defending zone deliberately removes their helmet or facial protector, blocker or trapper during the last two minutes of regular playing time, or any time in overtime"* — **the only one of the four books to put your blocker and trapper in the same clause as your mask.** Its Interpretation 11 to Rules 10.6(a) and 3.6 answers the same facts from the other side: a defender who removes their own helmet *"in an effort to get a stoppage of play"* against a breakaway concedes the shot. **Neither the NHL nor the IIHF writes a last-two-minutes limb for any of this**, and **USA Hockey publishes no interpretation of 610(e)** — none of Rule 610's eleven Casebook Situations mentions a helmet or a facemask, so the clause stands on its bare text. *(One narrow exception, in USA Hockey's Disabled Hockey section: in **sled hockey** a goalkeeper who removes the mask to force a stoppage during a penalty shot gets a minor and the shot is **re-awarded**, not conceded. That is a sled rule and not a stand-up one.)*
> 
> **A mask that comes off by itself is a different rule and no penalty at all — and all four books stop the play for you, on three different tests.** Under NHL and IIHF Rule 9.6 the test is possession: if **your** team has control of the puck, play stops immediately so you can recover it; if the **other** team has control, play stops *"only if there is no immediate and impending scoring opportunity"*, and the ensuing face-off comes to one of your own end-zone spots. **USA Hockey Rule 304(e) attaches no condition at all:** *"In all classifications, if the goalkeeper's helmet/facemask comes off during play, the officials shall stop play immediately"* — and *"the goalkeeper shall not be ruled off the ice prior to the face-off"*, the carve-out that keeps you in your net where a skater in the same position is sent off. **Hockey Canada Rule 3.6(d) writes the widest version of all, and the widest list of equipment:** *"When a goaltender loses their helmet, facial protector, blocker or trapper, or skate blade, the play will be stopped immediately, unless there is an imminent scoring chance that does not pose a safety risk to the goaltender."* ⚠️ **Read that exception: it turns on your safety, not on who has the puck, and it covers a lost blocker, a lost trapper and a lost skate blade as well as your mask.** So the whistle you were tempted to buy is usually coming to you anyway, on the referee's judgement rather than yours. ⚠️ **A mask that stays on and takes the shot is a different question, and only two of the four books answer it at all — see [If you take one off the mask](#if-you-take-one-off-the-mask) under *Equipment Notes*.**

### Penalty shots

```facts
Rule: You must remain in your crease until the shooter has touched the puck, or the shot is retaken (NHL Rule 24)
Rule: A second violation adds a misconduct and a third a game misconduct, the third retake being against the alternate goalkeeper (NHL Rule 24.4)
```
