```facts
Rule: Under IIHF Rule 24.4 the third violation is a game misconduct and "a goal shall be awarded" — no retake, no alternate goalkeeper
Rule: No goal can be scored on a rebound, with one exception — off the post or crossbar, then off you, then in (NHL Rule 24)
Rule: The spin-o-rama is not permitted; the lacrosse-style move is, below shoulder height and released below the crossbar (NHL Rule 24)
Rule: Only a designated goalkeeper or alternate goalkeeper may defend a penalty shot (NHL Rule 24.2)
Key: There is no clock on it, so be patient and let them run out of ideas
```

Same as a breakaway, plus rules you should know precisely (NHL Rule 24):

- **You must remain in your crease until the shooter has touched the puck.** If you leave early and the shot fails, it is taken again. A second violation adds a misconduct; a third, a game misconduct — and the shot is retaken each time, the third retake being taken *"against the alternate goalkeeper"* (NHL Rule 24.4).

  **⚠️ 🇬🇧 The IIHF ends the ladder differently, and it ends it worse for you.** IIHF Rule 24.4 tracks the NHL through the retake and the second-violation misconduct, then diverges at the top: *"A third (3rd) such violation shall result in the goalkeeper being assessed a game misconduct penalty **and a goal shall be awarded**."* No fourth attempt, no alternate goalkeeper, no chance to save it. Under the book you play in Britain, a third early departure simply concedes.
- **The shooter must keep the puck moving toward your goal line.** Once they shoot, the play is complete. **No goal can be scored on a rebound**, with one exception: puck off the post or crossbar, then off you, then in.
- **The spin-o-rama (a full 360° turn on approach) is not permitted.** The lacrosse-style move — picking the puck up on the blade and whipping it — *is* permitted, provided the puck is never raised above shoulder height and is not released higher than the crossbar.
- **Only a designated goalkeeper may defend a penalty shot** — Rule 24.2: *"Only a player designated as a goalkeeper or alternate goalkeeper may defend against a penalty shot."* **IIHF Rule 24.2 says the same thing in the same words**, and adds one provision the NHL does not: the team against whom the shot was awarded **may swap goalkeepers to face it**, but *"the substitute goalkeeper is required to remain in the game until the next stoppage of play."*

**Everything else in this list holds under the IIHF book too**, in the same Rule 24 and with the same numbering — the crease requirement, the single rebound exception off post or crossbar then off you, the banned spin-o-rama, and the permitted lacrosse move below shoulder height and released no higher than the crossbar are all verbatim in IIHF Rule 24.2. Only the violation ladder differs, and that is flagged above.

Practically: they get one attempt, from centre ice, with a long approach and no rebound available, and the pressure is all on them. **There is no clock on it** — neither NHL Rule 24 nor the shootout provisions of Rule 84.4 sets a time limit, and neither does IIHF Rule 24, whose only timing provision (24.7) is that *"The time required for the taking of a 'Penalty Shot' shall not be included in the regular playing time or overtime."* So any "you have N seconds" figure you hear is folklore under either book. Be patient and let them run out of ideas.

### 2-on-1s — and what you want from your defenceman

```facts
Convention: The standard convention is that your defenceman takes away the pass and you take the shot — a convention, not a law
Action: Play the puck carrier as if it were a one-on-none — square up, come out to your normal depth, make the save
Key: The cross-ice pass to an open forward is the chance you cannot stop; the shot is the one you can
Action: Say it out loud — "I've got the pass" / "take the shooter" — it only works if you both know which half is yours
Never: Let your defenceman slide or dive before the pass is released where your team plays the passing lane; that opens both options at once
Options: Some teams instead coach a timed slide across the lane to kill the pass and force the shot — there the slide is the plan, so expect it and play the shooter the same way
Action: Ask your defencemen and your coach what your team does — some take away the shooting lane instead, and what must not happen is the two of you holding different answers
```

**The standard convention is that the defenceman takes away the pass and you take the shot.** Your defenceman positions in the passing lane with their stick flat on the ice, and you play the puck carrier as if it were a one-on-none: square up, come out to your normal depth, and make the save. The cross-ice pass to an open forward is the chance you cannot stop; the shot is the one you can.

The call for it is **"I've got the pass" / "take the shooter."** Say it out loud rather than assuming it — this only works if both of you know which half is yours.

**Name it as a convention, because it is one.** Some coaches vary it: against a strong shooter with a weak trailer, or when a backchecker has picked up the second attacker, the defenceman may instead take away the shooting lane. **And some teams play the alternative system outright** — the defenceman times a slide flat across the passing lane to remove the pass and force the carrier to shoot. Where that is your team's method the slide is the plan, not an error, and it does not change your job: you still have the shooter, square and set. What must not happen is the defenceman sliding **when your team plays the passing lane**, because there it opens both options at once — and, more than either, the two of you arriving at the rush with different answers. Ask your defencemen and your coach what your team does, before the game rather than during it. The full treatment of every numerical situation, from the skaters' side, is in [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md).

### 3-on-2s

```facts
Convention: The common default is each defenceman taking an outside attacker, the middle man left to whoever backchecks that lane
Read: A 3-on-2 is the same read as a 2-on-1, but with a wider back door
Action: Don't over-challenge. Stay a little deeper instead and prioritise lateral movement over the last foot of angle
Action: Call "odd man" to tell everyone to stop thinking about the puck and start counting
Action: Find out which 3-on-2 version your team plays, because the two fail in different directions
```

The common default is each defenceman taking an outside attacker and neither chasing the carrier, with the middle attacker left to whoever is backchecking that lane — usually the centre, though some teams instead have the strong-side defenceman take the puck carrier and the weak-side defenceman split the middle and far man. Both are coaching choices and they fail in different directions, so find out which one your team plays ([Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md) sets out both). From your side, the read is the same as a 2-on-1 but with a wider back door: don't over-challenge, stay a little deeper, and prioritise the ability to move laterally over the last foot of angle. Talk — "odd man" tells everyone to stop thinking about the puck and start counting. (An **odd-man rush** is any attack where the attackers outnumber the defenders who got back: a 2-on-1, a 3-on-2, a breakaway.)

### Shootouts

```facts
Rule: Shootout attempts are governed by the penalty-shot rules, including staying in your crease until the shooter touches the puck (NHL and IIHF Rule 84.4)
Rule: Once the shootout begins you cannot be replaced unless injured, and a substitute goalie gets no warm-up shots (NHL Rule 84.4)
Rule: The IIHF reverses that no-replacement rule — "The goalkeepers from each team may be changed after each shot" (IIHF Rule 84.4(VI))
Rule: NHL shootouts run three shooters a side; IIHF championship shootouts run five (IIHF Rule 84.4(II))
Rule: In Britain the shootout count is three, not the IIHF's five — overtime runs only in NIHL and some SNL fixtures (In-House Rules, Rule 84 and Appendix 1)
Key: Each shooter gets a long, slow approach — patience is the whole thing
Never: Commit first; most shootout goals are scored on goalies who do
```

A shootout attempt is governed by the penalty-shot rules (Rule 84.4 in both books, and IIHF 84.4(VII) says so explicitly: *"The shots will be taken in accordance with Rule 24 – Penalty Shot"*), including the requirement that you stay in your crease until the shooter touches the puck. Two things worth knowing under the NHL book: **once the shootout begins you cannot be replaced unless you are injured**, and **no warm-up shots are permitted for a substitute goalie.** So there is no rescue and no easing in.

**⚠️ 🇬🇧 The IIHF says the opposite, and it is the difference between having a rescue and not.** IIHF Rule 84.4(VI) is one line: *"The goalkeepers from each team may be changed after each shot."* There is no injury requirement and no warm-up prohibition. A coach under the IIHF book can pull you after a single shootout goal against; a coach under the NHL book cannot. It also runs longer — the IIHF championship procedure begins with *"five (5) different shooters from each team taking alternate shots"* against the NHL's three, before either goes to sudden death.

**Whether you face a shootout at all in Britain is a competition question, not a rules one.** Overtime is not universal in England Ice Hockey and SIHA competition, and where it is played the game-winning-shots format is set locally rather than by IIHF Rule 84.4 — the format, the shooter count and which competitions use it are in [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md). Check your league's regulations rather than assuming either the NHL or the IIHF championship procedure.

Approach: you get three or more shooters, each with a long, slow approach. Patience is the whole thing. Most shootout goals are scored on goalies who commit first.

### Power play (your team is up a man)

```facts
Read: On your own power play, very few shots — and then occasionally a rush the other way against tired defencemen
Risk: The failure mode on a long power play is being cold and mentally elsewhere when that rush arrives
Action: Stay engaged — track the puck at the far end and move your feet at every stoppage
Action: Talk more, not less — your point men have their backs to the neutral zone and you have the clearest view of what is coming
Read: Be ready for the shorthanded rush, usually a 2-on-1 or a breakaway, because your team has committed bodies
```

Quiet. You will see very few shots and then, occasionally, a rush the other way against tired defencemen. The failure mode is being cold and mentally elsewhere when it arrives.

- **Stay engaged.** Track the puck at the far end. Move your feet at every stoppage.
- **Talk more, not less.** Your **point men** — the teammates stationed at the opposition blue line — have their backs to the neutral zone; you have the clearest view of a shorthanded break developing.
- **Be ready for the shorthanded rush**, which is usually a 2-on-1 or a breakaway, because your team has committed bodies.

### Penalty kill (your team is down a man)

```facts
Read: On the penalty kill expect a lot of traffic, a lot of shots you cannot see, and lateral puck movement designed to make you move
Action: Know whether your team runs a box, a diamond or wedge+1 — it tells you which lanes are conceded
Convention: In wedge+1 the +1's route is a coaching choice published descriptions differ on — straight out from the goal, sweeping across the top of the zone, or roaming; ask which one your team runs
Priority: Prioritise seeing the puck; whether that means playing deeper is the depth-against-a-screen coaching split, so follow your goalie coach
Goal: Use freezes to buy whistles, not seconds — fresh legs, a reset and a draw — but only a freeze you were entitled to make
Rule: Manufacturing a stoppage is itself the offence — a minor to a goalie who could have played the puck before being pressured and stopped play instead (USA Hockey 610(b), whose Casebook answers that exact act "must be penalized" at Rule 610 Situation 3)
Rule: Hockey Canada warns you first only inside the crease — Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a) clause iii gives a Minor "after one warning" for freezing "in the crease when not under pressure"; clause v, once you have left the crease, is a Minor with "No warning will be issued"
Key: A freeze takes no time off the penalty, because play is stopped and the clock stops with it
Rule: Shorthanded icing is allowed only if your rule set grants it — USA Hockey Rule 624(b)(1) grants it to Youth 15-Only and above, Girls 16U and above, High School and Adult only, so below those levels your killers can be iced
Rule: NHL Rule 81.6 withdraws the shorthanded icing exemption from a team shorthanded by a major that has left the penalty bench empty
Rule: IIHF Rule 81.6 carries no such withdrawal, so under the UK's book shorthanded icing is unconditional
```

The opposite: a lot of traffic, a lot of shots you cannot see, and a lot of lateral puck movement designed to make you move.

- **The default shapes are the box (two forwards high, two defencemen low), the diamond (a single apex, used against a 1-3-1 because a box leaves the bumper in the middle of the slot unmarked), and wedge+1 (a tight triangle around the net plus a fourth player, the "+1", who pressures whoever has the puck rather than following it around the perimeter — when the puck moves, the nearest triangle player steps out in his place).** The route that +1 takes — straight out from the goal, sweeping across the top of the zone, or roaming — is a coaching choice that published descriptions differ on. Know which shape *and* which version of it your team runs, because it tells you which lanes are being conceded and therefore where the shots will come from. See [Special Teams](../systems/special_teams.md).
- **Expect shots from the point through traffic**, so prioritise seeing the puck. Whether that means playing deeper than usual is the coaching split flagged under [Playing bigger and deeper through traffic](#playing-bigger-and-deeper-through-traffic) — follow whichever your goalie coach teaches.
- **Use freezes to buy whistles — not seconds. But only a freeze you were entitled to make anyway.** A freeze on the kill is worth more than a freeze at even strength, because it gets exhausted killers a change and forces the power play to set up again from a faceoff. Be clear about what it does *not* do: a freeze stops play, so the clock stops with it. **No time comes off the penalty.** What you are buying is fresh legs, a reset and a draw — which is plenty. **⚠️ What you may not do is manufacture the stoppage, because that is the offence itself, and being on the kill is no defence to it.** USA Hockey Rule 610(b) assesses a minor to a goalkeeper who *"has an opportunity to play the puck prior to being pressured by an attacking player, but instead intentionally causes a stoppage of play"*, and its *Casebook* rules on exactly that act — a goalkeeper who comes out, waits until the chasing attacker *"is close enough"* and then freezes — that it *"must be penalized"* (Rule 610, Situation 3). ⚠️ **Neither the rule nor that situation writes a warning step, and the same rule writes one expressly where the book means one** (610(g), a bench minor *"after warning by the Referee"*) — but reading that silence as "no warning" is a reading of the text, not a ruling USA Hockey publishes. Hockey Canada does publish one, and it says how far the warning goes: Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a), clause iii, assesses a Minor *"after one warning"* to a goaltender who *"freezes the puck in the crease when not under pressure from attacking players"* — **in the crease.** Once you have left it, clause v is a Minor with *"No warning will be issued."* So the whistle is a by-product of a save you had to cover, never the reason for covering it. (And remember Rule 63.8: if they dumped it in from their own side of the centre red line, freezing it stops *your* team changing too.)
- **Your team may ice the puck freely while shorthanded — if your rule set allows it.** Say so, because killers under pressure sometimes try to make a play they don't need to make. But find out first: USA Hockey grants the exemption only *"For all Youth 15-Only and above, Girls 16U and above, High School and Adult classifications only"* (Rule 624(b)(1)), so below those levels shouting "ice it" buys your tired killers a defensive-zone draw instead of a rest. NHL Rule 81.6 also withdraws it from a team shorthanded by a *major* that has left the penalty bench empty. **🇬🇧 Under the IIHF book neither restriction exists.** IIHF Rule 81.6 grants the exemption flatly — *"If the puck was so shot by a Player of a side below the numerical strength of the opposing team, play shall continue, and the 'icing' violation shall not be called"* — with no classification limit and no empty-penalty-bench clawback, and the England Ice Hockey and SIHA In-House Rules do not amend it. So in Britain "ice it" is always available to a killer, at every level. Full rule in [Special Teams](../systems/special_teams.md).

### 6-on-5 with your net empty

```facts
Action: Go hard and go early when called — a slow change risks a too-many-men penalty or a goal with you halfway up the ice
Rule: Leaving your stick or equipment near the net, or piling snow there, awards a goal to the other team — but only once you have been replaced for an extra attacker; otherwise it is a minor (NHL and IIHF Rule 67.5)
Rule: The NHL and the IIHF write Rule 67.5 in materially identical words — the IIHF writes "their Players' bench", "their stick" and "their net" where the NHL writes "his"
Rule: You may shoot at their empty net with your stick, but not throw the puck forward (Rule 67.3(iii)) or participate in the play past the centre red line — all four books penalise that (NHL Rule 27.7; IIHF Rule 27.6; USA Hockey Rule 407(c); Hockey Canada Rule 4.13(c))
Rule: NHL Rule 27.7 and IIHF Rule 27.6 judge play past the red line on the position of the puck; USA Hockey Rule 407(c) and Hockey Canada Rule 4.13(c) judge it on your skates, so keep your skates and the puck both on your own side
Action: Coming back into your net after an empty-net shift, reset your references — posts, paint, depth — before the faceoff
Read: Defending a 6-on-5, play a little deeper and expect low-to-high plays and back-door passes rather than clean shots
Key: A whistle against a 6-on-5 does not run their clock down; what it destroys is the possession they spent thirty seconds building
Rule: Freeze a puck you had to save, in your crease — even there a cover that only manufactures a stoppage is a minor unless you are being checked (63.2(vii)); outside it the NHL and IIHF protect the cover that follows your own save alone (63.2(i), Note to 63.2(vi)), and USA Hockey protects more
```

When your coach pulls you for an extra attacker, your job changes completely.

- **Go hard and go early when called.** A slow change to the bench is how you end up with a too-many-men penalty or a goal against with you halfway up the ice.
- **Do not leave anything behind.** If you intentionally leave your stick or any equipment near the net, or pile snow there, and it would tend to prevent the puck entering, a **goal is awarded** to the other team (Rule 67.5 in both books). ⚠️ **Both books attach the same condition to it, in the same words**: *"In order to award a goal in this situation, the goalkeeper must have been replaced for an extra attacker, otherwise a minor penalty shall be assessed."* So it is an awarded goal once you have gone to the bench for an extra attacker, and a minor while you are still in the net. ⚠️ **The other two books answer the same moment differently — and between the four of them there are three different answers.** Take the case you are most likely to be in: the stick is behind you, you are on your way to the bench but not yet replaced, and the shot hits it. The IIHF's own *Situation Handbook* confirms the minor on exactly those facts — *"Since the goalkeeper is still on the ice you cannot award a goal only a minor penalty"* (Situation 67.5). **USA Hockey awards the goal instead.** Its Rule 625(c) awards one *"regardless as to whether the goalkeeper is on the ice, in the act of leaving the ice or off the ice"*, and its *Casebook* answers those same facts *"A goal shall be awarded"* — while also narrowing that *"regardless"*, since it adds that *"[f]or a goal to be awarded, the goalkeeper must either be in the act of leaving the ice or actually off the ice"* (Rule 625, Situation 14). **Hockey Canada gives a penalty shot.** Interpretation 4 to Rule 4.12(a) and 4.12(b) asks the same question and answers *"A Penalty Shot will be awarded, as the goaltender is still on the ice"*, with an awarded goal only once you are off it. ⚠️ **And USA Hockey does not require that you meant to do it.** Its *Casebook* takes a goalkeeper who is bumped, loses the stick in front of the goal and carries on to the bench, and still rules a minor or an awarded goal, because *"[i]t makes no difference whether or not the goalkeeper deliberately left their stick in front of the goal"* (Rule 625, Situation 15) — where all four rule texts say *intentionally* or *deliberately*. Take your stick.
- **You are legally allowed to score.** If the puck is loose in your own end and the other net is empty, you may shoot it — with your stick. You may **not throw the puck forward toward the opponent's net** (Rule 67.3(iii)), and you may not participate in the play beyond the centre red line — **all four books penalise that** (NHL Rule 27.7; **IIHF Rule 27.6**; **USA Hockey Rule 407(c)**; **Hockey Canada Rule 4.13(c)**, penalised as interference under its Rule 8.3(a)). **⚠️ And they do not measure it the same way.** The NHL's and the IIHF's determining factor is the position of the *puck*; USA Hockey's and Hockey Canada's is the position of your *skates*. With your net empty and your team pressing, that is exactly when a goalie drifts up — so keep your skates and the puck both on your own side of the line, which is legal under all four. The full split is under *The trapezoid — the rule that defines what you can do*.
- **Coming back in.** When play stops, get in fast and reset your references — posts, paint, depth — before the faceoff.

Defending against a 6-on-5 (their net empty, you facing an extra attacker): play a little deeper, expect low-to-high plays and back-door passes rather than clean shots, and freeze every puck you are **entitled** to freeze. ⚠️ **Wanting the whistle is exactly the motive these rules are written against, and this is the game state where you will want it most.** In your crease, a cover that follows a save you had to make is what you are entitled to. **Outside the crease the NHL and the IIHF protect only that** — Rule 63.2(i)'s deliberate-stoppage minor reaches a goalkeeper *“outside of his goal crease area”* however many bodies are around you, and the Note to Rule 63.2(vi) — come out, cut down the angle, make the save, *then* cover — is the whole of the exception. **Under USA Hockey more is protected**, and [Freezing the puck — and the rules that govern it](#freezing-the-puck--and-the-rules-that-govern-it) above sets out how much under each book. A minor taken defending a 6-on-5 is about as expensive as a minor gets. **A whistle does not run their clock down** — play is stopped, so the clock is stopped too. What a whistle does is destroy the possession: they have to win a faceoff and rebuild the setup they spent thirty seconds establishing, with the pulled goalie exposed to any clearance. That is the value, and it is a real one. *(Watch Rule 63.8 here too: if the puck was shot in from their own side of the centre red line, freezing it means your five cannot change either.)*

### When a fight or a scrum breaks out

```facts
Rule: A goalkeeper who leaves the immediate vicinity of their crease during an altercation takes a minor penalty (NHL Rule 27.6; IIHF Rule 27.5) — this is why goalies stay in the net during a scrum
Rule: Where the altercation is at your own crease the referee "should direct the goalkeeper to a neutral location and not assess a penalty" — that is an instruction to the referee, so wait to be directed out (NHL Rule 27.6; IIHF Rule 27.5)
Rule: NHL Rule 27.6 and IIHF Rule 27.5 also excuse you if you were already legitimately on your way to the bench to be replaced by an extra attacker when the altercation started (NHL Rule 27.6; IIHF Rule 27.5)
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 615(e) is a minor for failing to move immediately to your crease or bench when the referee instructs you to during an altercation; its Casebook applies the same minor to a goalie who leaves and gets involved without permission (Casebook Rule 615, Situation 26)
Rule: Hockey Canada Rule 7.10(a) puts a minor on a goaltender who leaves their crease during a fight, and Interpretation 1 lets you go to the bench only "with the permission of the Referee"
Rule: Hockey Canada has a neutral-location route of its own — Rule 7.10(d) requires that "The goaltenders must stay in their goal creases or proceed to a neutral zone designated by the Referee"
Key: One instruction satisfies all four books during an altercation: stay in your net, and if you need to move, get the referee's permission first
Never: Go in to help a teammate — the first player into an altercation is ejected under USA Hockey's rules, and Hockey Canada ejects a peacemaker too
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 615(d) ejects "any player or goalkeeper who is the first to intervene in an altercation then in progress" — it names goalkeepers and sets no crease condition, so it reaches you wherever you came from
Rule: Hockey Canada Rule 7.10(e)(ii) ejects a player who "joins in a fight or acts as a peacemaker", and its Glossary settles that this reaches you — "The goaltender is considered a player, except where explicitly stated"
```

Gloves come off in front of you and the instinct is to go. Don't.

- **The default is a minor.** Under **NHL Rule 27.6** and **IIHF Rule 27.5** — the same rule, one number apart — a minor is imposed on a goalkeeper *"who leaves the immediate vicinity of"* their crease during an altercation. Staying put costs you nothing and is right under every book.
- **Two carve-outs are written into that rule, and both are worth knowing.** Where the altercation happens *"in or near the goalkeeper's crease"*, the referee *"should direct the goalkeeper to a neutral location and not assess a penalty for leaving the immediate vicinity of the goal crease."* And if you were already legitimately outside the crease *"for the purpose of proceeding to the players' bench to be substituted for an extra attacker"* and are then drawn in, *"the minor penalty for leaving the crease would not be assessed."* ⚠️ **Read the first of those the way it is written: it is an instruction to the referee, not a permission for you.** The non-assessment is tied to the goalkeeper being *directed* to a neutral location — the rule tells the official what to do about a fight that lands on your doorstep, and it does not license you to leave the crease on your own judgement because the fight happens to be next to it. Stay where you are and wait to be directed out.
- **⚠️ The North American amateur books answer the crease-adjacent case differently, and one of them wants you to ask first.** **USA Hockey Rule 615(e)** is written the other way round from the NHL's — it assesses a minor to any player who *"fails to move immediately and directly to their players' bench (or goal crease for a goalkeeper) when instructed to do so by the Referee during an altercation"* — and the book's own summary of penalties files it under goalkeeper infractions as *"Leaving crease area during altercation."* Its *Casebook* is generous on the facts: **Rule 615, Situation 26** has a goalkeeper leave a crease-side altercation *"to remain out of the altercation"* and answers **no penalty** — but the same answer ends *"if the Referee does not give their permission and the goalkeeper gets involved in the altercation outside of the crease, the minor penalty must be assessed."* **Hockey Canada Rule 7.10(a)** states it flatly — a minor to a goaltender who leaves their crease during a fight — and its **Interpretation 1** answers the crease-side fight this way: *"With the permission of the Referee, the goaltender may proceed to their bench. If they do so on their own accord, they are to be assessed a Minor penalty for leaving their crease during a fight."* **Hockey Canada has a neutral-location route as well, so do not read it as the one book without one:** **Rule 7.10(d)** requires every player to return to the bench or to a designated neutral area when a fight occurs, and names you in its own text — *"The goaltenders must stay in their goal creases or proceed to a neutral zone designated by the Referee."* That is Hockey Canada's counterpart of the NHL's and the IIHF's carve-out, and it runs the same way: the neutral zone is one the referee designates, not one you pick.
- **So there is one instruction that is safe under all four: stay in your net, and if you need to move, get the referee's permission first.** [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) sets out the four-book comparison in full.
- **Joining in is a separate and worse penalty, and it is in the playing rules rather than in a casebook.** **USA Hockey Rule 615(d)**: *"A game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player **or goalkeeper** who is the first to intervene in an altercation then in progress. This penalty is in addition to any other penalty incurred in the same incident."* **Note how wide that is.** It names goalkeepers expressly, it attaches **no crease condition at all**, and it stacks on top of whatever else you collect — so it reaches you whether you came from your crease, from the blue line or from the bench door. USA Hockey's *Casebook* then works the goalie's case at **Rule 615, Situation 10** — a goalkeeper who leaves the vicinity of the crease and is first in takes *"a minor penalty for leaving the crease, plus a game misconduct for being the first to intervene in an altercation, plus any other penalty they may receive"* — and gives its authority as Rule References 615(a), 615(d) and 615(e). It is applying 615(d), not supplying it. **Hockey Canada ejects the same act under Rule 7.10(e)(ii)**, a game misconduct to a player who *"joins in a fight or acts as a peacemaker"* — and read that second limb, because it means going in to **break it up** is caught by the rule just as squarely as going in to fight. **And 7.10(e)(ii) reaches you, even though it says only "a player."** Hockey Canada's Glossary settles it: *"PLAYERS: The members of the team physically participating in the game. **The goaltender is considered a player, except where explicitly stated** because special rules or privileges apply to them."* Rule 7.10(e) states no such exception, so you are inside it. *(Rules 7.10(a), 7.10(d) and 7.10(f) do name goaltenders separately — that is the Glossary's "except where explicitly stated" doing its work, not evidence that a bare "player" excludes you.)* Whichever book you are under, your team is short a goalie and short a player, and you are watching the rest of the game from the dressing room.

---

## Goaltender Interference, From Your Side of It

### The protection you have — and how it differs by book

```facts
Key: On a body in your paint when the puck goes in, the four books do not give the same answer, so find out which one your league runs before you rely on any of this
Rule: The blue paint is yours — an attacker who initiates contact with you inside your crease voids the goal (NHL Rule 69), and IIHF 69.1 adds what the NHL has not: the referee "has the possibility to stop the game" if an attacker in your crease will not leave
Rule: IIHF Rule 69 is the same rule with the same subsection numbers, so every citation here is your citation in Britain — but EIH and SIHA competition has no video review and no coach's challenge, so the referee's call stands (In-House Rules, Rules 37, 38 and 69.1)
Rule: NHL Rule 69.3 caps the penalty on an attacker who will not vacate your crease after you make contact establishing position at a minor; IIHF Rule 69.3 writes no ceiling on that clause, so in Britain do not assume two minutes — and both books' own tables assess none at all where the goal is scored
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 625(b) is a flat position bar — no goal with an attacking player in your crease whoever scored, unless the puck got there first, you are out of the crease area, or a defender put them in there; Casebook Situation 9 takes that middle one back if they stopped you returning
Rule: USA Hockey 625(b)'s push-in relief excuses the attacker's position and nothing more — 617(c)(4) still voids a goal for interfering with you; 625(b) also makes a neutral-zone faceoff a duty, but only with the puck in the attacking zone, their team in possession and you in contact with the crease
Rule: Hockey Canada Rule 8.5 is not flat — a teammate's goal from outside your crease stands if the crease player stays passive, and is disallowed if they play the puck, interfere with the play or obstruct your view or movements
Rule: Hockey Canada Rule 8.5 protects you outside the paint too — a goaltender is not "fair game" just because they are outside their crease, and unnecessary contact is a penalty anywhere on the ice
Rule: Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada reaches a screen set from outside your crease — USA Hockey writes no goaltender-vision provision at all, its grounds being position in the crease and 625(a)(8) contact, and Hockey Canada's two references to your view reach only a player in the crease
Rule: Hockey Canada penalises an attacker fouled into you who makes no effort to avoid you (Interpretation 2 to Rule 8.5(a)); the NHL's and the IIHF's Rule 69.1 relieves that attacker only while they are making a reasonable effort to avoid you
Rule: Hockey Canada is not capped at a minor — 8.5(b) reaches a major plus game misconduct at the referee's discretion on the degree of violence of impact, and mandatorily for charging you and for any interference-with-goaltender foul that injures; 8.5(c) is a match penalty
```

You should know exactly what protection you have, because a lot of goalies believe they have more than they do and play accordingly. **This section is the rule read from behind the goal line. [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) covers Rule 69** — the premise (an attacker's position, inside or outside the crease, does not by itself decide anything), the two grounds on which a goal is disallowed, the strict inside-the-crease standard of Rule 69.3, and what all of it asks of an attacking player. Read it once. The short version from your side is that **the blue paint really is yours**: an attacker who initiates contact with you inside your crease voids the goal, incidental or otherwise.

**🇬🇧 Every Rule 69 citation in this section is also an IIHF citation.** The IIHF's Rule 69 carries the same subsection numbers as the NHL's — 69.1 the premise and the two grounds, 69.3 inside the crease, 69.4 outside it, 69.7 rebounds and loose pucks — and the substance is the same, with one difference in severity. ⚠️ **The NHL caps the give-ground penalty at a minor and the IIHF does not.** Both books disallow the goal where you make contact establishing your position and the attacker then fails to vacate your crease immediately; the NHL's 69.3 goes on to say that attacker *"will receive a minor penalty for goalkeeper interference"*, while the IIHF's says they *"will receive the appropriate penalty for 'Goalkeeper interference'"*. ⚠️ **The range itself is common to both books, so do not read the NHL as capping Rule 69 at a minor.** The parenthetical *"(minor or major, as the Referee deems appropriate)"* appears word for word at **69.2** in the NHL's book and the IIHF's alike, and Rule 69 as a whole reaches a major in each of them. ⚠️ **And 69.2 does not govern the give-ground clause, so treat it as context rather than as the authority.** Its opening confines it to cases where an attacking player initiates *"intentional or deliberate"* contact with the goalkeeper — and in the give-ground case it is **you** who initiated the contact, establishing position. The difference sits in 69.3 itself: the NHL writes a ceiling into that clause and the IIHF does not, so *"the appropriate penalty"* is not another way of writing *"a minor"* — under your book the severity there is left open. The IIHF's premise clause is materially identical: an attacking player's position *"whether inside or outside the goal crease, should not, by itself, determine whether a goal should be allowed or disallowed."* It is one word off the NHL's, which writes *"the crease"* where the IIHF writes *"the goal crease"*. The drafting difference that matters is vocabulary — the IIHF writes **"relevant contact"** where the NHL writes "contact", and defines it in 69.1 as *"any contact that is made between or among a goalkeeper and attacking Player(s), whether by means of a stick or any part of the body."* That is a definition, not a narrower test: the standard you are protected by is the same one. ⚠️ **What the IIHF's 69.1 does add is a sentence the NHL's has not got, and it is the only real-time relief anywhere in Rule 69:** *"If an attacking Player is in the goal crease and does not leave immediately, the Referee has the possibility to stop the game and the subsequent 'face-off' shall take place at the nearest neutral zone face-off spot outside the attacking zone of the offending team."* **Read both of its limits before you rely on it.** The IIHF's stop-the-game sentence is a *possibility*, not a duty — nothing in Rule 69.1 obliges the referee to use it — and it reaches a player **in** your crease, not one screening you from in front of it. So it does not make a screen appealable; it is a whistle for a body in the paint that will not move. **The NHL's Rule 69 has no counterpart** — its only face-off provision, 69.6, follows a goal that has already been disallowed, and the whole of Rules 69.1 to 69.8 was read for one. **And no published IIHF interpretation of this sentence is held here**, so how readily referees actually reach for it is not something this document can tell you.

> **⚠️ 🇬🇧 What is not the same is whether anyone can look at it again — and for this rule that is the whole difference.** IIHF Rule 69.1 ends by saying the rule *"will be enforced exclusively in accordance with the on-ice judgement of the Referee(s), but may be subject to a Video Review … or to a Coach's Challenge."* **The IHUK In-House Rules delete that half.** They amend Rule 69.1 to read *"On-ice video review is not available in EIH or SIHA competition"*, amend Rule 37 to say video review *"may not be used by referees, official scorers, timekeepers, or any other game official"*, and amend Rule 38 so that *"a Coach's Challenge is not permitted in EIH or SIHA competition."*
>
> **So in EIH and SIHA competition, goaltender interference is decided once, live, by a referee looking through traffic, and that is the end of it.** Goals disallowed on review in NHL highlights are decided in your league by whether the referee happened to see the contact. This has two practical consequences and they pull in opposite directions. **First: say it at the time or not at all.** There is no later. If someone is in your paint, the moment to make it visible is before the puck goes in, not after. **Second: expect to eat some.** A goal that would be wiped out on review in a televised game will stand in yours. That is not an injustice to argue about for the next ten minutes; it is the competition you signed up to. See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md) for the full amendment and the other four IIHF rules it strips review from.

> **⚠️ Under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada you are protected by a differently shaped rule — stronger inside your paint, weaker outside it.** Neither book runs 69.1's premise at all. Both start from an attacker's **position in your crease** rather than from whether you were impaired — but only USA Hockey's version is flat. **Hockey Canada's is not**, and the two books give opposite answers on a situation you will meet at the net front.
>
> - **USA Hockey Rule 625(b):** *"No goal may be scored with an attacking player in the goal crease unless the puck has preceded the player(s) into the goal crease or the goalkeeper is out of the goal crease area."* The same clause stops play before it gets that far — a neutral-zone face-off *"any time an attacking player stands, holds their stick, or skates through the goal crease provided the puck is in the attacking zone, the attacking team has possession of the puck and the goalkeeper is in contact with the crease."* Note the last condition: **that stoppage is yours only while you are in contact with your crease.** And note what 625(b) does *not* ask: it does not matter who shot it, and the preconditions it does set are whether the puck *"has preceded"* the attacker in and whether you were out of the crease area. **That second one runs out the moment the attacker is what stopped you getting back to it.** ⚠️ **And there is a third way the goal stands that is not about you at all, because one of your own team caused it.** The rule's next paragraph: *"However, if the attacking player has been physically interfered with by the actions of a defending player that causes them to be in the goal crease, play shall not be stopped and any legal goal scored shall be allowed."* If your own defenceman shoves an attacker into your paint, the position bar gives you nothing — though the relief answers only for where that attacker is standing, and Rule 625(a)(8) is still a minor for physical contact that interferes with your movement.
>
>   ⚠️ **That last precondition is only half the answer, and the half you are missing is the one that protects you.** The rule text stops at *"the goalkeeper is out of the goal crease area"*; the **USA Hockey Casebook 2025-29, Rule 625, Situation 9** puts exactly the play you will meet — you leave the crease to stop a shot, an attacker gets into it ahead of the puck, and a teammate scores — and answers *"Yes"*, the goal is good: *"If the goalkeeper is completely out of their crease, an attacking player may precede the puck into the crease and any legally scored goal in this situation shall be allowed."* **Then it gives the goal back to you if that attacker is what stopped you getting home:** *"However, if the goalkeeper is attempting to return to the goal crease and there is any interference by the attacking player who prevents the goalkeeper from making a play, then the goal must be disallowed and an interference penalty shall be assessed."* So being out of your paint does not surrender the crease — it surrenders it only for as long as you are genuinely out of the play. **If you were fighting your way back and somebody stood in the way, that is a disallowed goal and two minutes for them**, and it is worth knowing so you say it at the time rather than assuming there was nothing to say.
>
> - **Hockey Canada Rule 8.5 — and read the sentence after the one everybody quotes.** Its Goal Crease Area paragraph begins the same way: *"Unless the puck is in the goal crease area, a player of the attacking team may not stand in the goal crease. If the puck should enter the net while such conditions prevail, the goal will NOT BE ALLOWED."* Then it carves that case straight back out: *"However, if an attacking player is in the goal crease but does not interfere with the Goaltender and another attacking player (who is outside the goal crease) scores, the goal WILL BE ALLOWED provided that the player who was in the goal crease does not attempt to play the puck, interfere with the play or obstruct the Goaltender's view or movements."* **So a body in your paint while a teammate scores from outside it does not by itself take the goal off the board under Hockey Canada — and it does under USA Hockey.** What takes it off is that player *doing* something, and Hockey Canada writes that as a test in its own right: Interpretation 3 to Rule 8.5(a) says an attacker is *"NOT committing a foul by simply standing in the goal crease"*, but *"if while standing in the crease, the attacking player attempts to play the puck, interfere with the play, or impede the goaltender's vision or movements, then no goal may be scored."* **That is an impairment test and it names your vision** — the counterpart of NHL Rule 69.3, not of 69.1. Hockey Canada also separates disallowing the goal from punishing anyone, in terms: it is *"reasonable for a Referee to judge that a situation may warrant disallowing a goal under this rule without assessing an attacking player a penalty."* And it states the balance in a sentence no other book has: *"The onus is always on the attacking player and players who do not make an effort to avoid the goaltender must be penalized."*
>
> **Outside the paint the difference between the books narrows almost to nothing, and that is the half to plan around.** Rule 69.1 does name an attacker's **positioning** alongside contact — the NHL's clause (1) is the first of two grounds, and the rule reaches them under the words *"Goals should be disallowed **only if**"*: *"an attacking player, either by his positioning or by contact, impairs the goalkeeper's ability to move freely within his crease or defend his goal"*, and the IIHF's first ground is the same clause written in *"their"*, with *"a 'relevant contact'"* where the NHL writes *"contact"* — word for word the same in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions. ⚠️ **But that "only if" is a ceiling on when a goal may come off the board, not a direction to take one off — and every sentence in Rule 69 that actually disallows a goal for where an attacker stood is keyed to your crease.** Rule 69.1's own disallowing sentence reaches one who *"enters the goal crease"*; Rule 69.3's no-contact screening ground asks for a *"significant position within the goal crease"*; and Rule 69.4, the rule for outside it, has no positioning limb at all. **Both books then answer the screening case in terms, and against you:** NHL Reference Table 14 and IIHF 2025/26 Appendix IV Table 16, **Situations 5C and 5E** — an attacker outside the crease, or planted on the crease line, who obstructs your vision **and** impairs your ability to defend your goal — both answer *"Goal is allowed."* ⚠️ **That is a reading of the rule's structure rather than something either book states in terms**, and both add that Rule 69 *"will be enforced exclusively in accordance with the on-ice judgement of the Referee(s)"* — so it is what the text supports, not a promise about the call you will get. **What it means from behind the goal line is that 69.1 is not the lever against a screen that its wording suggests, and your defencemen are still the answer.** **Neither of the other two books writes even a clause that reaches outside the crease without contact** — inside it both of them have one, and those are the crease rules set out above. ⚠️ **That is a statement about what those two books write, and not a claim that they stop protecting you once you are outside your crease** — both of them can still take a goal off the board out there, and USA Hockey's route is the easy one to miss. **Rule 617(c)(4)** disallows a goal scored while an attacking player was *"interfering with the goalkeeper"*, a limb its own words do not confine to the crease, and its *Casebook* applies that limb to a player who is plainly outside one: **Rule 625, Situation 13** allows a goal with an attacker slid inside the goal frame behind the goal line, who is *"not in the goal crease area as the rulebook defines it"*, on the express condition that *"the player must not interfere with the goalkeeper in any way."* **What every one of those routes runs on is contact**, and both books say so in their own definitions. ⚠️ **So do not read any of this as the other books protecting you less once you leave the paint — on contact they protect you more.** **Hockey Canada 8.5** writes it in terms: *"A Goaltender is not 'fair game' just because they are outside their goal crease. A penalty under this rule will be called where an opposing player makes unnecessary contact with the goaltender **anywhere on the ice**."* And its Interpretation 1 disallows the goal for any contact an attacker initiates, *"regardless of whether the contact occurs inside or outside of the goal crease"* — narrower than the NHL's incidental-contact allowance, not wider. USA Hockey's playing rules contain **no provision about screening a goaltender or obstructing their vision at all** — its grounds are position *in the crease* and physical contact, 625(a)(8) reaching *"physical contact, using their stick or body, in a manner that interferes with the movement of the goalkeeper, unless otherwise specified in the rules."* Hockey Canada mentions your view twice and **both times only for a player who is in your crease** — the two clauses quoted above, one conditioned on standing *"in the goal crease"* and the other on *"while standing in the crease"*. For a **penalty** it asks for more again: it defines the foul as impeding you *"by actual physical contact"*, and no penalty follows *"unless the attacking player's body or stick makes actual physical contact with the goaltender."* **So under those two books a screen set outside your paint, without contact, is not merely "normally legal" — there is no provision to call it under.** Your defencemen are the entire answer. *(That is a search of the two playing rule books, and the separate USA Hockey Rules and Casebook has since been searched as well: its seventeen situations under Rule 625 reach the crease repeatedly and the goaltender's vision not once, and the word "screen" appears in the whole volume twice, neither time about a goaltender. So the USA Hockey half stands on both books. Hockey Canada's interpretations are printed inside the playing rules book already searched.)*
>
> **All four books address in terms the attacker who lands on you because your own defenceman put them there — but Hockey Canada is the only one that writes a mandatory penalty on the attacker into that clause itself, and the only one that also sends the referee after the defenceman who caused it.** Hockey Canada Interpretation 2 to Rule 8.5(a): where an attacker is *"tripped, hooked, cross-checked, or otherwise interfered with, falls, and makes contact with the goaltender, there must be an effort by the attacking player to avoid making contact with the goaltender. If the player does not make an effort to avoid contact with the goaltender, then they must be penalized for interference with the goaltender"* — and *"The referee should also penalize the defending player who committed the initial foul under the appropriate rule."* Its Interpretation 1 is the relief and it is narrow: the exception is an attacker *"fouled by a defending player and, as a result, is unable to avoid contact with the goaltender."*
>
> ⚠️ **The NHL's and the IIHF's version of this sits in Rule 69.1, and the reason Hockey Canada stands out is not that those two books let the attacker off.** Both write that where an attacker *"has been pushed, shoved, or fouled by a defending player so as to cause him to come into contact with the goalkeeper, such contact will not be deemed contact initiated by the attacking player for purposes of this rule, provided the attacking player has made a reasonable effort to avoid such contact"* — that is NHL Rule 69.1, and the IIHF's Rule 69.1 is the same sentence with *"them"* where the NHL writes *"him"*. **The proviso is the whole clause.** The relief runs only while the attacker is making a reasonable effort to avoid you. Without it the sentence does not apply at all, and you are back to the ordinary question of whether they initiated the contact — which, inside your crease, is exactly what the first sentence of Rule 69.3 turns on, incidental or otherwise, subject to the rebound and loose-puck exception at Rule 69.7 that both books carry. And neither book is short of mandatory penalty wording once it gets there: Rules 69.2 and 69.3 in each of them say the attacking player *"will receive"* a penalty. What neither writes anywhere in Rule 69 is a word about **penalising** the defenceman who put them there. Their **69.7** does name him — but only as a reason a goal *can be permitted*, never as somebody to penalise. Under USA Hockey the same push-in is relieved more broadly than under any of the other three — *"play shall not be stopped and any legal goal scored shall be allowed"*, with no effort proviso at all — but that relief reaches the attacker's **position** only. Rule 617(c)(4) disallows the goal on a second limb, *"or interfering with the goalkeeper"*, which the relief never touches.
>
> **⚠️ And Hockey Canada prices what was done to you, not only whether the goal counts — the tier that ejects sits in the same rule.** Everything above decides a goal. Rule 8.5's penalty limbs decide whether the player who ran you is still in the game. 8.5(a) is the minor. **8.5(b)** reaches *"A Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty"* at the referee's discretion *"based on the degree of violence of impact"* — and **mandatorily**, *"will be assessed"*, in two named cases: *"to any player who **charges the goaltender**"* (Note 1 sends the definition of charging to Rule 7.4(b) Interpretation 2), and *"to any player who **injures an opponent** by an Interference with the Goaltender infraction that would otherwise call for a Minor penalty."* **8.5(c)** is a **match penalty** for a player who *"attempts to injure or deliberately injures a goaltender by Interference."* **8.5(e)** makes the game misconduct automatic on any 8.5(b) major, and **8.5(d)** rules a misconduct out altogether — *"No Misconduct penalty may be assessed for interference with the goaltender"* — so there is no ten-minute rung between the two minutes and the ejection. *(Those are Hockey Canada's limbs. **The other three books have since been read for the same question, and all three write tiers above a minor** — USA Hockey's is set out in the next subsection. ⚠️ **The four do not line up on one axis, so no single book is simply "the strongest".** On the **floor**, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada both refuse to price running you at two minutes flat, and the NHL and the IIHF do not: NHL Rule 42.1 offers *"[a] minor, major or a major and a game misconduct"* for charging a goalkeeper in the crease, so a bare minor is available there and under the IIHF. On **charging specifically**, Hockey Canada is the highest of the four and the only mandatory one — 8.5(b) *"will be assessed"*. On the **spread of tiers**, USA Hockey's Rule 607 is the one with no bare minor at any limb at all.)*
>
> **Find out which book your league runs before you rely on any of this.** On a body in your paint while the puck goes in, the four books do not give the same answer.

### The tier for running you — and which of the four books prices it highest

```facts
Key: Everything above decides whether a goal counts; this decides whether the player who ran you is still in the game, and it is a protection you have rather than a restriction on you
Rule: USA Hockey has no bare minor for it — Rule 607(c) assesses "a minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct" to a player who body checks or charges a goalkeeper in the goal crease or privileged area, and 607(e) adds that a match penalty "may also be assessed for charging"
Rule: Your protected area there is bigger than your crease — USA Hockey 607(d) Note 2 runs the privileged area from the end-zone faceoff spots perpendicular to the end boards, and Casebook 607 Situation 6 confirms it "extends to the closer end boards"
Rule: USA Hockey 607(d) also protects you outside it — "A goalkeeper is NOT 'fair game' because they are outside the privileged area", and a penalty for interference or charging should be called in every case of unnecessary contact
Rule: Do not ask for the top tier on every bump — USA Hockey 607(d) Note 1 sends accidental or unavoidable contact to the Interference rule, a 625(a)(8) minor, and only deliberate body contact or a check to charging
Rule: USA Hockey Casebook 607 Situation 4 gives three rungs — no penalty only where a defending player forced them into you and they clearly tried to avoid it; minor plus misconduct on an honest attempt; major plus game misconduct or match where they accelerate through
Rule: The NHL and IIHF price charging you in your crease from a minor upward at the referee's discretion — NHL 42.2 to 42.5 run minor, major, match, and a game misconduct on a major causing a face or head injury; IIHF 42.2 to 42.4 run minor, major, major plus game misconduct
Rule: In Britain that IIHF ladder applies unamended — the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 amend Rule 69 for goaltender interference but leave Rule 42 alone, so charging you is priced by the IIHF book itself; no IHUK Rule Bulletin has been obtained here
Rule: Hockey Canada is the only one of the four that mandates ejection for it — 8.5(b) "A Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed to any player who charges the goaltender", 8.5(d) removes the misconduct rung, and 8.5 protects you "anywhere on the ice"
Action: Say what happened, not what you want — "he drove through me" gets you the charging rule; "that's a penalty" gets you nothing
```

⚠️ **The section above answers whether a goal counts. This one answers whether the player who ran you is still on the ice** — and a lot of goaltenders under USA Hockey believe their whole protection is the **625(a)(8)** minor quoted above. **It is not.** Charging you is a separate rule with a separate ladder, and **the rule writes no bare minor at any limb.**

**USA Hockey Rule 607(c):** *"A minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to a player who body checks or charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease **or privileged area**."* **Rule 607(e)** sits above it: *"A match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed for charging."* So the floor is two minutes **plus a misconduct**, and the ceiling ends their night. ⚠️ **How long that misconduct runs is not fixed at ten minutes outside adult play.** **Rule 404(a)** writes it as removal *"for a period of 10 minutes, **or the designated misconduct penalty time**"*, and the **Note** above it authorises affiliates and governing bodies, *"(Except for Adults)"*, to reduce it by period length — **8:00** where periods run *"[m]ore than 12 but less than 17 minutes"*, and **6:00** at *"12 minutes or less"*. **It is an authorisation, not an automatic cut**: ten minutes stands unless your affiliate has set otherwise, and it stands for adults whatever the periods run. **None of that moves the ladder** — 607(c) still writes no bare minor — it changes only how long the player who ran you actually sits.

⚠️ **And the area USA Hockey protects you in is much larger than your paint, which is the half most worth knowing.** **USA Hockey Rule 607(d) Note 2** defines the *privileged area* as *"an area outlined by connecting the end zone face-off spots with an imaginary line and imaginary lines from each face-off spot running perpendicular to the end boards"*, and the *Casebook* answers the obvious question at **Rule 607, Situation 6** — *"Does the goalkeeper's Privileged Area include the area behind the goal line? Yes… The goalkeeper's Privileged Area extends to the closer end boards."* **So you carry it out to the top of the circles, across the goal line, and behind your own net** — the whole of the ice you play the puck on, minus the corners. [Rink Map and Glossary](../foundation/rink_map_and_glossary.md) has the shape. And **USA Hockey 607(d)** does not abandon you outside it either: *"A goalkeeper is NOT 'fair game' because they are outside the privileged area. A penalty for interference or charging should be called in every case where an opposing player makes unnecessary contact with a goalkeeper."*

⚠️ **Now the counterweight, because a goalie who asks for a match penalty on every collision stops being listened to.** **USA Hockey Rule 607(d) Note 1** decides which rule the referee is even in: *"any **accidental or unavoidable** contact that occurs with the goalkeeper shall be penalized under the Interference rule"* — the 625(a)(8) minor — while *"[a]ny **deliberate body contact or check** that is delivered to the goalkeeper shall be penalized as charging."* The line is deliberateness, not how much it hurt. **And inside charging, the USA Hockey *Casebook* at Rule 607, Situation 4 publishes three rungs**, of which the top one is narrower than goaltenders assume:

- **No penalty at all — and it needs both of two conditions, not either one.** It applies only *"[i]n the case where a defending player has forced the attacking player into their own goalkeeper"*, and then only *"provided the attacking player has clearly made every attempt to avoid the contact with the goalkeeper."* ⚠️ **Both halves.** Your own defenceman shoving someone into you is not on its own an answer.
- **A minor plus misconduct** — where the attacker was *"going hard to the goal"* and *"makes an honest attempt to avoid the goalkeeper once it is realized contact is unavoidable"*.
- **A major plus game misconduct, or a match penalty** — where they *"make[] no attempt to avoid the goalkeeper and recklessly endanger[] the goalkeeper by accelerating through the contact, or by extending the arms or dropping the shoulder to deliver the hit."*

**The other three books all write tiers above a minor too — but only one of the three refuses to *start* at one.** **NHL Rule 42.1** puts the range in the rule text: *"A minor, major or a major and a game misconduct shall be imposed on a player who charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within his goal crease."* Its **42.2**, **42.3** and **42.4** then place the minor, the major and a match penalty at the Referee's discretion — the match where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured"* you — and **42.5** adds a game misconduct where a major is imposed for an infraction *"resulting in an injury to the face or head of an opponent"*. **IIHF Rule 42.1 — your book in Britain, and one nothing domestic alters** — writes the shorter sentence, *"A penalty shall be imposed on a Player who charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within their goal crease"*, with **42.2**, **42.3** and **42.4** running minor, major, and major plus game misconduct for reckless endangerment. ⚠️ **Neither of those two books has a privileged area — the word appears in neither — so outside your crease you are back on Rule 42.1's *"not 'fair game'"* sentence and on Rule 69.4, not on a drawn boundary.** ⚠️ **And read those two ranges for where they start: both books put a bare minor at the bottom of them**, so under the NHL and the IIHF running you can be priced at two minutes and nothing else, at the referee's discretion. **Hockey Canada is the one that cannot be**, as set out above: 8.5(b) *"will be assessed"* — a mandatory major plus game misconduct for charging you, with 8.5(d) removing the misconduct rung between, and 8.5's protection running *"anywhere on the ice"*. ⚠️ **So the honest comparison is not that one book is strongest.** Hockey Canada writes the highest and the only mandatory floor for charging a goaltender; USA Hockey's Rule 607 is the only one of the four with no bare minor at any of its limbs, for a goalkeeper or anyone else; and the NHL and the IIHF leave the whole range, minor upward, to the referee. **Which of those you are playing under is the thing to find out.**

⚠️ **🇬🇧 That "your book in Britain" is not an assumption.** The **IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27** open by recording that IHUK and the British Universities Ice Hockey Association *"adopt the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025) as the foundation for the rules of the game to be applied in England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland (the Home Countries)"*, and that *"[u]nless otherwise stated, all rules will be enforced in line with the IIHF Rule Book."* **And nothing in their amendments reaches Rule 42.** The amended rule numbers run up to 38 and then resume at 60, leaving 42 in the gap, and no charging provision appears anywhere in the document — so the IIHF ladder above is the ladder you play under, unaltered. **Two gaps in that go with it**, and [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md) sets them out: the In-House Rules say IHUK *"may also issue Rule Bulletins from time to time"* and no such Bulletin has been obtained here, and the individual leagues' competition regulations are not held either.

**What to do with it.** Describe the act, not the verdict. *"He drove through me"* and *"nobody pushed him"* are the two facts 607(d) Note 1 and Situation 4 actually turn on, and a referee who hears them is being handed the rule. *"That's a penalty"* is not information. ⚠️ **And in EIH and SIHA competition there is no review of any of it** — say it while it is happening or not at all.

### What it changes about what you do

```facts
Rule: Outside your crease incidental contact is permitted while you play the puck, if the attacker made a reasonable effort to avoid it (NHL and IIHF Rule 69.4)
Rule: On rebounds and loose pucks incidental contact — and only incidental contact — is permitted and the goal counts under the NHL and IIHF (69.7); neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada writes that carve-out, and the Hockey Canada clauses standing in its place bind the attacker, not you
Rule: A screen from outside your crease without contact is normally legal; inside the crease only a significant position voids the goal — a substantial part of a body there for more than an instantaneous period, obstructing your vision and impairing your ability to defend (NHL and IIHF Rule 69.3)
Rule: An attacker who pushes you into the net together with the puck after you made the stop kills the goal and may take a penalty with it — unless one of your own players fouled them into you, when it can be allowed (NHL and IIHF Rule 69.7; disallowed-goal lists 78.5(ix))
Rule: You can be penalised too — Rule 64.1 makes it a minor for diving/embellishment if you deliberately initiate contact other than to establish position in the crease, or if you otherwise act to create the appearance of other than incidental contact: selling it is two minutes, IIHF and NHL alike
Action: Establish and hold your crease position early, and shout for your defenceman rather than dropping to the ice
Never: Embellish — referees see everything you do all night, and a goalie who sells contact gets fewer calls when it matters
Rule: Establishing crease position is not immunity — if you do it with excessive force, or in a manner that would otherwise warrant a penalty, the penalty is yours at 6G and 6H: a minor at NHL Reference Table 14, "the appropriate penalty" and no cap at IIHF 2025/26 Table 16
Rule: 6F is your excessive force plus the attacker refusing to give ground — the NHL's Table 14 answers "Coincidental minors (goalkeeper/attacking player)"; the IIHF 2025/26 Table 16 says only "the appropriate penalty/ penalties to the goalkeeper and/or attacking Player", so it may be you, them or both
Rule: The stick and body fouls bind you as they bind a skater — slashing, cross-checking, roughing and the rest are written for "a player", which NHL and IIHF Rule 5.1 define as including goalkeepers; a blocker punch is a match penalty at NHL 51.3, a major plus automatic game misconduct at IIHF 51.3
Rule: An attacker who will not give ground after your contact may still be penalised — at 6D, NHL Table 14 and IIHF 2025/26 Table 16 both answer "a minor penalty is not assessed (loss of goal only)", but NHL 69.3 mandates a minor; IIHF 69.3 writes "the appropriate penalty", which may be no penalty at all
```

- **You are not "fair game" just because you are outside your crease (NHL and IIHF Rule 69.4).** Contact other than incidental contact that produces a goal means the goal is disallowed, and unnecessary contact should be penalised in every case. But **incidental contact is permitted** when you are in the act of playing the puck outside your crease, provided the attacker made a reasonable effort to avoid it. So leaving the paint to play a puck costs you some of your protection — not all of it, but some. Know that before you go.
- **Rebounds and loose pucks are the one place contact with you is permitted — and only *incidental* contact, under the NHL and IIHF books (Rule 69.7).** **Under those two books**, when you and an attacker are simultaneously going for a loose puck, inside or outside the crease, incidental contact is permitted and a resulting goal counts. ⚠️ **That is a carve-out for a scramble, not a licence to run you** — 69.7's very next paragraph disallows the goal where an attacker pushes you into the net together with the puck after you made the stop. ⚠️ **Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada writes a rebound carve-out at all** — and the Hockey Canada clauses that stand where it would be bind the **attacking player**, not you. Its Interpretation 1 to 8.5(a) disallows the goal for *any* contact **an attacking player** initiates, *"regardless of whether the contact occurs inside or outside of the goal crease"*, and Rule 8.5's onus sentence puts the mandatory minor on **them**: *"The onus is always on the attacking player and players who do not make an effort to avoid the goaltender must be penalized."* So Hockey Canada is stricter than 69.7 — but stricter **against the attacker**. Do not wait for a whistle in a scramble; win the puck.
- **A screen set from outside your crease, without contact, is normally legal** — both qualifiers belong to the legality claim itself; a significant vision-obstructing position established *inside* the blue paint voids the goal under Rule 69.3. ⚠️ **But read what that rule actually asks for, because it demands a good deal more than "he was in my paint and I could not see."** It disallows the goal where an attacking player *"establishes a significant position within the goal crease, so as to obstruct the goalkeeper's vision **and** impair his ability to defend his goal"* — and it then defines the first half rather than leaving it to the eye: a player does that *"when, in the Referee's judgment, his body, or a substantial portion thereof, is within the goal crease **for more than an instantaneous period of time**."* That is NHL Rule 69.3; the IIHF's Rule 69.3 is the same test written in *"their"*, identical in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions. **So a body that crosses your paint and is gone does not meet it** — and the limb asks for the obstruction *and* the impairment, not the obstruction alone. Argue past that and you are arguing for a call the rule does not give you. See [Screens, Tips and Traffic](#screens-tips-and-traffic) above, which is where the skill of dealing with it lives. Rule 69.1 names positioning as well as contact, but under a *"should be disallowed only if"* ceiling, and Rule 69.1's own disallowing sentence reaches an attacker who *"enters the goal crease"* — so do not read it as a lever against a screen set outside your paint, where both books answer that exact case *"Goal is allowed"* (NHL Reference Table 14 and IIHF 2025/26 Appendix IV Table 16, Situations 5C and 5E). It is in any event a post-goal judgement rather than real-time relief — 69.1's one real-time limb, which is the IIHF's and not the NHL's, reaches a player **in** your crease who will not leave, never one standing in front of it — **and outside the paint under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada, not even that.** USA Hockey has no goaltender-vision provision at all to hang it on, and Hockey Canada's does not reach past the crease line: its ground is a player *"standing in the crease"* who impedes your *"vision or movements"*. If you are blind, that is a problem for you and your defencemen to solve.
- **Being pushed into your own net with the puck by an attacker means the goal is disallowed — and that is written into 69.7 itself, and again on both books' disallowed-goal lists.** The NHL's words: *"In the event that a goalkeeper has been pushed into the net together with the puck by an attacking player after making a stop, the goal will be disallowed. If applicable, appropriate penalties will be assessed."* **The IIHF writes the same paragraph at the same number**, and both books list the play among the goals that must be disallowed — NHL 78.5(ix) and IIHF 78.5(IX), *"When a goalkeeper has been pushed into the net together with the puck after making a save."* ⚠️ **But it is not automatic, and the exception is in the same paragraph:** *"If, however, in the opinion of the Referee, the attacking player was pushed or otherwise fouled by a defending player causing the goalkeeper to be pushed into the net together with the puck, the goal can be permitted."* **So your own defenceman shoving an opponent into you can cost your team the goal.** One more paragraph covers the version where the puck is under somebody in front of you: where the puck is under a player in or around the crease, deliberately or otherwise, *"a goal cannot be scored by pushing this player together with the puck into the goal"* — and 69.7 adds that if the puck is *"deemed to be covered in the crease deliberately"* the appropriate penalties follow, a penalty shot among them (the rule points you to Rule 63, delaying the game).
- **You can be penalised too, and not only for interference.** If, by your actions outside your crease, you deliberately interfere with an attacker trying to play the puck or an opponent, that is on you. **And the ordinary stick and body fouls bind you exactly as they bind a skater.** Slashing, cross-checking, high-sticking, roughing, hooking, holding and tripping are each written for *"a player"* in the NHL and IIHF books — none of them mentions a goalkeeper at all — and Rule 5.1 defines that word as *"both skaters and goalkeepers"*. So a chop at an attacker's stick in your blue paint, or a cross-check to move a body off your doorstep, is the same two minutes it would be anywhere else on the ice. **[Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md) sets those fouls out in full, and it is written for you as much as for the other five.** ⚠️ **One of them is written about you specifically, and it is not a minor.** Punching an opponent with the blocking glove appears in both books' goalkeeper tables — the NHL's Reference Table 11 at its thirteenth row, and the IIHF's Table 13 — Table 11 in the IIHF's 2026/27 edition, same row, same 51.3 reference — and the two books price it differently. **NHL Rule 51.3** allows a **match penalty** where a goalkeeper uses the blocker *"to punch an opponent in an attempt to or to deliberately injure an opponent"*. **IIHF Rule 51.3** — your book in Britain — allows *"a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty to a goalkeeper who uses their blocker glove to punch an opponent and the action is likely to result in injury to the opponent."* Note where the IIHF threshold sits: **likely to injure**, not intending to. And note who pays for the smaller stuff — under **NHL and IIHF Rule 27.1** a teammate designated by your coach serves your minor, so your retaliation is your team's penalty kill and you are the one who has to stop it.
- **Don't embellish — there is a named penalty for it, and it names goalkeepers.** The rule is enforced on the referee's on-ice judgement, subject to a coach's challenge in the NHL — **and to nothing at all in EIH or SIHA competition**, as above. **Rule 64.1 writes a goalkeeper-specific clause into diving/embellishment in both the IIHF and NHL books, in identical terms.** The IIHF's, which is your book in Britain: *"A goalkeeper who deliberately initiates contact with an attacking Player other than to establish position in the crease, or who otherwise acts to create the appearance of other than 'incidental contact' with an attacking Player, is subject to the assessment of a minor penalty for 'diving / embellishment'."* Selling it is not just reputationally expensive under the rules you play — it is two minutes. And referees see everything you do all night, so a goalie with a reputation for it gets fewer calls in the games where it matters.

**Practically:** establish and hold your crease position early; if someone is standing in it, make it visible and shout for your defenceman rather than dropping to the ice. And accept the part you cannot change — a screen set from **outside** your crease, without contact, is legal, and dealing with it is a skill, not an injustice. What is *not* legal is a **significant** position established inside your blue paint — a substantial part of a body in the crease for more than an instantaneous period — that obstructs your vision and impairs your ability to defend the goal (Rule 69.3). That voids the goal, so say so out loud at the time. ⚠️ **Rule 69.1's other limb — positioning anywhere that actually stops you moving or defending — is the weaker of the two, and do not expect it to rescue you from a screen set outside the paint.** Both books publish worked situations on that exact fact pattern: NHL Reference Table 14 and IIHF 2025/26 Appendix IV Table 16, **Situations 5C and 5E** — an attacker outside the crease who obstructs your vision and impairs your ability to defend your goal — and both answer *"Goal is allowed."* They are appendix interpretations rather than operative rule text, so they do not repeal 69.1, but they are printed in both rulebooks and both come down on the screener's side. ⚠️ **The IIHF table numbers in this section are the 2025/26 book's.** The 2026/27 edition merges three earlier tables in the same appendix, which shifts every later one down by two, so this table is numbered **Table 14** there — the situation letters and their answers are identical in both. [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) carries the full comparison. **Under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada that positioning limb does not exist at all, and the crease rule is written differently in each of them**: under USA Hockey a body in your crease voids the goal on position alone; under Hockey Canada it voids the goal only once that body plays the puck, interferes with the play, or obstructs your view or movements. **Outside the crease, neither book disallows a goal for anything an attacker does to you short of contact** — Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.5(a) disallows the goal for contact an attacker initiates *"regardless of whether the contact occurs inside or outside of the goal crease"*, and USA Hockey's Rule 617(c)(4) disallows one scored while an attacking player was *"interfering with the goalkeeper"*, a limb its own words do not confine to the crease and which Rule 625(a)(8) reaches only through *"physical contact, using their stick or body, in a manner that interferes with the movement of the goalkeeper, unless otherwise specified in the rules."* **The IIHF Situation Handbook confirms the blunt version at Situation 69.1:** a player standing in the crease screening the goalkeeper when a goal is scored means *"The goal shall be disallowed, and no additional penalty assessed."* **And it answers the mirror image at Situation 69.2 — the one where you are the one who moves.** A goalkeeper comes across the crease and initiates contact with an attacker standing with one foot in the paint; the puck goes in as the contact is made.
