The answer: *"No. If a goalkeeper, in the act of establishing its position within the crease, initiates contact with an attacking player who is in the goal crease, and this results in an impairment of the goalkeeper's ability to defend the goal, and a goal is scored, the goal will be disallowed. No goal – no penalty. The attacking player must give ground to the goalkeeper to play its position within the goal crease."* ⚠️ **Note the second half: no goal *and* no penalty.** Both books' appendix tables answer the same play the same way — NHL Reference Table 14 and IIHF 2025/26 Appendix IV Table 16, both at **Situation 6D**: *"Goal is disallowed. A minor penalty is not assessed (loss of goal only)."* ⚠️ **But the tables and the rule text disagree here — and it is the NHL's book, not the IIHF book you play under in Britain, where they disagree outright.** Rule 69.3's third paragraph reaches exactly 6D's facts — an attacker who, after your contact, does not immediately vacate their position in the crease, which the rule glosses as giving ground to the goalkeeper. **The NHL's sentence leaves the referee nothing:** *"In all such cases, whether or not a goal is scored, the attacking player will receive a minor penalty for goalkeeper interference"* — set against its own Table 14 answering that a minor *"is not assessed."* **The IIHF's 69.3 writes *"the appropriate penalty"* into that same slot, and no penalty at all is one of the things that phrase can carry, so the IIHF book accommodates its own table where the NHL's cannot.** ⚠️ **Nothing published reconciles the NHL's two answers, and nothing outside that rulebook ranks one of them above the other** — Table 14 is printed in the same book as Rule 69.3, so the conflict is internal to it, and this document does not pick a side in it. What survives under either book is the narrow thing: do not tell an opponent standing in your crease that the rules give them a free one — under the NHL's own rule text they are penalised, and under the IIHF's they get whatever the referee judges appropriate, which may be nothing. ⚠️ **And the row beside it is the one that protects you, so know it is there: an attacker does not have to refuse to move for the goal to come off.** Both tables carry a **Situation 6C** — you initiate contact to establish position and the attacking player *"vacates the position immediately at the time a goal is scored"* — and the answer is still *"Goal is disallowed"*, because *"Even though the attacking player vacates his position immediately, the contact impairs the goalkeeper's ability to defend his goal"* (the IIHF's row is the same sentence in *"their"*). The **IIHF Situation Handbook** gives it in its own words at **Situation 69.6**: *"The goal is disallowed, even though the attacking player vacates the established position immediately, the contact impairs the goalkeeper's ability to defend its goal."* **What 6C turns on is the impairment, not their refusal**, and its authority is Rule 69.3's *second* paragraph rather than the third: the goal is disallowed where a goalkeeper, *"in the act of establishing his position within his goal crease, initiates contact with an attacking player who is in the goal crease, and this results in an impairment of the goalkeeper's ability to defend his goal"*. ⚠️ **Do not carry 6D's "no penalty" across to it, in either direction: 6C says nothing at all about a penalty**, where 6D states in terms that none is assessed. **Neither book's 6C row, nor the Situation Handbook at 69.6, says anything either way** — and no published interpretation of that silence was found for this document. ⚠️ **But do not read that as immunity for you, because it is not.** Rule 64.1's *"other than to establish position"* carve-out is scoped to **diving / embellishment** and nothing else. Where you establish position *"by using excessive force or acting in a manner which would otherwise warrant a penalty"*, the penalty is **yours**: IIHF 2025/26 Table 16 Situations **6G and 6H** put it on the goalkeeper alone, the table saying only that the Referee *"must assess the appropriate penalty to the goalkeeper"*, which caps nothing — where the NHL's Table 14 answers those two rows in minutes: *"A minor penalty to the goalkeeper and the goal is disallowed"* and *"Minor penalty to goalkeeper."* ⚠️ **Situation 6F is the one row where the penalty can reach both of you — and it takes both halves to get there.** It is not simply the row where an attacker will not move: 6F is where you establish position *"by using excessive force or acting in a manner which would otherwise warrant a penalty"* **and** the attacking player *"refuses to give ground at the time a goal is scored."* The difference between 6F and 6D is your own conduct, not theirs. **The two books do not price it the same way.** The NHL's Table 14 answers *"Coincidental minors (goalkeeper/attacking player)"* — two minutes each, both of you, every time. The IIHF's is looser: the Referee assesses *"the appropriate penalty/ penalties to the goalkeeper **and/or** attacking Player"*, which caps nothing and does not require that both of you are punished. So under the IIHF book you play in Britain, Situation 6F — your excessive force plus an attacker who will not give ground as the goal goes in — may land a penalty on the goalkeeper alone. The Situation Handbook answers **6G's** facts at **69.5** — it reaches neither 6H nor 6F — and it does cap that row where the table does not: *"A minor penalty to the goalkeeper and the goal is disallowed."* ⚠️ **Its own rule reference on that answer points at *"Table 16 (Rule 69 Situation 6C)"*, which is the wrong row** — the facts 69.5 sets out are 6G's, excessive force and all. That is the Handbook's slip, recorded here so it does not throw you when you look it up. Even a clean attempt is judged, in identical words in both books at **6B**: *"A possible penalty depends on the Referee's judgment as to the degree of contact and degree of resistance with the attacking player and whether the goalkeeper was truly trying to establish a position."* ⚠️ **But read who pays at 6B, because it is not you.** What that sentence governs is a penalty on the **attacking** player: the NHL's Table 14 answers *"Minor penalty. This player runs the risk of 'bad things' happening by being in the crease"*, and the IIHF's Table 16 says it outright — *"The Referee in their judgment must assess the appropriate penalty to the attacking Player."* **6B judges whether your attempt to establish position was genuine; it does not price it against you.** The rows that do reach the goalkeeper are 6E — where you initiate contact *"other than to establish position in the crease, or otherwise act[] to make unnecessary contact"* — and 6F, 6G and 6H, all three of which turn on establishing position *"by using excessive force"*. [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) carries the same table-against-rule-text tension for Rule 69.1 and the screening rows.

**One thing worth knowing that is not in Rule 69 at all.** An attacker who holds a glove or a stick directly in front of your face to blind you is not doing a legal screen — under the IIHF's published answer it is not judged under Rule 69 but under **Rule 75, unsportsmanlike conduct**, and it is a minor. IIHF Situation Handbook, Situation 69.3, asks whether an attacking player *"can … hold its hand/glove or stick directly in front of the goalkeeper's eyes/face with the obvious intent of impairing the ability to see the puck/play"* and answers: *"No. This shall be treated as unsportsmanlike conduct and penalized with the assessment of a minor penalty under Rule 75 if witnessed by the Referee(s)."* Note the last four words. With no video review in EIH or SIHA competition, *"if witnessed"* is the whole rule — so call it loudly while it is happening.

---

## What a Goalie Needs From Their Skaters

```facts
Action: Clear the net front with technique — inside shoulder ahead of theirs, box out, and lift their stick up rather than sideways and below their bottom hand
Never: Screen your own goalie, or back into the crease when you are being pushed. You arrive backwards onto a goaltender who is down, square to the shot and unable to see you coming
Never: Block a shot from a screening position. Get to the shooter and block from close, head up and out of the shot lane, or get out of the lane entirely
Action: On a 2-on-1 take the pass and give your goalie the shot, and don't slide before the pass is released unless the timed slide is your team's system
Action: Let your goalie play the puck when they call for it — "I've got it" means get to your breakout position — and tell them what you want while you are still coming back, not as you arrive
Never: Shoot at your goalie's head in warm-ups, or fire pucks at them from ten feet in drills — only two books stop play for the hit itself; the rest need a referee to judge the goalie hurt first
Rule: Warm-up is inside the rules — all four books keep each team in its own half (NHL and IIHF Rule 86.6; USA Hockey 636(c); Hockey Canada 2.2(i)); USA Hockey 502(b) and Hockey Canada 2.2(j) let officials penalise it there, while the NHL and the IIHF report it instead
Rule: If your goalie's stick breaks, carry the replacement and put it in their hand — NHL Rule 10.4 bars throwing or shooting it and IIHF 10.4 requires the stick be "passed from hand to hand", a minor to you either way
Rule: Hockey Canada names sliding it too, and does not cap it at a minor — Interpretation 1 to Rule 3.2(c): a minor under 10.5(a), a penalty shot if you slide it at the puck or puck carrier in your defending zone, and a second minor to the goalie who picks it up (10.6(a))
Action: Box out on rebounds — find a body, not the puck
Never: Blame the goalie out loud; answer their calls instead, because one word back is enough
```

**This section is written for the other five players.** Many net-front goals against are not goalie errors at all — they are skater habits that make the goalie's job impossible. That is a coaching judgement rather than a measured share: no goaltending source here counts goals by cause. Two measurements in reach answer narrower questions: [Game Management](../systems/game_management.md) counts the tip, deflection and rebound share of goals scored with an extra attacker on the ice, and [Offensive Zone Play](../systems/offensive_zone_play.md) counts the share of all goals that arrive within three seconds of a preceding shot — a proxy for the rebound, counted from the attacking side. Neither reaches the screen: the play-by-play feed both use carries no marker for one, so the net-front cause this section leans on hardest is exactly the one that stays uncounted.

- **Clear the net front, with technique.** Get your inside shoulder ahead of theirs, box out, and lift their stick — **up rather than sideways, and below their bottom hand**, because at or near the hands it is hooking under three of the four books — on the location alone under the IIHF's 55.1, and under USA Hockey's 623 and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to 8.2(a) where it stops the opponent playing the puck. ⚠️ **One qualification on the IIHF, and the book does not resolve it:** 55.1's very next sentence exempts contact that is stick-to-stick only *"when a Player is checking another"* — which a lift under the shaft is — so the two sentences pull against each other, no IIHF interpretation reconciling them is held here, and the caution here may be stricter than the rule. Note the scope of the exemption even so: it covers a player **checking** an opponent, not stick-on-stick contact generally. Low is still the answer. Only the NHL leaves a clean lift there alone, so do not carry that book's answer into the others. Position *usually* beats strength — [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md) covers that claim and flags it as coaching consensus rather than a measured law, with a large enough strength gap winning anyway — and a stick lift takes away the tip and the rebound without giving up your own body position — while repeated cross-checks to the back put your team down a man. Full detail in [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md).
- **Do not screen your own goalie.** Standing in front of your own net with your back to the shooter, in your goalie's sightline, is a cause of goals against that goalies raise constantly and skaters almost never think about — coaching observation, not a counted ranking. If you are the one blocking the view, move. ⚠️ **And do not back into the crease when you are being pushed — a goal against is not the only thing it costs.** You arrive there backwards, onto a goaltender who is usually down, square to the shooter and unable to see you, with the post beside the pair of you — so it is a collision as well as a screen, and the answer is to fight the shove early rather than ride it into the paint. **That second half is coaching caution: no source here counts injuries from it.** From your side of the play this is what the **"screen"** call is for, and it is the argument for making it before the shot rather than after. [Body Contact and Battles → Defending the net front](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md#defending-the-net-front) is the same collision written for the skater doing the backing.
- **Do not block a shot from a screening position.** Half-blocking a puck your goalie cannot see is *worse* than not blocking it, because you have just deflected an unseen puck. Get to the shooter and block from close, head up and out of the shot lane, or get out of the lane entirely. There is no useful middle. [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md) has the rest of what to protect, and the full list of when not to block at all.
- **On a 2-on-1, take the pass and give your goalie the shot** — and do not slide or dive before the pass is released, unless your team plays the timed slide as its method, in which case the slide is the plan. Say which half you are taking, and make sure your defenceman has the same answer you do.
- **Let your goalie play the puck when they call for it.** "I've got it" means get to your breakout position, not to the puck. Two players arriving at the same puck behind your own net is how goals get scored on empty nets.
- **Tell your goalie what you want before you need it.** If you want the puck stopped and left behind the net, say so while you are still coming back for it — a defenceman who calls "leave it" from the neutral zone gets a dead puck waiting for them; the same call as they arrive gets a collision.
- **If their stick breaks, hand them yours.** A goalie may not go to the bench for a replacement and must receive one from a teammate on the ice — NHL and IIHF Rule 10.4, USA Hockey Rule 605(c), which puts a minor for delay of game on the goalie who goes to the bench during a stoppage. **Carry it and put it in their hand — never throw, shoot or slide it.** NHL Rule 10.4 puts a minor on the teammate who throws or shoots it, IIHF Rule 10.4 requires that *"[t]he stick must be passed from hand to hand"*, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 3.2(c) puts a minor on you under 10.5(a) for throwing, shooting, kicking or sliding it — **and, unlike the other books, a second minor on your goalie under 10.6(a) if they pick it up off the ice.** ⚠️ **Note which book each limb comes from, and note that Hockey Canada does not stop at two minutes.** The slide is named by Hockey Canada and reached by the IIHF's hand-to-hand requirement; **NHL Rule 10.4 names only throwing or shooting** — though its own index routes *"sliding stick"* to that rule, so do not read the gap as a permission. And the same Hockey Canada interpretation adds that *"A Penalty Shot may be awarded under this rule, if the player throws, shoots, kicks, or slides the stick at the puck or puck carrier in their defending zone."* The minor is the floor there, not the exposure. ⚠️ **One permission runs the other way, and it is the IIHF's alone: a stick your goalie dropped intact is not the same as a replacement for a broken one.** IIHF Rule 10.4 lets an abandoned, intact stick *"accidentally dropped by the goalkeeper"* be *"passed from hand to hand to the goalkeeper, or moved, pushed, or slid toward the goalkeeper by a teammate near the goalkeeper on the ice"* — provided the moved stick does not interfere with play or distract opponents, and *"a minor penalty or 'penalty shot'"* if it does. The NHL writes the opposite, extending its throw-or-shoot ban to *"situations where the goalkeeper has lost his stick and a teammate is trying to return it to him."* **Put it in their hand and you are right in all four books.** You can play a shift without a stick far more cheaply than they can.
- **Box out on rebounds.** After the save, the goalie is on the ice recovering. The most dangerous player is the one arriving unmarked at the second puck. Find a body, not the puck.
- **Answer their calls.** A goalie whose calls get no response stops making them, and then you have lost the only player with a full view of the ice. One word back is enough.
- **Don't shoot at your goalie's head in warm-ups**, and don't fire pucks at them from ten feet in drills. Both are more common than they should be. **Not shooting there is a coaching caution rather than a rule of hockey** — and there are two separate reasons to take it seriously, one about their head and one about the penalty sheet.
  - ⚠️ **Only two books stop play for the hit itself — everywhere else a referee has to judge the goalie hurt first.** Every book *does* write a stoppage for an injured goalkeeper (**NHL and IIHF Rule 8.1**, **USA Hockey Rule 206(a)**, **Hockey Canada Rule 2.4(b)**, **CARHA Rule 20(d)**), so "no rule covers them" would be wrong. The catch is the trigger: all of those ask an official to read an injury off a player, which is what a goalie behind a cage supplies least of. Only **IIHF Rule 9.6** and **Hockey Canada Rule 6.18(a)** key a stoppage to the puck hitting the mask, and both are narrower than they sound — the IIHF's is a *"may"* that turns on the referee believing the goalkeeper is *"dazed"* and is cancelled by an immediate scoring chance; Hockey Canada's *"shall"* reaches only *"categories of U13 and below"*. **The NHL, USA Hockey's stand-up rules and CARHA write no mask-specific counterpart at all** — USA Hockey's only version of it is a Sled Hockey rule, and CARHA's Rule 24(f) stops play when a goalkeeper *loses* their helmet rather than when one takes the shot and keeps it on. So a shot you take at their head in a drill is one no official is obliged to notice. The full picture, and what a goalie should do about it, is under *If you take one off the mask*.
  - ⚠️ **And the warm-up is inside the rules, not outside them.** All four books confine each team to its own half of the ice: **NHL and IIHF Rule 86.6** — *"each team shall confine its activity to its own end of the rink"*, both cross-referencing their own fighting rule at that point — **USA Hockey Rule 636(c)**, which gives each team its warm-up *"on their half of the rink"*, and **Hockey Canada Rule 2.2(i)** — the letter, in a list running (a) to (l), not a Roman numeral — under which *"players will be restricted to their own half of the ice"*. So shooting at the **opposition's** goalie in warm-up breaks a rule in all four, which shooting at your own does not. ⚠️ **What happens next splits two-and-two, and the half that matters to a British reader is not the penalty half.** **USA Hockey Rule 502(b)** says *"[p]enalties may also be assessed during the warm-up period"*, and **Hockey Canada Rule 2.2(j)** that the referee *"will penalize any player who commits a violation of the rules during the pre-game warm-up"*, with the penalty *"served at the start of the game"*. 🇬🇧 **The NHL and the IIHF report it rather than penalising it** — NHL 86.6 has a violation *"reported to the Commissioner"*, IIHF 86.6 *"reported to the Proper Authorities"*, both under **Rule 86.6**, which is the operative one. ⚠️ **IIHF Rule 5.6 is often reached for here and will not carry it:** its *"No penalties are to be assessed but a report of the incident must be submitted to the Proper Authorities"* — word for word in the 2025/26 (v1.1) and 2026/27 editions — follows three provisions about protective equipment, players not on the roster sheet and warming up at a stoppage, 5.6 does not confine the teams to their own halves at all, and the same book penalises fighting before the drop of the puck at Rule 46.10. **Neither rule is a licence** — reporting routes the incident past the referee to the people who decide whether you play next week.
- **Never blame the goalie out loud.** They already know. The only thing your comment changes is their next twenty minutes.

---

## Equipment Notes

Brief here — full treatment in [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md).

### Protection, and the neck laceration protector

```facts
Rule: Everything except your skates and stick must be built solely for protection, with no undue assistance in keeping goal (NHL and IIHF Rule 11.1)
Rule: Your mask is required equipment and it is not a skater's helmet — IIHF Rule 11.8 (same words in both editions), USA Hockey 304(d) HECC-certified below Adults, Hockey Canada 3.6(b) a CSA helmet with a CSA facial protector attached
Rule: An expired certification sticker is no certification — USA Hockey Rule 304(c); and Hockey Canada 3.6(b) says any alteration to a certified helmet or facial protector "automatically destroys the certification"
Rule: Across the Home Countries — England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland — a neck laceration protector is mandatory for everyone, adults included, with no warning and an immediate 10-minute misconduct (IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27, Rule 9.12)
Rule: If it comes off in play you must leave the ice and may not retrieve it, and a second offence escalates to a game misconduct (IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27, Rule 9.12)
Rule: Nothing exempts university hockey — the BUIHA adopts the In-House Rules alongside IHUK and they are "the overriding interpretation" domestically, but Rule 9.12's own competition list names IHUK, EIH and SIH and not the BUIHA, so wear it and ask your league (IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27)
Rule: IIHF Rule 9.12 is the base — mandatory in all categories, minor penalty after a warning; the In-House Rules remove the warning
Rule: Neck laceration protection is mandatory and a dangler does not satisfy it — Hockey Canada Rule 3.6(c) requires a BNQ-certified throat protector for players registered in minor and female hockey
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 304(g) requires a HECC-certified neck laceration protector in every age classification except Adults
Action: Borrow the pads, blocker, trapper and chest protector; buy the mask — an in-date sticker is a certification clock, not a condition report, and tells you nothing about a loaned mask's impact history
Action: Replace gear you have outgrown — gear that hurts you teaches you to flinch, and that cannot be coached out; fit and comfort beat brand and looks, and dull edges destroy lateral movement first
```

- **Everything except your skates and stick must be built solely for protection** (NHL Rule 11.1, and IIHF Rule 11.1 in near-identical words). You may not wear anything that gives "undue assistance in keeping goal." At professional level, pad width, pad height, chest protector and pant dimensions are all regulated and inspected. The pad specifics are in **Rule 11.2**, not 11.1: NHL leg pads "shall not exceed eleven inches (11") in extreme width when on the leg of the player", and each goalie's maximum pad height is set individually by the League from their own floor-to-knee and knee-to-pelvis measurements. **IIHF Rule 11.2 sets the same width in metric — "The leg guards worn by goalkeepers shall not exceed 28cm in extreme width" — but sets no individual height entitlement**, so the NHL's per-goalie height calculation is a League scheme, not something to expect anywhere else.
- **Your mask is required equipment, it is not a skater's helmet, and its certification is the part you can actually check.** **IIHF Rule 11.8**, in the same words in the 2025/26 (v1.1) and 2026/27 editions: *"Goalkeepers must wear their face mask at all times during game action. Protective face masks of a design approved by the IIHF must be worn by goalkeepers…"* and *"[t]he face mask must be constructed in such a way that a puck cannot penetrate the openings."* **USA Hockey Rule 304(d)** requires *"[a]ll players, including goalkeepers, in all age classifications below Adults"* to wear a **HECC-certified** facemask, and **Rule 304(c)** makes an expired sticker no certification at all — *"a helmet that has an expiration date that has expired is no longer considered certified."* **Hockey Canada Rule 3.6(b)** requires *"all players, including goaltenders"* to wear a **CSA-certified** helmet *"to which a CSA-certified facial protector must be securely attached and not altered in any way"*, and adds that *"[a]ny alteration to a CSA-certified helmet or facial protector automatically destroys the certification."* ⚠️ **So look at the sticker on anything you are loaned or handed down — and then read the second half of this bullet, because the sticker is where the checking starts and not where it stops.** An expiry date is a **certification clock, not a condition report**: it tells you when the certification lapses, and nothing at all about how many pucks that shell has taken or what state the liner underneath is in. Foams absorb energy by crushing, and crushed foam does not un-crush. A club's spare mask has been on more heads and stopped more pucks than any hand-me-down, and nobody has logged them. **So the working answer is the one [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md) lands on, and it owns this: borrow the pads, the blocker, the trapper and the chest protector; buy the mask.** Ask what a loaned mask's age and impact history are by all means — but a mask nobody can account for is one you do not put your head in. *(That last step is a reading of the certification rules above rather than a sentence any rule book writes: no book distinguishes a loaned mask from an owned one.)*
- **🇬🇧 Neck laceration protection is mandatory for you, whatever your age, and this is the rule most likely to catch out a British goalie who learned the game from North American material.** This is the signature catastrophic injury in your position, so treat it as the first line of the section rather than the last. **Across all four Home Countries** — the IHUK In-House Rules are adopted by Ice Hockey UK and the British Universities Ice Hockey Association, and applied *"in England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland (the Home Countries)"* — a neck laceration protector is *"mandatory for all players in all IIHF categories and IHUK/EIH/SIH competitions"* — **adults included** — with **no warning given** and an **immediate 10-minute misconduct** for going without, a requirement to leave the ice if it comes off in play, and a second offence that escalates to a game misconduct. **If you play in Wales or Northern Ireland, that is your rule too**, and if you play university hockey it reaches you as well: the In-House Rules are *"the overriding interpretation of how the IIHF Rule Book is to be applied domestically"*, the BUIHA adopts them alongside IHUK, and the BUIHA section of the book routes a fixture against a non-BUIHA opponent back to the standard IHUK in-house rules. Rule 9.12's own competition list names IHUK, EIH and SIH rather than the BUIHA, and no clause says in terms that the list reaches university hockey — but nothing exempts it either, and the underlying mandate is the IIHF's own, which covers every category of IIHF competition. That is stricter than the IIHF book it amends: **IIHF Rule 9.12** already makes it mandatory *"For all Players in all categories of IIHF Competition"* but assesses only a minor *"after warning by the Referee."* The In-House Rules delete the warning. Enforcement detail, the visor and chin-strap rules that sit beside it, and the rest of the amendment list are in [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).

  **The dangler is an addition, not a substitute, under every book that addresses it.** IIHF Rule 9.12 permits it separately — *"A goalkeeper may attach a throat protector to the chin of their facemask"* — as a permission alongside the mandate, not in place of it. **Hockey Canada Rule 3.6(c)** says so outright, requiring a **BNQ-certified throat protector** for players registered in minor and female hockey and adding that *"goaltenders who wear an attachment to the mask or helmet designed to protect the throat, must still wear a BNQ-certified throat protector."* **USA Hockey Rule 304(g)** requires a **HECC-certified neck laceration protector** *"that covers as much of the neck area as possible"* for all players including goalkeepers in **every age classification except Adults** (BNQ-certified protectors also satisfy it through the 2026-27 season; Adults are strongly recommended to wear one) — **and that adult exemption is the North American position, not yours.** Full standards, certification periods and the Canada/US/Europe divergence are in [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md).
- **Equipment confidence is a real thing, and old gear destroys it.** Knowing your arm pads will handle a high shot, and that your pads will not be overpowered when you close the five-hole, is what lets you stay in the shot instead of turning away from it. Gear that hurts you teaches you to flinch, and flinching is a technique problem you cannot coach out — so if you are an adult returning to the position in equipment you bought as a teenager, replacing it is probably the single largest available improvement to your game. Fit and comfort beat brand and looks, and a properly sharpened, correctly profiled skate matters more than either: dull goalie edges destroy lateral movement first.

### If you take one off the mask

```facts
Rule: 🇬🇧 The IIHF is the book that writes you a stoppage for this — hit on the face mask by a hard shot and believed dazed, the referee "may stop play for safety reasons if there is no immediate scoring chance for the attacking team" (IIHF Rule 9.6, 2025/26 v1.1 and 2026/27 alike)
Rule: Read both of IIHF Rule 9.6's limits — it is "may" and not shall, and an immediate scoring chance for the attacking team defeats it outright, so the whistle you need may never come
Rule: Hockey Canada writes the stronger version for a narrower group — a goaltender struck in the head by the puck means the referee "shall stop play as soon as it is safe to do so", but only "In categories of U13 and below" (Rule 6.18(a))
Rule: Hockey Canada 6.18(a) also tells the officials what the stoppage is for — "always allow team staff to evaluate the goaltender during a stoppage under this rule … to allow for concussion protocols be applied"
Rule: The NHL, CARHA and USA Hockey's stand-up rules write no mask-specific counterpart — none covers a mask that stays on and takes the shot, and USA Hockey's only version of it is a Sled Hockey rule
Rule: Every book still stops play for an injured goalkeeper — NHL and IIHF 8.1, USA Hockey 206(a), Hockey Canada 2.4(b), CARHA 20(d) — but each needs a referee to judge you hurt, which is what a cage hides
Rule: NHL 8.2's "must be ready to resume play immediately" is a rule against delaying the game, not a verdict on your head — and the same rule lets you stay in where the Trainer comes on and there is no undue delay
Action: Say it at the next stoppage — to the nearest official, to your bench, to your captain. You are the only player on the ice whose face nobody can see
Never: Play on to find out how it feels. A concussion needs no knock-out and no body check, and judgement is one of the things it impairs
Key: The symptoms, the red flags that mean an ambulance and what happens next are in Conditioning and Recovery; when you play again is a qualified medical professional's decision, not yours
```

A puck off your mask is the one impact in this position nobody else can assess for you, because your face is behind a cage and the rest of you looks fine. **Only two of the four books write a stoppage keyed to the hit itself — one discretionary, the other capped by age — and CARHA, the adult rec book, writes no mask-specific stoppage either.** ⚠️ **What every one of them does write is a stoppage for an injured goalkeeper**, so "no rule covers me" is the wrong thing to carry onto the ice. The catch is in the trigger: those rules ask an official to judge that you are hurt, and yours is the one face on the ice they cannot see. So the whistle is not the thing to rely on.

- **🇬🇧 The IIHF book has a clause for exactly this, and it is the book you play under in Britain.** IIHF Rule 9.6 — the same rule as the lost-mask and mask-removal limbs above — carries one more sentence, in identical words in the 2025/26 (v1.1) and 2026/27 editions: *"When a goalkeeper is hit on the face mask by a hard shot with the puck and the Referee believes the goalkeeper is, or may be, dazed by that hard shot, the Referee may stop play for safety reasons if there is no immediate scoring chance for the attacking team."* Nothing in the IHUK In-House Rules searched for this document touches Rule 9.6 — their Rule 9 amendments run 9.1, 9.3, 9.7, 9.8 and 9.12 — so it reaches you unaltered. ⚠️ **Read both of its limits, because together they mean the whistle may not come.** It is *may* and not *shall*, and it turns on a referee's belief about a face they cannot see; and an immediate scoring chance for the other team defeats it outright — which is precisely the moment you are least able to make the save and most likely to take a second one.
- **Hockey Canada writes the stronger version, for a narrower group.** **Rule 6.18(a)**: *"In categories of U13 and below should a Goaltender be struck in the head by the puck the Referee shall stop play as soon as it is safe to do so"*, unless the puck deflects directly in, in which case the goal stands. **That is a *shall* — and it stops at U13.** A fourteen-year-old under the same book gets nothing. The rule also says what the stoppage is for: *"Officials should always allow team staff to evaluate the goaltender during a stoppage under this rule (if necessary) to allow for concussion protocols be applied."*
- **Every book does stop play for an injured goalkeeper — the gap is in the trigger, not in whether a stoppage exists.** **NHL Rule 8.1** and **IIHF Rule 8.1** stop play once a player is *"injured so that [they] cannot continue play or go to [their] bench"*, and let an official stop it at once where *"it is obvious that a Player has sustained a serious injury"* — and both books' Rule 8.2 then presupposes exactly that: *"When play has been stopped by the Referee or Linesperson due to an injured goalkeeper…"*. **USA Hockey Rule 206(a)** writes the same stoppage and puts goalkeepers inside it by carving them out of a *different* consequence — *"A player other than a goalkeeper, whose injury appears serious enough to warrant the stoppage of play, may not participate further in the game until the completion of the ensuing face-off"* — which is only necessary if you are inside the stoppage to begin with. **Hockey Canada Rule 2.4(b)** does it the same way, its Note 2 excluding the goaltender only from having to leave the ice. **And CARHA, the adult rec book, writes the lowest threshold of the five:** Rule 20(d) stops play immediately, and its Note 1 reads *"Where there is suspicion that a player has sustained an injury, the Referee may stop the play immediately"* — no *serious*, no *obvious* — with the book's own definitions settling that it reaches you: *"Except where special rules apply, the goalkeeper is to be considered as one of the players."* ⚠️ **So the honest version is not that no rule covers you. It is that every one of these rules asks an official to see that you are hurt — and a goalie who stays upright behind a cage is the hardest player on the ice to see it in.** That is what IIHF 9.6 and Hockey Canada 6.18(a) are for, it is why they are the only two that trigger on the puck rather than on your condition, and it is why saying something yourself is still the part that is genuinely yours.
- **The NHL, CARHA and USA Hockey's stand-up rules write no *mask-specific* counterpart — and USA Hockey's one exception is in a section that does not govern you unless you play sled hockey.** NHL Rule 9.6, USA Hockey Rule 304(e) and Hockey Canada Rule 3.6(d) all cover a mask that comes **off**; none of them covers a mask that stays on and takes the shot. **CARHA — the adult rec book, which governs CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — is the same:** its Rule 24(f) stops play *"[w]hen a goalkeeper loses their helmet"*, and its face-protection clauses are about what you must wear rather than about what a shot does to it — 24(d), *"Goalkeepers must still wear a full face mask"*, and 23(d), which has you take the face protector off for identification if the referee asks. Nothing in the book reaches a mask that stays on and takes the puck. ⚠️ **The one place USA Hockey writes the IIHF's clause is its Sled Hockey rules**, under *Goalkeeper's Equipment — Helmet*: *"Should a hard shot hit the goalkeeper's facemask while play is in progress, the play may be stopped at the discretion of the Referee only if no immediate scoring chance is presented to the opposing team."* That is the same discretionary test as IIHF Rule 9.6, and it is written for sled hockey — so if you are a stand-up goalie under USA Hockey rules, it is not yours. *(The same book writes the broken-stick play-on allowance in the same place and nowhere else; see* If your stick breaks *below.)* ⚠️ **And do not read NHL Rule 8.2 as a verdict on your head.** Its *"must be ready to resume play immediately or be replaced by a substitute goalkeeper and no additional time shall be allowed"* is a rule about not delaying the game — **and quoting it that far and stopping loses the counterweights in the same rule.** It substitutes you *"only if he has to proceed to the players' bench to receive medical attention"*; *"[i]f the Trainer has come onto the ice to attend to the goalkeeper and there is no undue delay, the goalkeeper may remain in the game"*; and where a goalkeeper is *"removed from the game for mandatory medical evaluation/injury, the replacement goalkeeper may be afforded a brief warm-up."* IIHF Rule 8.2 reads the same way and adds that a team doctor may come onto the ice *"without waiting for the Referee's consent"*. **Hockey Canada runs furthest the other way:** under **Rule 2.4(c)**, where no alternate goaltender is dressed, an injured goaltender *"will be allowed 10 minutes to recuperate"*, and fifteen if a replacement is needed. ⚠️ **None of that is a clearance to carry on.** It is the books declining to rush you, which is a different thing from telling you that you are fine — and it is still an official's judgement, not a medical one.

**So the part that is genuinely yours is small, and it is not optional: say something.** At the next stoppage, tell the nearest official, your bench and your captain. The position rewards toughness in a way that makes this hard, and it is worth being blunt about the arithmetic: a goalie who comes off and turns out to be fine has cost their team a goalie change, and a goalie who stays on and is not fine has taken a second impact on an injured brain. ⚠️ **A concussion does not require being knocked out and does not require a body check.** [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md#concussion) has the symptoms, the red flags that mean an ambulance, and why nobody returns the same day. **What it will not give you — and neither will this document — is a timeline for coming back. That is a decision for a qualified medical professional who has assessed you.**

### Your stick — paddle length

```facts
Rule: Paddle length is capped at 26 inches (NHL Rule 10.2), and only players 6'6" or taller may apply for the exemption
Rule: IIHF Rule 10.2 allows 71 cm, about 28 inches, with no exemption process
Key: Too long a paddle makes you stand taller, drifts your stick hand from your body and opens the six-hole
```

- **Paddle length is capped at 26 inches** (Rule 10.2 — the widened portion of the stick may not extend more than 26 inches from the heel, nor exceed three and a half inches in width). **There is an exemption process, and only players 6'6" or taller may use it:** a longer paddle must be requested in writing and approved by Hockey Operations before the stick is used. *(Oddly, the 2025-26 rulebook sets the maximum length of an exempted paddle at "twenty-six inches (26")" — the same as the ordinary limit, which makes the exemption as written do nothing. The parallel provision for skaters' sticks in Rule 10.1 does grant real extra length, 65 inches against 63. Read the goalkeeper's figure as probably stale; it is not something to plan equipment around.)* For everybody else 26 inches is the ceiling under the NHL book. **🇬🇧 Under IIHF Rule 10.2 the limit is 71 cm — about 28 inches — with no exemption process at all**, and a 9.0 cm width cap that works out at 3.54 inches against the NHL's three and a half — the same limit for practical purposes. So the stick that is illegal in the NHL is legal in Britain, which is a licence rather than an instruction: the extra two inches are exactly the two inches that make you stand taller, drift your stick hand from your body and open the six-hole. Get this right before you rework your technique.

### If your stick breaks

```facts
Rule: In stand-up hockey, playing on with a broken stick is a goalkeeper's permission under NHL and IIHF Rule 10.4 alone — you are not required to drop it there, and neither USA Hockey's stand-up rules nor Hockey Canada writes you that allowance
Rule: Hockey Canada bars it in terms — "A goaltender may not continue to play with a broken stick of any kind" (Rule 3.2(c)), penalised as a minor for illegal equipment under 3.2(d) and 10.6(a)
Rule: USA Hockey's stand-up rules write a goalkeeper no play-on allowance — 605(a) names you once, only to grant "reasonable time to be aware that the stick is broken", so on the rule as written you take the minor a skater would; its sled hockey rules do grant it, and they do not govern stand-up play
Rule: Never go to the bench for a replacement stick — a teammate hands you one on the ice (NHL and IIHF Rule 10.4; USA Hockey 605(c), a minor for delay of game on a goalie who goes during a stoppage)
Rule: A whistle does not release you: at a stoppage, going to the bench for a stick, equipment, repairs, an injury or instructions "without first obtaining permission to do so from the Referee" means immediate replacement or a bench minor (NHL Rule 27.6; IIHF Rule 27.5)
Rule: A skater's stick is legal for you until a goalkeeper's stick reaches you, under all four books — NHL and IIHF 10.4, USA Hockey Casebook Rule 605 Situation 5 within goalkeeper stick measurements, Hockey Canada 3.2(c) until the next stoppage
Rule: Under Hockey Canada leave a slid stick where it lies — Interpretation 1 to Rule 3.2(c) assesses the goaltender a minor under 10.6(a) for picking it up, and none at all if they do not
Rule: Tell your skaters not to slide it either — Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to 3.2(c) puts a minor on them under 10.5(a) and reaches a penalty shot where the stick is slid at the puck or puck carrier in your defending zone
Never: Assume the NHL and IIHF play-on allowance travels to USA Hockey play. If you are a goalie under its rules, ask your league
```

- **If your stick breaks, the rule that applies to you is not the same in every book.** Under **NHL Rule 10.4** a goalkeeper *"may continue to play with a broken stick until a stoppage of play or until he has one legally provided to him by a teammate"*, and **IIHF Rule 10.4** says the same in its own words — *"until they have one legally handed to them by a teammate"*. You are not required to drop it, which is the opposite of the skater rule. ⚠️ **Neither of the other two books gives you that in stand-up play.** USA Hockey's stand-up rules write no equivalent allowance. Its Rule 605(a) opens *"Any player whose stick is broken must drop their stick prior to participating in the play"* and closes *"A minor penalty for an equipment violation shall be assessed for participating in play with a broken stick"*, and neither sentence carves you out. **The rule names you exactly once, in the sentence between them** — *"A player or goalkeeper shall be allowed reasonable time to be aware that the stick is broken"* — **and what that grants you is time to notice, not permission to play on.** 605(c) covers only where the replacement comes from. So on the rule as written a USA Hockey goalie who plays on with a broken stick takes the minor a skater would. **The one place USA Hockey does grant it is its Sled Hockey rules**, which say in terms that *"A goalkeeper is allowed to play with a broken stick until the next stoppage of play or until the goalkeeper has been legally provided with a new stick. The goalkeeper can go to the bench and change their stick when the play is in progress."* That reverses 605(c) as well as 605(a) — and it governs sled hockey, not the stand-up game, so do not carry it across. **Hockey Canada bars it outright**, at Rule 3.2(c): *"A goaltender may not continue to play with a broken stick of any kind"*, penalised under 3.2(d) as a minor for illegal equipment — **but read the whole of 3.2(c), because its first sentence is a permission**, and it is the one you will actually use: *"A goaltender who breaks or loses their stick may use a player's stick that has been handed to them until the next stoppage of play. In this case the players' stick will not be considered illegal equipment."* **What you do instead is in the books, so do not improvise it:** you may not go to the bench for one (NHL and IIHF Rule 10.4, and USA Hockey 605(c)), a teammate hands you a replacement on the ice, and **a skater's stick is legal in the meantime** — NHL Rule 10.4 permits it *"until such time as he is legally provided with a replacement goalkeeper's stick"*, the USA Hockey Casebook allows it at Rule 605, Situation 5 on one condition, *"[a]s long as the stick does not exceed the maximum measurements set forth for goalkeeper sticks"*, and Hockey Canada 3.2(c) allows *"a player's stick that has been handed to them until the next stoppage of play"*, at which point a proper goalkeeper's stick must reach you. ⚠️ **Under Hockey Canada, take it from a hand and not off the ice:** Interpretation 1 to 3.2(c) says that if a teammate slides it and *"the goaltender picks up the stick, they will be assessed a Minor penalty under Rule 10.6 (a) – Illegal Equipment"*, and none at all *"if they do not pick up the stick."* **Tell your skaters what sliding it costs them, because it is not only your problem:** the same interpretation puts a minor on the teammate under 10.5(a) and adds that *"A Penalty Shot may be awarded under this rule, if the player throws, shoots, kicks, or slides the stick at the puck or puck carrier in their defending zone."* Until it reaches you, you have a blocker, a glove and your body, and that is the trade the rule makes. Do not assume the NHL allowance travels to USA Hockey play; if you are a goalie under USA Hockey rules, ask your league. Full treatment in [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md#broken-sticks).
- **⚠️ A whistle does not release you, and this is the limb most goalies never hear.** Rule 10.4 is not the only rule about your bench trip. A second one sits in the same rule that keeps you in your net during a fight — **NHL Rule 27.6** and **IIHF Rule 27.5**, whose operative words are the same in both books. Quoting the IIHF's, which is the one Britain plays: *"during stoppages of play in the game"* a goalkeeper *"must not proceed to their Players' bench for the purpose of receiving a replacement stick or equipment or repairs thereto, or due to an injury, or to receive instructions, without first obtaining permission to do so from the Referee."* Otherwise they *"must be replaced by the substitute goalkeeper immediately (without any delay) or be assessed a bench minor penalty"* — the NHL writing the same sentence with *"he"* and *"his"* and adding *"for delay of game"* at the end. **Note who pays.** A bench minor is served by a teammate, so those two minutes come off your team rather than off you, and the alternative is being pulled for your backup on the spot. **Note also that this is a different penalty from 10.4's.** Rule 10.4 in both books bars the bench trip flatly — *"may not go to the Players' bench for a replacement but must receive their stick from a teammate"* — and puts *"a minor penalty … on the goalkeeper"*, while 27.6 / 27.5 open a permission route at a stoppage and put a **bench** minor on your team if you take it without asking. **The books do not say how the two fit together at a whistle, and this document does not resolve it either.** What follows from that is one instruction rather than a ranking. A broken stick at a whistle is the likeliest moment in a game that you will meet either of them: **catch the referee's eye and ask before you move**, and if the answer is no, take a teammate's stick on the ice and wait.

### A stick loose on your ice, and the goal that goes in off it

```facts
Rule: A goal is disallowed if an attacker deliberately propels a lost or broken stick, glove or other object at the puck to direct it into the net — new in the IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27 at Situation 10.26, with no counterpart in the 2025/26 book Britain plays
Rule: Intent is the key at Situation 10.26: the IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27 lets a goal stand where a lost or broken stick, glove or object accidentally deflects the puck in, and it ranks itself below the rulebook — ask your referee rather than assume it settles a goal in your league
Key: Play the puck, not the argument — the call is made after the fact, so if you saw a deliberate kick say so while the picture is fresh
```

- **There is a published answer to the other half of the broken-stick question — a stick loose on your ice that an opponent kicks at the puck.** The **IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27** puts it at **Situation 10.26**: a Team A goalkeeper or player loses their stick, and a Team B player *"kicks the stick at the puck, hitting the puck and causing it to enter the goal."* The answer: *"If, in the judgement of the Referee, an attacking player propels a lost or broken stick, glove or any other object at the puck, with intent to deliberately direct the puck into the net, the goal shall be disallowed."* The note beside it is the operative half — *"The key element to this situation is intent. If the lost or broken stick, glove or any other object accidentally contacts the puck knocking it into the net, the goal may be allowed."* **So it turns on what the referee judges the opponent meant, and it is decided after the fact, not during it.** Nothing about the save changes: play the puck, not the argument. What changes is the aftermath — if you saw a deliberate kick, say so while the picture is fresh, and do not assume a puck that went in off a loose stick has necessarily counted. ⚠️ **Know what this answer is and what it is not.** It is in the **2026/27** Handbook and has no counterpart in the 2025/26 one, so under the older edition there is no published answer to point at. The Handbook ranks itself below the rulebook in terms — its Foreword reads *"in case of any contradictions, the valid IIHF Rulebook is always the basis"* — and this particular answer cites no rule number, where most of its neighbours do. And it is a **later** edition than the rulebook the British In-House Rules adopt: the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 name the IIHF Rule Book *"Version 1.1, published July 2025"*, which is the 2025/26 book. So read Situation 10.26 as the IIHF's current interpretation, and ask your referee rather than assuming it settles a goal in your league.

---

## The Mental Side

### The goal that goes in

```facts
Mindset: Every goalie lets in goals and some will be bad — the skill is what you do in the ninety seconds afterwards
Action: Have a reset ritual and perform it identically every time, so it signals that the previous shift is finished
Never: Do the analysis during the game — "what could I have done?" takes attention you need for the next play
Key: Short memory, long memory — forget the goal for the rest of the game, remember the read in the week after
Never: Point at a teammate, with your hands, your body language or a look
```

Every goalie lets in goals. Some of them will be bad. The puck will go through you, or off your defenceman's shin, or you will be on the wrong post. The building will react and there is nowhere to hide.

The skill is not avoiding this. It is what you do in the ninety seconds afterwards.

- **Have a reset ritual and use it every time.** Skate a small circle. Get a drink. Scrape your crease. Tap both posts. Whatever it is, make it identical every time, so that performing it becomes the signal to your body that the previous shift is finished.
- **Do the analysis later.** During the game, "what could I have done?" is a trap; it takes attention you need for the next play. There is video and there is tomorrow.
- **Short memory, long memory.** Forget the goal for the rest of the game. Remember the read that caused it in the week after.
- **Never point at a teammate.** Not with your hands, not with your body language, not with a look. It is the fastest way to lose a dressing room — and Mitch Korn treats blaming your defence as a symptom of a confidence problem rather than a description of what happened. His full list of those symptoms is under *Spotting your own confidence problems*, below.

### Spotting your own confidence problems

```facts
Key: Korn's defence mechanisms are symptoms of your head, not real problems
Read: Blaming your equipment — suddenly nothing is right, the stick, the skates, the arm pads
Read: Blaming your defence — "there was no way to stop that one"
Read: Positioning drift — hiding very deep, or running at shooters and losing the net. Both show up plainly in warm-ups
Read: Mood swings to either extreme — quiet and sorry for yourself, or conspicuously cocky
Read: Guessing and flopping — leaving your feet early and no longer tracking the puck off the stick
```

Mitch Korn's list of defence mechanisms is uncomfortably accurate. If you catch yourself doing these, treat them as symptoms of your head rather than as real problems:

- **Blaming your equipment.** Suddenly nothing is right — the stick, the skates, the arm pads.
- **Blaming your defence.** "There was no way to stop that one."
- **Positioning drift.** Either hiding very deep in the net, where the posts feel safe, or running at shooters to cut the angle down to nothing and losing the net entirely. Both show up plainly in warm-ups.
- **Mood swings to either extreme** — going quiet and sorry for yourself, or going conspicuously cocky.
- **Guessing and flopping.** Leaving your feet early, no longer tracking the puck off the stick, and ending up swimming on the ice.

### The honest version of the mental demand

```facts
Mindset: "The most psychologically demanding position in team sport" is widely repeated with no study behind it. Treat it as opinion
Key: The supported version: at the top level the separation is mental — reading, anticipation, tracking, save selection and toughness
Risk: Confidence is startlingly fragile — several strong games, one bad period, and it can be gone for a week
Key: Nobody else on your team plays a position where every mistake is final and public
Mindset: Goalies need to enjoy making saves — if the save is the thing you love, the goals are a price
```

Goaltending is routinely described as the most psychologically demanding position in team sport. **That is a widely repeated claim with no study behind it, so treat it as opinion.** The narrower version is well supported by coaches and still substantial: at the top level, where everyone has the physical skills, the separation is made in **mental** ones — reading, anticipation, tracking, save selection and toughness; confidence is startlingly fragile, and a goalie can play several strong games, have one bad period and lose it for a week; and nobody else on your team plays a position where every mistake is final and public.

The counterweight is the part that actually keeps people in the position: **goalies need to enjoy making saves.** If the save is the thing you love, the goals are a price. If the goals are the thing you think about, no amount of technique will hold up. Full treatment in [Mental Game](../off-the-ice/mental_game.md).

---

## Common Mistakes

- **Dropping into the butterfly before the shot.** You have given away the top of the net and removed your own save selection. Go down when going down is the answer.
- **Hands at your hips.** Get them out in front where pucks can hit them.
- **Half-seeing a screen from both sides.** Around or over is a genuine coaching split — this document's default is around (tall means immobile), and the BVHS manual teaches over to maintain size. Ask your own coach. What nobody defends is splitting the difference: pick a side and find the puck cleanly there.
- **Being deep and off-angle.** Under the mainstream teaching — angle first, then depth — deep and off-angle is a goal. Treat that as a coaching philosophy rather than a law: there is a documented exception when you step off the post to a short-side shot, where depth is what closes the gap you just opened. Ask your coach which they teach.
- **Charging at shooters.** Cutting down the angle is not the same as running out of your net. You lose the net, the pass and your feet at once.
- **Shuffling too big.** Large shuffles open the five-hole mid-step. Smaller is better every time.
- **Rebounds into the slot.** Angle your blocker and pads at the corners. Know where their forwards are *before* the shot.
- **Losing sight of the puck at contact.** Explosive, flailing saves see nothing and control nothing. Watch it into the equipment.
- **Being a spectator on a pass.** Move when the pass is released, not when it arrives.
- **Dropping to a post out of habit, with no read.** Late on and early off, with post seals kept for pucks genuinely below the goal line, is **one school — the conservative North American default this document follows.** The Swedish tradition where RVH originated teaches an earlier, more systematic seal with a defined trigger line, and both are defensible. Ask what your trigger is. The actual mistake is going down there because it is what you always do.
- **Going from the dressing room straight into live shots.** The butterfly drops, the slides and the post seals are all force produced at the end of your hip range, and the BVHS goalie manual is blunt about what that costs cold: *"Jumping straight into live shots without a sweat drastically increases the risk of pulled groin or hip flexor muscles."* ⚠️ **That is a coaching manual's judgement rather than a measured rate, and it attaches no figure to it — none is invented here.** Get warm and moving through range first; [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md#warm-up) owns the warm-up, and the evidence it sets out favours moving over holding a stretch. **Groin or hip pain that persists or keeps coming back is a physiotherapist's or sports physician's problem, not a document's.**
- **Playing the puck in the corner under NHL or IIHF rules.** Two minutes, and it is entirely avoidable.
- **In England and Wales, hearing "the restricted area isn't enforced here" as "I can do what I like behind the net".** England Ice Hockey's Rule 22.3 suspends the **Restricted Area** and nothing else. **IIHF Rule 63.2(I)** is a separate minor, untouched by anything British held here, and it still catches a goalkeeper who *freezes* the puck to cause a stoppage anywhere *"outside of their goal crease area"* — your **crease**, not the trapezoid. Playing it out there may be free; sitting on it is not. **And do not over-correct:** coming out to cut down the angle, making the save and *then* covering is legal in terms (63.2(VI) Note).
- **Under USA Hockey, declining a cover you were entitled to make.** Rule 614(c) bars the corner, the ice behind your own goal line, and a freeze you had the time and the space to play with your stick — **not** a smother made under pressure in front of your net, which its *Casebook* allows whether or not any part of you is touching the crease (Rule 614(c), Situations 8, 10, 11 and 12). A puck left loose in traffic because you thought you could not touch it is a scramble you did not need to have.
- **Under Hockey Canada, reading clause v on its own and letting go of a puck you had already covered.** Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a), clause v, is blunt: leave your crease and freeze the puck and it is a Minor penalty, *"No warning will be issued."* ⚠️ **Clause ii of the same Interpretation expressly permits the opposite** — coming out *"to cut down the angle and catch the shot or, after stopping the shot, cover the puck"* — and **Hockey Canada nowhere says which of the two governs a cover made after a save outside the crease**, so reading ii as the exception is a reading rather than a Hockey Canada ruling. It is how the other books print the same pair: the Note to **NHL Rule 63.2(vi)** and **IIHF 63.2(VI)** say a come-out, cut-the-angle save followed by a cover *"shall be legal"*, and USA Hockey's *Casebook* answers that case **no penalty** at Rule 614(c) Situation 8 — with the proviso that a minor **is** assessed if *"the goalkeeper had sufficient time to drop the puck and play it with no attacking players in their immediate vicinity."* What clause iv penalises with no warning is a different act: the goaltender who races an attacker to a loose puck and, instead of playing it, *"simply jumps on the puck, causing a stoppage of play"* — no save involved. Ask your league which way its referees take clause v — but do not open your glove in a net-front scramble on the strength of clause v alone.
- **Banking snow up in front of your net, or dropping a covered puck onto the netting to buy a whistle.** Both are minors written for goalkeepers specifically: the netting act at **NHL and IIHF Rule 67.3(iv)** and **85.6**, **USA Hockey Rule 610(f)** and **Hockey Canada Rule 10.1(vi)** — though the *pads* half is written by the first three books only, Hockey Canada's clause naming the netting alone; the snow at **NHL and IIHF Rule 67.3(v)** and the same **USA Hockey Rule 610(f)**. ⚠️ **Under Hockey Canada the snow is not delay of game at all but Interference (Rule 8.3(a)), and Rule 4.11(a)(iv) turns it into a penalty shot** where the pile actually prevents a goal with you legally on the ice. Scrape rather than heap. **And a puck that lands on your netting by itself is not the same act** — the NHL and the IIHF let you freeze it for a defending-zone faceoff and no penalty (Rule 85.2), but USA Hockey's *Casebook* gives you a minor for it (Rule 610, Situation 8) and Hockey Canada reaches it through Rule 6.12(b). If it is going to fall off on its own, let it.
- **Pulling your own mask off to buy a whistle.** It is a delay-of-game minor in all four books — NHL and IIHF Rule 9.6, USA Hockey Rule 304(e), Hockey Canada Rule 3.6(d). ⚠️ **Do it against a breakaway, a penalty shot or a shootout attempt and the NHL and the IIHF stop pricing it as a penalty and award the goal**; USA Hockey Rule 610(e) and Hockey Canada Rule 4.11(a)(iii) award a penalty shot, and both of those add a last-two-minutes-and-overtime limb with no breakaway needed. **If the mask genuinely comes off, all four books stop the play for you anyway.**
- **Drifting toward a dumped puck you wanted whistled.** Under NHL and IIHF rules, moving in its direction waves off the icing and hands them a live puck in your end. Decide first, then stand still or go.
- **Stepping over the centre red line to reach a puck still on your own side.** A minor under **USA Hockey Rule 407(c)** and **Hockey Canada Rule 4.13(c)**, which judge it on the position of your skates — and how much North American rec and youth hockey runs on one of those two books is a question about leagues, and this document has no count for it. Under the NHL and the IIHF the same move is legal, because their rules judge it on the position of the puck (NHL Rule 27.7; IIHF Rule 27.6). Keep your skates and the puck both on your own side and you are legal under all four.
- **Leaving your net when a fight starts.** A minor under **NHL Rule 27.6** and **IIHF Rule 27.5**, and under **Hockey Canada Rule 7.10(a)** unless the referee has given you permission to move. Stay in the crease; if the altercation is in it, wait to be directed out — the NHL and IIHF carve-out is written to the referee, and **Hockey Canada Rule 7.10(d)** likewise has you stay in your crease *"or proceed to a neutral zone designated by the Referee."* Going in to help costs you a game misconduct on top: **USA Hockey Rule 615(d)** ejects *"any player or goalkeeper who is the first to intervene"*, with no crease condition on it, and **Hockey Canada Rule 7.10(e)(ii)** ejects a player who *"joins in a fight or acts as a peacemaker"* — and its Glossary makes that "player" you, so going in to break it up is caught as squarely as going in to fight.
- **Taking a loaned or handed-down mask without looking at the sticker.** Goalkeeper face protection is required under every book — **IIHF Rule 11.8**, **USA Hockey Rule 304(d)** (HECC-certified below Adults) and **Hockey Canada Rule 3.6(b)** (a CSA helmet with a CSA facial protector attached) — and certification expires: USA Hockey **Rule 304(c)** says *"a helmet that has an expiration date that has expired is no longer considered certified."* ⚠️ **But the sticker is a certification clock, not a condition report** — it carries an expiry date, and tells you nothing about that mask's impact history or the state of its liner, which is what actually protects your head. Club gear is how most new goalies get on the ice at all and borrowing it is right: **borrow the pads, the blocker, the trapper and the chest protector, and buy the mask.** [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md) owns used and second-hand head protection and sets out why.
- **🇬🇧 Going without a neck laceration protector because a North American source said adults are exempt.** That exemption is USA Hockey's (Rule 304(g)), and Hockey Canada's rule is scoped to minor and female hockey (Rule 3.6(c)). **IIHF Rule 9.12 makes one mandatory *"For all Players in all categories of IIHF Competition"*, adults included** — and every British level plays the IIHF book, with the In-House Rules deleting the warning and going straight to a 10-minute misconduct. **A throat dangler on your mask is an addition, not a substitute**, under every book that addresses it.
- **Playing on after a hard shot off your mask because no whistle came.** ⚠️ **Under the IIHF book — yours in Britain — that whistle is discretionary, and an immediate scoring chance defeats it.** Rule 9.6 says the referee *"may stop play for safety reasons if there is no immediate scoring chance for the attacking team."* **Hockey Canada makes it a *shall* only at U13 and below** (Rule 6.18(a)), and the NHL, CARHA and USA Hockey's stand-up rules write no *mask-specific* counterpart at all — USA Hockey's only version of it is in its Sled Hockey rules, which do not govern stand-up play. ⚠️ **Every book still stops play for an injured goalkeeper** — NHL and IIHF Rule 8.1, USA Hockey Rule 206(a), Hockey Canada Rule 2.4(b), CARHA Rule 20(d) — **but each of those needs a referee to judge that you are hurt, which is precisely what a cage hides.** So the stoppage may never arrive, and the part that is yours is saying something at the next one. ⚠️ **A concussion needs no knock-out and no body check to have happened**, and judgement is one of the things it impairs — so this is not a call to make on your own read of how you feel. Symptoms and red flags are in [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md#concussion); when you play again is a qualified medical professional's decision.
- **Shooting at your goalie in warm-up or in a drill — the mistake in this list that belongs to their teammates.** Warm-up is inside the rules, not outside them: all four books confine each team to its own half — **NHL and IIHF Rule 86.6** (*"each team shall confine its activity to its own end of the rink"*), **USA Hockey Rule 636(c)** and **Hockey Canada Rule 2.2(i)** (*"players will be restricted to their own half of the ice"*) — so a puck sent at the **other** team's goalie breaks a rule in every book, and USA Hockey and Hockey Canada let officials penalise it there and then (**Rule 502(b)**; **Rule 2.2(j)**, the penalty *"served at the start of the game"*). 🇬🇧 **The NHL and the IIHF report it instead of penalising it**, which is not a licence — **IIHF Rule 86.6** is the operative one, and *"any violation of this rule by the Players shall be reported to the Proper Authorities."* (**IIHF Rule 5.6**'s *"No penalties are to be assessed"* is often quoted here, but that sentence follows three provisions about protective equipment, unlisted players and warming up at a stoppage, and the same book penalises fighting before the drop of the puck at Rule 46.10 — so do not read it as blanket immunity for warm-up conduct.) ⚠️ **At your own goalie only two books stop play for the hit itself, which is the point:** IIHF Rule 9.6's is a *"may"* defeated by an immediate scoring chance and Hockey Canada Rule 6.18(a)'s *"shall"* stops at U13, while the NHL, USA Hockey's stand-up rules and CARHA write no mask-specific stoppage at all. **Every book does stop play for an injured goalkeeper** (NHL and IIHF 8.1, USA Hockey 206(a), Hockey Canada 2.4(b), CARHA 20(d)) — **but each of those waits on a referee judging them hurt, and a goalie in a cage is who that reads worst on.** Head-high in warm-up and pucks fired from ten feet in drills are both commoner than they should be. **Not shooting there is a coaching caution rather than a rule of hockey** — which is exactly why a teammate who does not shoot there is the only protection on offer.
- **Assuming you may play on with a broken stick.** NHL and IIHF Rule 10.4 let you; **USA Hockey's stand-up rules write no such allowance** — the one place it grants it is its sled hockey rules, which do not govern stand-up play — **and Hockey Canada bars it in terms** (Rule 3.2(c)). Under either of those books, drop it and call for one from a teammate on the ice — and under Hockey Canada the teammate's own stick is legal in your hands until the next stoppage, because the same Rule 3.2(c) says a player's stick handed to a goaltender *"will not be considered illegal equipment"*.
- **Lifting a clear over the glass.** Under **NHL and IIHF Rule 63.2(iii)** a puck you shoot or bat directly out of the rink from your defending zone is a minor, and that clause has **no deliberateness element**, and since almost every puck you play is inside your defending zone when you play it, it is live nearly every time you touch one. A teammate serves it (Rule 27.1). Under **USA Hockey Rule 610(c)** only the deliberate clear is penalised. Your save is not caught: on a stick save that deflects over the glass the goalkeeper *"did not 'shoot' the puck over the glass"* — that answer is the **IIHF's** Situation Handbook, at Situation 63.29, and the NHL's clause carries the same *"shoots or bats"* wording, though the NHL's own situation handbook has not been read for this document. ⚠️ **Do not stretch it into a deliberateness defence:** the same answer goes on to reason that *"Rule 63.2. talks about deliberate"*, which cuts against Rule 63.2(iii)'s own words.
- **Firing the puck out of the rink after the whistle, and pricing it at two minutes.** It is two minutes under the NHL and the IIHF, which write no tier above Rule 63.2 for putting the puck out of play — their one other puck clause, **39.4(ii)/(II)**, is a misconduct for knocking it *"out of the reach of an official who is retrieving it"*, a different offence. **Under the other two books it is a ladder.** USA Hockey prints the same act at Rule 610(c) as a minor **and** at Rule 601(c)(10) as a **misconduct**, without saying which governs — read as two clauses side by side, not as a ruling — and Rule 601(d)(7) makes it a **game misconduct** *"when done in protest of an official's decision"*, as does 601(d)(9) for a puck sent *"in the general direction of an on-ice official, or in the direction of a spectator"*. Hockey Canada could not put the escalation in its delay-of-game rule — 10.1(d), *"No Misconduct penalty may be assessed for Delay of Game"* — so it lists *"Shooting the puck out of the playing area, after the whistle"* under **Unsportsmanlike Conduct at Rule 11.1, preamble item (v)**, a minor at 11.1(a), a Misconduct at 11.1(d)(i) and a **Game Misconduct** at 11.1(e). ⚠️ **It is not a ladder you have to be walked up.** 11.1(d)(i) reads *"[p]ersists in Unsportsmanlike Conduct, after receiving a Minor penalty under Rule 11.1 (a)"*, but **Note 1 to 11.1(d) and Note 1 to 11.1(e) each remove that gate** — a referee *"is not required to assess a Minor penalty under this rule before assessing a Misconduct penalty, but may assess either penalty initially"*, and the same for the Game Misconduct. **You do not get one free minor first.** ⚠️ **And a game misconduct is where you stop being a spectator to your own penalty:** USA Hockey 407(a) has a teammate serve your minor or misconduct, but 407(b) replaces you in the net.
- **Assuming the stick fouls are a skater's problem.** Slashing, cross-checking, high-sticking and roughing are all written for *"a player"*, and NHL and IIHF Rule 5.1 make that word include you. A chop at a stick in your crease is two minutes, and a teammate serves it.
- **Assuming two minutes is the worst that happens to a player who runs you.** Under **USA Hockey** it is not: Rule 607(c) writes **no bare minor** for body checking or charging a goalkeeper — it is *"a minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct"* — and 607(e) adds that a match penalty *"may also be assessed for charging."* ⚠️ **It covers the *privileged area*, not just your crease**, which 607(d) Note 2 runs from the end-zone faceoff spots back to the end boards. **The counterweight travels with it:** accidental or unavoidable contact is interference, a 625(a)(8) minor (607(d) Note 1), so describe the act rather than demanding a tier.
- **Waiting for a whistle in a rebound scramble.** Under the NHL and IIHF books there is no whistle coming: Rule 69.7 permits incidental contact where you and an attacker are both going for the same loose puck, inside or outside the crease, and the goal counts. Win the puck. **What the same rule does not permit is being driven into your own net** — an attacker who pushes you in together with the puck after your stop has the goal disallowed, unless one of your own players fouled them into you.
- **Handling the puck because you can.** Every touch is a chance for a turnover in front of your own empty net.
- **Silence.** You have the best view on the ice. A goalie who doesn't talk is throwing away the most valuable thing they own.
- **Calling "screen" once and giving up.** Say it every time, loudly, until it changes something.
- **Carrying the last goal into the next shift.**

## Check yourself

*Answer each question before you go on — a real attempt, not a guess. Then go
and check, because the checking is the part that does the work. Producing an
answer and getting it wrong is worth more than recognising a right one, but only
when the right answer follows: the section named with each question, and the Key
Takeaways after them, are where you get it. Where the answer lives in another
document, that is named too.*

1. One shooter, no pass available, shot from distance. How far out do you go,
   what are the three things that depth costs you — and if you cannot have all
   three of out, square and set, which one do you give up? *(§Depth is the
   fundamental decision, and §Playing the angle)*

2. There is a body in front of you and a point shot coming. **Two parts of this
   have no single right answer** — name both splits, say what each side is
   trading against the other, and say what both schools agree you must not do.
   *(§Fighting for a sightline, and §Playing bigger and deeper through traffic)*

3. You go to the post in RVH. Which pad goes where, what is the load-bearing
   detail most descriptions of it leave out, and what is the one thing about post
   play that nobody defends? *(§Post integration: VH and RVH)*

4. A shot is coming from the top of the circle and your instinct is to drop.
   Give three concrete reasons that instinct fails, and say which goalies it
   fails worst for. *(§The butterfly is a tool, not a religion)*

5. Their winger dumps the puck in from just inside their own blue line and it is
   coming round to you. You could drift two strides toward it, you could smother
   it, or you could stand still. Say what each of those three costs or buys you —
   and which rule book changes the answer. *(§Icing — you are the one who can
   wave it off, and §Freezing the puck — and the rules that govern it)*

6. That same rim is running round the boards outside the trapezoid and you think
   about dropping to stop it with your pads. What happens, where on the ice would
   the identical act have been completely legal, and what should you do instead?
   *(§Stopping pucks for your defence, and §The trapezoid — the rule that defines
   what you can do)*

7. You shout "shot" at your defenceman. What does that call actually mean, what
   does it not mean, and what do you do if your team already uses the word the
   other way round? *(§The core calls)*

8. The puck is on your blade behind your own net with a forechecker arriving, and
   you decide to get rid of it high and hard. Which two delay-of-game clauses are
   you inside, which of them does **not** require you to have meant it, and who
   serves the penalty if you get it wrong? And if the whistle had
   already gone and you did it in temper, under which two books is two minutes
   not the whole of the price? *(§Putting the puck out of play — the
   minor that is yours too, and the tier above it after a whistle)*

9. An attacker is standing in your blue paint obstructing your view and the puck
   goes in. What does the rule say — and what is different about that answer in
   EIH or SIHA competition, and what does that change about *when* you say
   something? *(§Goaltender Interference, From Your Side of It)*

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Positioning, not reflexes.** Good goaltending looks boring. Be out, be square, be set — Mitch Korn's three steps, in his order. **He numbers them but does not rank them**, so treating out-and-still-moving as worse than slightly-shallow-and-set is a coaching judgement rather than his — one manual gives up depth only against a back-door or deke threat, so ask your coach. **Depth is the fundamental decision**, and it always trades the shooter in front against the pass behind: deeper against passes, further out against a lone shooter. **Depth against a screen is genuinely disputed between coaching schools — ask yours.**
