2. **Angle before depth — a coaching philosophy, not a law.** Deep and off-angle is a goal; on-angle and slightly deep is a save. One documented school reverses that when you step off the post to a short-side shot. **Ask your coach which they teach.**
3. **Track the puck all the way into your body.** Goalie coaches put almost every soft goal down to losing sight of the puck — an observation, not a count.
4. **The butterfly is a save you select, not an identity, and not a religion.** Stay on your feet until going down is the answer. **Be wary of the style labels** — "profly" and "hybrid" are used inconsistently, and which one dominates is not settled.
5. **Rebound control in priority order: prevent, cover or clear, direct safely, recover.** Direct everything to the corners and know where their forwards are before the shot.
6. **Your voice is the most valuable thing you do that is not a save.** "Time," "man on," "leave it," "screen," "back door." Early, short, every time.
7. **Under NHL and IIHF rules you may play the puck behind the goal line only inside the trapezoid** — the one exception is skate contact with the crease, **and under the IIHF you must also have actually *played* the puck**: a puck that comes off the glass and hits you while you retreat is no penalty, and a puck you stop on the goal line is not behind it (Situation Handbook, Situations 27.9 and 27.6, in the same words in the 2025/26 v1.1 and 2026/27 editions). **Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada writes a trapezoid at all**, and how much rec, beer-league and youth hockey that adds up to is a question about leagues rather than about books, which this document has no count for — **but no trapezoid is not no restriction**: Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a), clause v, gives a minor with *"No warning will be issued"* to a goaltender who leaves the crease and freezes the puck. ⚠️ **Never act on that clause alone: clause ii of the same Interpretation expressly permits the opposite**, coming out *"to cut down the angle and catch the shot or, after stopping the shot, cover the puck"* — and clause iv, in between, penalises racing an attacker to a loose puck and simply jumping on it, with no warning. **Hockey Canada nowhere says which of ii and v governs a cover made after a save outside the crease**, so treating ii as the exception is a reading and not a ruling — it is how the other three books print the same pair, with the exception written in. Carry both halves or you will drop a puck you had legitimately smothered, in traffic. USA Hockey Rule 614(c) assesses the same two minutes for *freezing* the puck behind the goal line with your body entirely outside the crease, or anywhere outside the "goalkeeper's privileged" area, which excludes the corners. **Inside that area and in front of the goal line you have more latitude than the rule text suggests** — its Casebook decides those covers on the "act of playing goal", not on crease contact, and excuses one made prone and fully outside the crease (Rule 614(c), Situations 8 to 12). **In England and Wales the status is unsettled**: England Ice Hockey's *Rules & Regulations* 22.3 say the restricted area is *"not currently enforced"* (the IIHF numbers it Rule 27.7, not the NHL's 27.8). Play it as though it binds. ⚠️ **And 22.3 suspends that rule alone.** IIHF Rule 63.2(I) still gives you a minor for *freezing* the puck to cause a stoppage anywhere *"outside of their goal crease area"* — your crease, not the trapezoid — so a suspension frees where you may *play* it and never where you may *sit on* it, while 63.2(VI)'s Note keeps a come-out, save, then cover legal. **In front of the goal line no trapezoid reaches you, but the bar on participating in the play past the centre red line does, in all four books — and they measure it opposite ways:** the puck's position under NHL Rule 27.7 and IIHF Rule 27.6, your skates' position under USA Hockey Rule 407(c) and Hockey Canada Rule 4.13(c). Keep both on your own side. **And wherever you are, keep your clears *along* the glass rather than over it:** under **NHL and IIHF Rule 63.2(iii)** a puck you shoot or bat directly out of the rink from your own zone is two minutes, and that clause carries **no deliberateness element** — under **USA Hockey Rule 610(c)** only the deliberate clear is penalised. Your save is not caught: the **IIHF's** own Situation Handbook answers that case at Situation 63.29, and the NHL's own situation handbook has not been read for this document. ⚠️ **Two minutes is the whole price only under the NHL and the IIHF.** For a puck put out *after a whistle*, USA Hockey also writes Rule 601(c)(10), a **misconduct**, without saying which of it and 610(c) governs, and 601(d)(7) makes it a **game misconduct** *"when done in protest of an official's decision"*; Hockey Canada bars a misconduct for delay of game at 10.1(d) and puts the same act under Unsportsmanlike Conduct at **Rule 11.1, preamble item (v)**, reaching a Game Misconduct at 11.1(e) — which **Note 1 to each of 11.1(d) and 11.1(e) lets a referee assess without the rungs below it**, so there is no free minor first. A teammate serves your minor, and under USA Hockey 407(a) your misconduct too (Rule 27.1) — **but 407(b) takes your place in the net on a game misconduct.**
8. **A screen from *outside* the crease, without contact, is normally legal** — both qualifiers belong to the claim. Rule 69.1 helps you only where an attacker's positioning or contact actually stops you moving or defending — **and do not count on even that outside the paint**, because both books' worked situations answer *"Goal is allowed"* for an outside-the-crease screen that does impair you (NHL Reference Table 14 and IIHF 2025/26 Appendix IV Table 16, situations 5C and 5E). **And in EIH or SIHA competition nobody reviews it**, so the referee's live call stands. **Nothing in USA Hockey or Hockey Canada reaches a screen set from outside the paint at all** — USA Hockey writes no goaltender-vision provision, and Hockey Canada's reaches only a player standing in the crease. **Inside it the two part company**: USA Hockey 625(b) voids the goal on an attacker's position alone — unless the puck preceded them into the crease, you are out of the crease area, or a defender's interference is what put them there. **That second one comes back to you if they blocked your return:** its Casebook, Rule 625 Situation 9, disallows the goal and adds an interference minor where you were *"attempting to return to the goal crease"* and the attacker prevented you from making a play. **And the third one — your own defenceman putting them there — answers for that attacker's position only, not for what they then do to you:** 617(c)(4) disallows the goal both for an attacker *positioned in the crease* **and** for one *"interfering with the goalkeeper"*, with the push-in parenthetical written into the first limb alone, while **625(a)(8)** is still a minor for *"physical contact, using their stick or body, in a manner that interferes with the movement of the goalkeeper"* — so ask for it rather than assuming your own team's foul cost you the call. Meanwhile Hockey Canada 8.5 lets a teammate's goal stand if the player in your crease stays completely passive — the carve-out fails the moment they **attempt to play the puck, interfere with the play, or obstruct your view or movements**. **And under Hockey Canada what happens to the player who runs you is not capped at two minutes:** Rule 8.5(b) reaches a major plus game misconduct — an ejection — **at the referee's discretion**, on the degree of violence of impact, and makes one **mandatory** for charging the goaltender and **mandatory** for any interference-with-goaltender foul that injures; 8.5(c) reaches a match penalty.
9. **On a 2-on-1 you want the shot and your defenceman takes the pass** — say it out loud, because it is a convention, not a law. **You will let in bad goals**: use a reset ritual every time, and do the analysis tomorrow.
10. **🇬🇧 Your mask and your neck laceration protector are both mandatory, and the neck one at every age, adults included — a dangler does not replace it.** Goalkeeper face protection is required by every book (**IIHF Rule 11.8**; **USA Hockey Rule 304(d)**; **Hockey Canada Rule 3.6(b)**), and an expired certification sticker is no certification (USA Hockey Rule 304(c)) — so look at it on anything you are loaned. ⚠️ **An in-date one is not a clearance, though: it is a certification clock, not a condition report**, silent on impact history and on the state of the liner — so **borrow the pads, the blocker, the trapper and the chest protector, and buy the mask**, which is [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)'s reading of the certification rules rather than a sentence any book writes. The neck is the signature catastrophic injury in the position. IIHF Rule 9.12 requires one *"For all Players in all categories of IIHF Competition"* and every British level plays that book; across the Home Countries — England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland — and in university hockey, the IHUK In-House Rules delete the warning and assess an immediate 10-minute misconduct, escalating to a game misconduct on a second offence. USA Hockey's adult exemption (Rule 304(g)) and Hockey Canada's minor-and-female scope (Rule 3.6(c)) are not yours. ⚠️ **The head half of the same problem has a rule most goalies never hear, and it is weaker than you would expect.** A hard shot off your face mask is a stoppage the referee *may* make under **IIHF Rule 9.6** — defeated by an immediate scoring chance — and *shall* make under **Hockey Canada Rule 6.18(a)** only at U13 and below; the NHL, CARHA and USA Hockey's stand-up rules write no mask-specific counterpart, USA Hockey's only version sitting in its Sled Hockey rules. **Every book does stop play for an injured goalkeeper** (NHL and IIHF 8.1, USA Hockey 206(a), Hockey Canada 2.4(b), CARHA 20(d)) — **but those wait on a referee judging you hurt, and your face is the one they cannot see. The whistle may not come, so say something.** [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md#concussion) has the symptoms and the red flags, and returning to play is a medical decision.
11. **Charging you is priced above a bare minor under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada, and not under the NHL or the IIHF — and no one book is simply the strongest.** Rule 607(c) writes **no bare minor** for body checking or charging a goalkeeper — it is *"a minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct"* — 607(e) adds a possible match penalty, and it protects you out to the **privileged area**, which 607(d) Note 2 runs from the end-zone faceoff spots back to the end boards, so it covers your net front, your goal line and the ice behind your net. **Accidental or unavoidable contact is not that rule** — 607(d) Note 1 sends it to interference, a 625(a)(8) minor — so describe the act to the referee rather than naming a tier. **The NHL and the IIHF do not share a ceiling, so do not read them as one book.** NHL 42.2 to 42.4 run minor, major, **match** at the referee's discretion, with 42.5 adding a game misconduct where a major is imposed for an injury *"to the face or head"*; the **IIHF stops a rung lower** — 42.2 to 42.4 run minor, major, and major plus game misconduct, its 42.5 is supplementary discipline rather than an on-ice penalty, and the words *"match penalty"* appear nowhere in its 2025/26 rulebook. Neither book has a privileged area — so under both a bare minor is available, and NHL 42.1 says so in terms. **Hockey Canada writes the highest floor of the four and the only mandatory one**: 8.5(b) *"A Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty **will be assessed** to any player who charges the goaltender"*, with 8.5(d) removing the misconduct rung and 8.5 protecting you *"anywhere on the ice"*. **Find out which book you are under.**
12. **The section written for your teammates has one item that is a safety rule rather than a coaching point: do not shoot at your goalie in warm-up or from ten feet in a drill.** At your own goalie that is a coaching caution rather than a rule; at the other team's it is a rule, because warm-up is inside the rules — all four books confine each team to its own half (**NHL and IIHF Rule 86.6**, *"each team shall confine its activity to its own end of the rink"*; **USA Hockey Rule 636(c)**; **Hockey Canada Rule 2.2(i)**), and what follows splits two-and-two. **USA Hockey Rule 502(b)** and **Hockey Canada Rule 2.2(j)** let officials penalise it on the spot. 🇬🇧 **The NHL and the IIHF report it instead** — under their own **Rule 86.6**, a violation is *"reported to the Proper Authorities"* (IIHF) or *"to the Commissioner"* (NHL). ⚠️ **At your own goalie only two books stop play for the hit itself:** **IIHF Rule 9.6**'s is a *"may"* cancelled by an immediate scoring chance and **Hockey Canada Rule 6.18(a)**'s *"shall"* reaches only *"categories of U13 and below"*, while the NHL, CARHA and USA Hockey's stand-up rules write no mask-specific stoppage. **Every book stops play for an injured goalkeeper** (NHL and IIHF 8.1, USA Hockey 206(a), Hockey Canada 2.4(b), CARHA 20(d)) — **but each needs a referee to judge them hurt first, which is exactly what a cage hides.** Two of the five skaters' items are the same shape — do not screen your own goalie, and do not back into the crease when you are shoved.
13. **The physical cost of the position lands on your hips and groin, and the part of it that is yours on game day is simply not arriving cold.** A goalie coaching manual puts jumping straight into live shots without a sweat at *"drastically increases the risk of pulled groin or hip flexor muscles"* — **its judgement, not a measured rate, and it attaches no figure to it.** What *is* measured is narrower and lives elsewhere: among **NHL goaltenders** in a 2025 *American Journal of Sports Medicine* study, proximal adductor injuries were the largest single injury category and cost more days missed than any other — professionals, on the ice far more than you, and [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md#groin-and-adductor-strain--the-classic-hockey-injury) owns the numbers, the population and the caveats. **Warm up by moving rather than by holding stretches.** And take groin or hip pain that persists or recurs to a physiotherapist or sports physician rather than to a document — that goes for how to treat it and for when you play again.

---

*Sources — retrieved 27 July 2026; UK and IIHF layer retrieved 29 July 2026:*

*Rules: [NHL Official Rules 2025-2026 (PDF)](https://media.d3.nhle.com/image/private/t_document/prd/slwjuaqwmuvj5bkplixo.pdf) — **Rule 5.1** (Eligible Players — *"any reference to 'player' shall refer to both skaters and goalkeepers"*, the definition that puts a goalkeeper inside every bare-"player" infraction), Rule 1.7 (Goal Crease), Rule 1.8 (Goalkeeper's Restricted Area markings), **Rule 42 Charging**, read 1 September 2026 — 42.1's *"A minor, major or a major and a game misconduct shall be imposed on a player who charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within his goal crease"*, ⚠️ **a longer sentence than the IIHF's at the same number**, with **42.2** (minor), **42.3** (major), **42.4** (match) and **42.5** (game misconduct on a major *"resulting in an injury to the face or head of an opponent"*); the book writes no privileged area, Rule 10.2 (Goalkeeper's Stick), **Rule 10.4** (Broken Stick – Goalkeeper: the permission to play on, the hand-to-hand replacement, the bar on going to the bench, and the skater's stick in the meantime), Rule 11 (Goalkeeper's Equipment), Rule 24 (Penalty Shot), **Rule 27.1** (Minor Penalty to Goalkeeper — *"A goalkeeper shall not be sent to the penalty bench for an offense which incurs a minor penalty"*, the teammate designated by the coach, and *"[a] penalized player may not serve a goalkeeper's penalty"*), **Rule 27.6** (Leaving Goal Crease — the minor during an altercation, its two express carve-outs, and the separate bar on going to the players' bench during a stoppage *"without first obtaining permission to do so from the Referee"*), Rule 27.7–27.8 (Goalkeeper's Penalties / Restricted Area — 27.7 on participating in the play beyond the centre red line, whose *"position of the puck is the determining factor"* sentence is the half USA Hockey and Hockey Canada reverse), Rule 63.2 (Delaying the Game, including **63.2(i)** — the freeze outside the crease, which that clause says in terms *"applies outside of his goal crease area"* — **63.2(vii)**, the in-crease clause with its *"unless he is actually being checked by an opponent"*, **63.2(ii)**, the deliberate puck-out-of-play minor *"from anywhere on the ice surface"*, and **63.2(iii)**, the separate minor for shooting or batting the puck *"directly (non-deflected) out of the playing surface from his defending zone"* — read for a deliberateness element, **of which that clause has none** — with its NOTE on the players' bench and the face-off; **63.2(v)**, *"On a player other than the goalkeeper"*, the express exclusion that shows the book excludes goalkeepers when it means to; and 63.2(viii) with its NOTE, the restricted-area penalty and the sole skate-contact exception), **Rule 63.9**, the Delaying the Game infractions list — *"(i) Deliberately shooting the puck out of play"*, among acts producing *"a penalty (minor, bench minor, penalty shot or awarded goal)"*, **with no misconduct in the list** — and **Rule 75 Unsportsmanlike Conduct**, read in full 1 September 2026 across 75.2, 75.3, 75.4 and 75.5 for a puck-out-of-the-playing-area limb, **of which the NHL book has none**; Rule 63.8 (freeze after a dump-in from centre), **Rule 64.1** (the goalkeeper embellishment clause, in the same words as the IIHF's), Rule 67.3–67.5 (Handling Puck), read in full 1 September 2026 — **67.3(iv)**, *"Drops the puck into his pads or onto the goal net"*, and **67.3(v)**, *"Deliberately piles up snow or obstacles at or near his net that, in the opinion of the Referee, would tend to prevent the scoring of a goal"*, both read for an escalation above the minor, **of which neither clause carries any**; **Rule 85.6**, the second and separate delay-of-game minor on *"a goalkeeper who deliberately drops the puck on the goal netting to cause a stoppage of play"*, which supplies the deliberateness element 67.3(iv) does not; and **Rule 85.2 Puck Unplayable**, whose three-second netting stoppage and whose sentence *"Should the goalkeeper use his stick or glove to freeze the puck on the back of the net … the face-off shall take place at one of the face-off spots in the defending zone"* answer that act with **no penalty at all** — the sentence straddles a page break in the extraction and was read flattened, Rule 69 (Interference on the Goalkeeper, including **69.2**'s minor-or-major range and **69.3**'s minor for refusing to give ground), **Reference Table 11** (Summary of Goalkeeper Penalties — its eighth row, *"Deliberately shoots or bats puck out of play — Rule 63"*, and its thirteenth row, *"Using blocking glove to punch an opponent in the head or face — Rule 51"*), **Rule 51.3** (the match penalty for a blocker punch *"in an attempt to or to deliberately injure an opponent"*), **Rule 81.1** (Icing — *"Should any player of a team, equal or superior in numerical strength (power-play) to the opposing team, shoot, bat or deflect the puck from his own half of the ice beyond the goal line of the opposing team, play shall be stopped"*) and **Rule 81.5**, read in full for a goalkeeper exemption — its three goalkeeper clauses reach the *opposing* goalkeeper's touch and a goalkeeper dislodging the puck from the back of the net, and **none exempts the goalkeeper who shot it**, **Reference Table 14** (Interference on the Goalkeeper Situations — situations 5C and 5E, the outside-the-crease screen, both answered *"Goal is allowed"*; and, under its heading **6, Crowding the Goalkeeper**, rows **6D** and **6F–6H** on a goalkeeper who establishes position in the crease — 6D answering that a minor is not assessed, **6G and 6H** putting the minor on the goalkeeper, and **6F** splitting it, *"Coincidental minors (goalkeeper/attacking player)"*; with **Rule 69.3**'s own third paragraph, which reaches 6D's facts and mandates the minor the table withholds. ⚠️ Cite the 6: Table 14 has two D rows, 5D being the screening case), Rule 81.3 (icing wave-offs, including the goalkeeper), Rule 84.4 (Shootout), **Rule 86.6 Pre-Game Warm-Up**, read 2 September 2026 — *"During the pre-game warm-up … each team shall confine its activity to its own end of the rink. Refer to Rule 46.9 - Fighting"*, with *"any violation of this rule by the players shall be reported to the Commissioner"*, **which is a report and not a penalty** · [USA Hockey Playing Rules 2025-29](https://www.usahockey.com/rulesandresources) — Rule 624(b), which does *not* wave off icing for goalie movement, and Rule 624(b)(1), which limits the shorthanded icing exemption to Youth 15-Only and above, Girls 16U and above, High School and Adult; **Rule 407(c)**, the minor where a goalkeeper *"deliberately participates in the play in any manner when they are beyond the center red line"*; **Rule 615(e)**, the minor on a player who *"fails to move immediately and directly to their players' bench (or goal crease for a goalkeeper) when instructed to do so by the Referee during an altercation"* — the book's *Summary of Penalties* lists it under **Goalkeeper Infractions** as *"Leaving crease area during altercation"*, though the literal phrase *"leaving the crease"* appears nowhere in the playing rules; **Rule 615(d)**, the game misconduct on *"any player or goalkeeper who is the first to intervene in an altercation then in progress"*, which names goalkeepers expressly, attaches no crease condition, and is *"in addition to any other penalty incurred in the same incident"*; and, for goaltender interference, **Rule 625(b)** (the flat bar on a goal with an attacking player in the crease, its neutral-zone face-off, and the push-in relief), **Rule 625(a)(8)** (the minor, which requires physical contact) and **Rule 617(c)(4)** (the two limbs on which a goal is disallowed); **Rule 607 Charging**, read in full 1 September 2026 — **607(c)**, *"A minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to a player who body checks or charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area"*; **607(d)**, *"A goalkeeper is NOT 'fair game' because they are outside the privileged area"*, with its **Note 1** routing *"any accidental or unavoidable contact"* to the Interference rule and *"[a]ny deliberate body contact or check"* to charging, and its **Note 2** defining the privileged area; and **607(e)**, *"A match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed for charging."* ⚠️ **Read in full for a bare-minor limb, of which Rule 607 has none at any limb** — 607(a) prices charging any opponent at *"[a] minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct penalty"* too; **Rule 404 Misconduct Penalties**, read 1 September 2026 — **404(a)**'s removal of a player *"for a period of 10 minutes, or the designated misconduct penalty time"*, and the **(Note)** immediately above it, *"(Except for Adults) Affiliates or governing bodies are authorized to reduce the length of misconduct penalties for games under their jurisdiction according to the following period lengths"*, at *"12 minutes or less – 6:00"*, *"More than 12 but less than 17 minutes – 8:00"* and *"17-20 minutes – 10:00"*, with **404(b)**, *"A 'GAME MISCONDUCT' penalty involves the suspension of a player or Team Official for the balance of the game with immediate substitution taking place on ice"* and the player *"shall also be suspended for the team's next game"*; **Rule 407(a) and (b)** (Goalkeeper Penalties), read 1 September 2026 — 407(a)'s *"A goalkeeper shall not be sent to the penalty bench for an offense that incurs a minor or misconduct penalty"*, and 407(b)'s *"Should a goalkeeper incur a game misconduct or a match penalty, their place will be taken by a substitute goalkeeper or a temporary goalkeeper (see Glossary) or an additional skater"*, with the time still served by a designated teammate; **Rule 601 Abuse of Officials and Other Misconduct**, read in full 1 September 2026 for a puck put out of the playing area — **601(a)(4)**, the unsportsmanlike minor for a player who *"[s]hoots the puck after the whistle, if in the opinion of the Official such shot was avoidable"*, with the escalation printed beneath 601(a), *"A misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who persists in such conduct and any further dispute by the same player shall result in a game misconduct penalty being assessed"*; **601(c)(10)**, the misconduct for a player who *"[s]hoots or bats the puck outside of the playing area during a stoppage of play (when not done in protest of an official's decision or in the direction of a spectator)"*; **601(d)(7)**, the game misconduct for the same act *"when done in protest of an official's decision"*; **601(d)(9)**, the game misconduct for a puck shot *"in the general direction of an on-ice official, or in the direction of a spectator during a stoppage of play"*; and the **Note to 601(d)**, *"A match penalty shall be assessed to any player who shoots the puck directly at an official or spectator in a manner that recklessly endangers the official or spectator"*. ⚠️ **Rule 601 and Rule 610(c) both reach a puck put out during a stoppage, at different tiers, and the book does not say which governs**; Rule 601's opening Note gives only *"A minor penalty or misconduct penalty should be assessed for actions that occur on the playing surface or in the penalty bench area"*, and the Casebook's Rule 601 situations were read in full and none concerns a puck put out of the playing area. and **Rule 605** (Broken Stick — 605(a), whose drop-the-stick sentence and whose equipment minor carve out no goalkeeper, and whose only mention of one is the sentence between them, allowing *"a player or goalkeeper"* alike *"reasonable time to be aware that the stick is broken"*; and 605(c), which covers only where a goalkeeper's replacement comes from, so the **stand-up** rules write no equivalent of the NHL's play-on allowance) — with the **Sled Hockey** section of the same book, which does write one, and reverses 605(c) too: *"A goalkeeper is allowed to play with a broken stick until the next stoppage of play or until the goalkeeper has been legally provided with a new stick. The goalkeeper can go to the bench and change their stick when the play is in progress."* It governs sled hockey only — as does the **only** counterpart in that book to IIHF Rule 9.6's mask-hit stoppage, in the same Sled Hockey section under *Goalkeeper's Equipment — Helmet*: *"Should a hard shot hit the goalkeeper's facemask while play is in progress, the play may be stopped at the discretion of the Referee only if no immediate scoring chance is presented to the opposing team."* ⚠️ **The stand-up playing rules were searched for a counterpart on nine phrasings — *dazed*, *hard shot*, *struck in the head*, *hit in the head by the puck*, *stop play for safety*, *concussion protocol*, *in the head*, *safety reasons* and every *may/shall stop play* occurrence, on punctuation-stripped flattened text — and contain none outside that section; the same searches return the quoted sentences in the IIHF and Hockey Canada books.** Also **Rule 502(b)**, *"Penalties may also be assessed during the warm-up period"*, and **Rule 636(c)**, under which each team warms up *"on their half of the rink"*. and, for neck laceration protection, **Rule 304(g)**, which requires a HECC-certified neck laceration protector *"that covers as much of the neck area as possible"* of all players including goalkeepers *"in all age classifications except Adults"* (BNQ-certified protectors satisfying it *"through the 2026-27 USA Hockey season"*, and Adults *"strongly recommend[ed]"* to wear one), with **Rule 303(b)**, the goalkeeper-specific limb — *"HECC-certified neck laceration protectors are required in all age classifications except for Adults"* — which recommends hanging throat protectors for Adult goaltenders alongside the protector rather than in place of it. Read in full for a screening or goaltender-vision provision: **the 2025-29 playing rules contain none** · [USA Hockey Official Rules and Casebook 2025-29 (PDF)](https://cdn3.sportngin.com/attachments/document/4f1a-3442850/2025-29_USAH_Playing_Rules___Casebook.pdf) — **Rule 610(c)**, the delay-of-game minor on *"any player or goalkeeper who delays the game by deliberately shooting or batting the puck outside the playing area"*, read in full for a defending-zone limb, **of which Rule 610 has none**; **Casebook Rule 610, Situation 5** (a rebound batted out of the air and out of the rink — *no penalty*, with the answer's *"Had the goalkeeper caught the puck first, thereby gaining control, and then deliberately batted the puck directly out of the playing area"*); Rule 610(b) and its Note; **Rule 610(f)**, the goalkeeper-specific delay-of-game minor — *"A minor penalty for delay of game shall be assessed to a goalkeeper who drops the puck into their pads or onto the goal net or deliberately piles up snow or obstacles near the goal that, in the opinion of the Referee, could prevent the scoring of a goal"* — **read for a tier above the minor and for a penalty-shot or awarded-goal limb, of which the clause has neither**; **Casebook Rule 610, Situation 8**, which extends it to a goalkeeper who covers a puck that reached the netting on its own (*"Stop play and assess a minor penalty"*), where **NHL and IIHF Rule 85.2 answer the same facts with a face-off and no penalty**; ⚠️ **the book's own summary tables and that Situation cite this clause as 610(g)**, one letter above the printed rule, and the body's lettering is used here; **Rule 625(c)** (a goalkeeper's stick left in front of the goal — a minor, with a goal awarded if the puck hits it and prevents an obvious and imminent goal *"regardless as to whether the goalkeeper is on the ice, in the act of leaving the ice or off the ice"*, which is **not** the condition the NHL's and IIHF's Rule 67.5 attaches) with **Casebook Rule 625, Situations 14 to 17** — ⚠️ **14 narrows that "regardless" back**, ruling that *"[f]or a goal to be awarded, the goalkeeper must either be in the act of leaving the ice or actually off the ice"*, so the live divergence from Rule 67.5 is the goalkeeper who is leaving but not yet replaced; **15** removes the intent requirement the rule text writes — *"[i]t makes no difference whether or not the goalkeeper deliberately left their stick in front of the goal"*; including 16's *"It is the responsibility of the goalkeeper to keep the area near their goal free of any obstacles that may prevent the scoring of a goal"*; **Rule 614(c)** — read in full for its four triggers and **read for an exception, of which the rule text has none**, together with the Note defining the *"goalkeeper's privileged"* area — and **Casebook Rule 614(c), Situations 5 to 14**, read in full — they supply the exceptions the rule text does not, answering it through the glossary's *"act of playing goal"*, and they go both ways: **7, 8, 10 and 13** answer *no penalty*, **6 and 9** answer *penalty*, **11** answers both (on whether the goalkeeper is being pressured) and **12** answers both (on whether the puck is inside the privileged area and in front of the goal line, with the minor otherwise *"must be assessed"*); **5** answers *no penalty* but on the three-second rule at a delayed penalty — Rule 614(c)(1) with the Note to Rule 409(a) — rather than on the act of playing goal; and **14** answers that the privileged area *"extends to the closer end boards"*. **Note what 8, 10 and 12 have in common: none of them requires crease contact** — and **Casebook Rule 407, Situations 12 and 13** — 13 answering that a goalkeeper *"whose skates are beyond the center red line"* may not play a puck still on their own side, because *"the determining factor is the position of the goalkeeper's skates and not the puck"*, and 12 answering *no penalty* where the puck strikes a goalkeeper accidentally over the line; and **Casebook Rule 615, Situations 10, 26 and 27** — 26 answering *no penalty* for a goalkeeper leaving a crease-side altercation *"to remain out of the altercation"*, with the minor still assessed where the referee gave no permission and the goalkeeper became involved outside the crease; 27 answering *"Yes"* to going to the bench when the fight is at the crease; and 10 assessing *"a minor penalty for leaving the crease, plus a game misconduct for being the first to intervene in an altercation"* on *"Rule References 615(a, d and e)"* — applying 615(d) rather than supplying it; and Casebook Rule 610, Situation 3, on when a goalkeeper may freeze the puck; and **Casebook Rule 605, Situation 5**, under which a goalkeeper handed a regular skater's stick may legally play with it *"[a]s long as the stick does not exceed the maximum measurements set forth for goalkeeper sticks"*; and, for goalkeeper contact, **Casebook Rule 607, Situations 4 and 6**, read 1 September 2026 — Situation 4's three rungs (*"no penalty should be assessed"* only *"[i]n the case where a defending player has forced the attacking player into their own goalkeeper"* and they have *"clearly made every attempt to avoid the contact with the goalkeeper"*; *"a minor plus misconduct penalty is deemed appropriate"* on an honest attempt once contact is unavoidable; *"the major plus game misconduct, or match penalty option"* where the player makes no attempt and accelerates through, extends the arms or drops the shoulder) and Situation 6 (*"The goalkeeper's Privileged Area extends to the closer end boards"*); and, for the crease, **Casebook Rule 625, Situations 7, 9, 10 and 11** — Situation 11 answering *"Yes"* to whether the official should stop play and face off outside the attacking zone, Situation 10 answering *"No"* for a stick or skate placed *momentarily* in the crease *"unless they interfere with the goalkeeper in any manner"*, and **Situation 9** supplying the limit on the goalkeeper-out carve-out that the rule text does not print — the goal stands where the goalkeeper is *"completely out of their crease"*, but *"if the goalkeeper is attempting to return to the goal crease and there is any interference by the attacking player who prevents the goalkeeper from making a play, then the goal must be disallowed and an interference penalty shall be assessed"* — with **Appendix II, Summary of Face-Off Locations**, which lists *"625(b) Goal crease violation"* under Neutral Zone Spot. The volume's seventeen Rule 625 situations were read in full for a goaltender-vision provision and contain none · [Hockey Canada Playing Rules 2026-2028 (PDF)](https://cdn.hockeycanada.ca/hockey-canada/Hockey-Programs/Officiating/Downloads/2026-28-hc-rulebook-e.pdf) — Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a), the five-part official interpretation of goaltenders freezing the puck, **Rule 10.1(v)**, the delay-of-game limb written for goaltenders alone — *"A goaltender who shoots or bats the puck directly out of the playing surface, with their stick"* — with no zone restriction and no *"deliberately"* of its own, where limbs (i), (ii), (iv) and (vi) each carry the word and Rule 10.1's opening sentence carries it for the rule as a whole; the book does not say which governs; with **10.1(d)**, *"No Misconduct penalty may be assessed for Delay of Game"*, and **10.1(e)**, *"No Game Misconduct penalty may be assessed for Delay of Game"*, which is why the after-the-whistle escalation is not in Rule 10.1 at all; **Rule 11.1 Unsportsmanlike Conduct**, read in full 1 September 2026 — clause **(v)**, *"Shooting the puck out of the playing area, after the whistle"*, and clause **(iv)**, *"Shooting the puck at or near the goal after the whistle"*, among the listed acts, with **11.1(a)** a Minor or Bench Minor, **11.1(b)** and **(c)** barring a Major and a Match, **11.1(d)(i)** a Misconduct for a player who *"[p]ersists in Unsportsmanlike Conduct, after receiving a Minor penalty under Rule 11.1 (a)"*, and **11.1(e)** a Game Misconduct for persisting after either, whose **Note 1** adds that *"[a] Referee is not required to assess a Minor or Misconduct penalty under this rule before assessing a Game Misconduct penalty, but may assess either penalty initially"*, read with the parallel **Note 1 to 11.1(d)**, *"A Referee is not required to assess a Minor penalty under this rule before assessing a Misconduct penalty, but may assess either penalty initially"* — **so neither the Misconduct nor the Game Misconduct is gated on the rung beneath it**, and 11.1(d)(i)'s *"after receiving a Minor penalty"* describes the ordinary case rather than a precondition; **Rule 10.1(vi)**, the goaltender's netting limb, restated at **Rule 6.12(d)**, with **6.12(b)** (deliberately making the puck *"unplayable in order to gain a stoppage of play"*) and **6.12(c)** (play continues where it *"comes off without any delay"*); **Rule 8.3(a) – Interference**, whose second paragraph assesses a minor to *"any player that deliberately piles snow inside their goal crease or is observed by the Referee to have deliberately placed an object in their defending zone at or near the goal"* and whose third escalates to a **penalty shot**, or to an awarded goal once the goaltender has been legally substituted, where the object *"actually prevents a goal"*, with **Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.3(a)**, *"It is the responsibility of the goaltender to keep the area near their net free of any obstacles that may prevent a goal from being scored"*, and **Rule 4.11(a)(iv)**, which lists that penalty shot for a goaltender who *"deliberately piles snow or other obstacles at or near their net"*; **Rule 4.12 Awarded Goals**, with **Interpretation 4 to Rule 4.12(a) and 4.12(b)** — which puts the stick left across the crease by a goaltender on the way to the bench and answers it in two parts, a penalty shot *"while the goaltender is still in the act of leaving the ice"* and an awarded goal once off — and **4.12(b)**, which deems the goaltender *"substituted"* once a legal change has occurred *"despite the fact that the goaltender may still be physically on the ice"*. ⚠️ **Hockey Canada is the only one of the four books that does not price piled snow as delay of game, and the only one that reaches a penalty shot for it.** ⚠️ **Hockey Canada cites that Roman-numeral list both ways in its own pages** — *"Rule 10.1 (a)(vii)"* in one cross-reference and *"Rule 10.1 (iv)"* in another — so the short form used here is one of the book's own two rather than a shortening invented for this document; the list itself is printed above clause (a), under Rule 10.1's opening paragraph, while the Interpretations that gloss it are all headed *"Rule 10.1 (a)"*, **Rule 4.13(c)**, which reaches a goaltender who *"intentionally participates in the play in any manner when they are beyond the centre red line"* and carries no penalty of its own, sending the offence to **Rule 8.3(a) – Interference**, with its **Interpretation 2**: *"The determining factor is the position of the goaltender's skates and not the position of the puck. Assess the goaltender a Minor penalty for Rule 8.3 (a) – Interference."*; **Rule 8.5(b)**, *"A Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed to any player who charges the goaltender"*, read 1 September 2026 with **8.5(d)**, *"No Misconduct penalty may be assessed for interference with the goaltender"*, **Rule 7.10(a)**, whose second limb assesses *"A Minor penalty … to a goaltender who leaves their crease during a fight"*, with **Interpretation 1**, under which the goaltender may proceed to the bench *"[w]ith the permission of the Referee"* and takes the minor if they go *"on their own accord"* (and **7.10(d)**, requiring goaltenders to *"stay in their goal creases or proceed to a neutral zone designated by the Referee"*, which is Hockey Canada's counterpart of the NHL's and the IIHF's neutral-location carve-out), **Rule 7.10(e)(ii)**, the game misconduct on a player who *"joins in a fight or acts as a peacemaker"*, read with the **Glossary** definition that settles that it reaches a goaltender — *"The goaltender is considered a player, except where explicitly stated because special rules or privileges apply to them"* — **Rule 3.2 (Lost or Broken Sticks)** — 3.2(c), which bars a goaltender from playing on with a broken stick of any kind and allows a handed player's stick until the next stoppage, 3.2(d) routing the penalty to Rule 10.6(a), and Interpretation 1 to 3.2(c) on carrying rather than sliding the replacement, and **Rule 8.5 Interference with the Goaltender** (in Section 8, Restraining Fouls) with its Interpretations 1, 2 and 3 — the crease bar **and the carve-out that follows it**, under which a goal scored by a teammate outside the crease *"WILL BE ALLOWED"* if the player in the crease stays passive; Interpretation 3's test of a crease player who attempts *"to play the puck, interfere with the play, or impede the goaltender's vision or movements"*; the *"onus is always on the attacking player"* sentence; the definition of the foul as impeding the goaltender *"by actual physical contact"*; the narrow push-in exception; and the mandatory penalty on an attacker who is fouled into the goaltender and makes no effort to avoid them. Also **8.5(a)–(e)**, the penalty tiers: the minor at (a); at **(b)** a major and game misconduct at the referee's discretion *"based on the degree of violence of impact"* and mandatorily — *"will be assessed"* — for a player who *"charges the goaltender"* (Note 1 referring the definition to Rule 7.4(b) Interpretation 2) and for one who *"injures an opponent by an Interference with the Goaltender infraction that would otherwise call for a Minor penalty"*; at **(c)** a match penalty for attempting to injure or deliberately injuring a goaltender by interference; at **(d)** the bar on any misconduct; at **(e)** the automatic game misconduct on any 8.5(b) major. **Rule 2.2(i)**, *"When both teams are on the ice during the pre-game warm-up, players will be restricted to their own half of the ice"*, and **Rule 2.2(j)**, *"The Referee will penalize any player who commits a violation of the rules during the pre-game warm-up. The penalty will be served at the start of the game"*; **Rule 6.18 Goaltender Safety**, read 2 September 2026 — 6.18(a)'s *"In categories of U13 and below should a Goaltender be struck in the head by the puck the Referee shall stop play as soon as it is safe to do so"* and its instruction that officials *"should always allow team staff to evaluate the goaltender during a stoppage under this rule … to allow for concussion protocols be applied"*, with **Interpretation 2**, under which *"the goaltender's head refers to the protective mask / helmet and does NOT include any throat protectors that may be affixed to or hanging from the mask or helmet"*; and **Rule 3.6(c)**, which requires a BNQ-certified throat protector *"for players registered in minor and female hockey"* and states the dangler point outright: *"Goaltenders who wear an attachment to the mask or helmet designed to protect the throat, must still wear a BNQ-certified throat protector."* Read in full for a goaltender-vision provision: the only two in the book reach a player **in** the crease · [Rule 27 Puts Goaltenders in Their Place — Scouting The Refs](https://scoutingtherefs.com/2017/01/16841/rule-27-puts-goaltenders-place/) · [IIHF Official Rule Book 2025/26 (PDF)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/contentimages/3_the_iihf/2025-26_iihf_rulebook_22122025-v1.pdf) — **Rule 10.4** (Broken Stick / Abandoned Stick – Goalkeeper), **Rule 75 Unsportsmanlike Conduct** in the 2026/27 edition, read in full 1 September 2026 across 75.2 to 75.5 for a puck-out-of-the-playing-area limb, **of which the IIHF book has none** — its 75.4(II) misconduct reaches *"any equipment"*, not the puck — and **Rule 69.1**, whose crease-stoppage sentence — the referee's *"possibility to stop the game"* where an attacking Player *"is in the goal crease and does not leave immediately"* — has no counterpart anywhere in NHL Rules 69.1 to 69.8, and reads word for word the same in the IIHF's 2026/27 edition · [IIHF – New Rule Book for global game](https://www.iihf.com/en/events/2021/wm/news/26308/new_rule_book_for_global_game) — the IIHF's own announcement, 5 June 2021, that it would *"introduce a new Rule Book for the 2021/22 season"*, listing among the rules to be integrated *"A restricted area for goalkeepers behind the net to create more offensive play (i.e. trapezoid familiar to NHL and KHL)"*. **It is the source for the IIHF's 2021-22 adoption date, and the only support held here for the KHL** — a governing body's aside rather than a KHL rule, and no KHL rulebook was available to check against. **Nothing held here dates the NHL's own adoption**; the NHL book gives no date in Rules 1.8, 27.8 or 63.2(viii).*

*UK rule set — the base book for every level of British hockey: [IIHF Official Rule Book 2025/26](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/contentimages/3_the_iihf/2025-26_iihf_rulebook_22122025-v1.pdf) — **Rule 5.1** (*"any reference to 'Player' shall refer to both skaters and goalkeepers"*), **Rule 27.1** (Minor Penalty to Goalkeeper, the NHL's 27.1 with *"penalty box"* for *"penalty bench"*), Rule 1.7 (Goal Crease), Rule 1.8 and **Rule 27.7** (Goalkeeper's Restricted Area — the trapezoid, and note the number), **Rule 42 Charging**, read 1 September 2026 in the 2026/27 book — 42.1's *"A penalty shall be imposed on a Player who charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within their goal crease"* with **42.2** (minor), **42.3** (major) and **42.4** (major plus game misconduct for reckless endangerment); the word "privileged" appears nowhere in the book, Rule 9.12 (Neck Laceration Protector — mandatory *"For all Players in all categories of IIHF Competition"*, with a minor penalty *"after warning by the Referee"*, and the separate permission that *"A goalkeeper may attach a throat protector to the chin of their facemask"*, which sits alongside the mandate rather than satisfying it), Rule 10.2 (Goalkeeper's Stick, 71 cm paddle), Rule 11.1–11.2 (Goalkeeper's Equipment), Rule 24 (Penalty Shot, including 24.4's awarded goal on a third violation), **Rule 27.5** (Leaving Goal Crease — the altercation minor, both carve-outs, and the bench-permission limb, all in the NHL's words at its 27.6), **Rule 27.6** (Participating in the Play Over the Center Red Line, with the same *"position of the puck"* determining factor as the NHL's 27.7), Rule 27.8 (Infractions – Unique to Goalkeepers, a pointer to Table 13 and nothing more — the pointer reads **Table 11** in the IIHF's 2026/27 edition, whose rows and rule references are identical), Rule 63.2 (Delaying the Game, including **63.2(II)** and **63.2(III)**, the counterparts of the NHL's (ii) and (iii) — **not verbatim the NHL's**: (III) writes *"protective glass"* where the NHL writes *"glass"*, and *"surrounding the Players' bench"* where the NHL writes *"'behind' the players' bench"*, both quoted and compared in the body — and 63.2(VIII)), **Rule 63.2(I), (VI) and (VII)**, read 1 September 2026 and **identical in the 2025/26 v1.0, v1.1 and 2026/27 books** — 63.2(I)'s *"On any Player, including the goalkeeper, who holds, 'freezes' or plays the puck… Regarding to a goalkeeper, this rule applies outside of their goal crease area"*, 63.2(VI)'s Note and 63.2(VII)'s *"unless they are actually being checked by an opponent"* — read with **Appendix IV Table 13**, which prints *"Playing puck outside restricted area"* (1, 27.7, 63.2 (VIII)) and *"Deliberately 'freezes' the puck inside or outside the goal crease"* (63.2 (I), (VI) & (VII), 67.3 (I) & (II)) as **two separate rows**, which is what settles that England Ice Hockey's Rule 22.3 suspension of the restricted area does not reach the freezing rules; ⚠️ **the *Rules & Regulations* and both editions of the In-House Rules were searched for any provision touching Rule 63 or the freezing of the puck, and none contains one**, **Rule 51.3** (a blocker punch — *"a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty … and the action is likely to result in injury to the opponent"*, where the NHL writes a match penalty for an attempt to injure), **Appendix IV Table 13** (Summary of Goalkeeper Penalties — *"Deliberately shoots or bats puck out of play"* against **63.2 (II)**, and the blocker punch against 51.3; **Table 11** in the 2026/27 edition, same rows), Rule 63.8 (No Line Change), Rule 64.1 (the goalkeeper embellishment clause, in the same words as the NHL's), Rule 67.3–67.5 (Handling Puck, including **67.3(IV)** and **67.3(V)** — the pads-and-net and the snow-and-obstacles minors, the NHL's sentences with *"their"* for *"his"*), **Rule 85.6**, the separate delay-of-game minor on a goalkeeper who *"deliberately"* drops the puck on the goal netting, and **Rule 85.2 Puck Unplayable**, whose three-second netting stoppage and whose *"Should the goalkeeper use their stick or glove to 'freeze' the puck on the back of the net…"* face-off sentence carry no penalty, Rule 69 (Interference on the Goalkeeper, including **69.2**'s minor-or-major range and **69.3**'s "appropriate penalty" for refusing to give ground, where the NHL's 69.3 writes a minor), **Appendix IV Table 16** (Interference on the Goalkeeper situations — 5C and 5E, both answered *"Goal is allowed"*; and **6B, 6D and 6F–6H** on a goalkeeper who establishes position in the crease; the same table is **Table 14** in the IIHF's 2026/27 edition, where those rows were read in full and carry identical wording and identical answers), Rule 81.1 and Rule 81.3–81.6 (Icing — 81.1's *"any Player"*, and 81.5 read in full for a goalkeeper exemption that is not there), Rule 84.4 (Shootout Procedure), **Rule 86.6 Pre-Game Warm-Up**, read 2 September 2026 — *"each team shall confine its activity to its own end of the rink"*, cross-referencing *"Rule 46.10 – Fighting Prior the Drop of the Puck"*, with violations *"reported to the Proper Authorities"*; and **Rule 5.6 Pre-Game Warm-Up on the Ice**, *"No penalties are to be assessed but a report of the incident must be submitted to the Proper Authorities"* — word for word in the 2025/26 v1.1 and 2026/27 editions · [IIHF Official Rule Book 2026/27 (Version 1.0, June 2026)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/downloads/rule%20book/2026-27_iihf_rule_book.pdf) — the current edition, read here for three things only: **Rule 27.8**, whose pointer now reads **Table 11**; **Appendix IV Tables 11 and 14**, the renumbered goalkeeper's-penalties and goalkeeper-interference tables, whose rows were read in full against the 2025/26 book's Tables 13 and 16 and carry identical wording, identical rule references and identical answers; and **Rule 69.1**, whose crease-stoppage sentence is word for word the 2025/26 book's. **Nothing else in it has been read for this document**, so the rest of the IIHF layer here is the 2025/26 book's · [IIHF Situation Handbook 2025/26 (Version 1.1, August 2025) (PDF)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/downloads/officiating%20files/situation%20handbook/2025_iihf_situationhandbook_17082025-v1_1.pdf) — Situations 27.5, 27.6, 27.7, 27.9, 27.10 and 27.11 (restricted area and the centre red line), **63.29** (a stick save deflected over the glass — *"No. The goalkeeper was simply making a save and did not 'shoot' the puck over the glass"*, identical in the 2026/27 edition), 63.33 (freeze after a dump-in), 69.1, 69.2, 69.3 and 69.5 (crease screens, giving ground, the glove in the face, and the penalty that falls on the goalkeeper), **67.5** (a goalkeeper who leaves the stick in front of the net and is still on the way off — *"Since the goalkeeper is still on the ice you cannot award a goal only a minor penalty"*, identical in the 2026/27 edition), 81.5 (what waves off an icing) · [IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27 (Version 1.0, June 2026) (PDF)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/downloads/officiating%20files/situation%20handbook/2026-27_iihf_situation_handbook.pdf) — **Situation 10.26** (a lost or broken stick propelled at the puck, and the goal disallowed only on intent) and the Foreword's *"in case of any contradictions, the valid IIHF Rulebook is always the basis."* Situation 10.26 has no counterpart in the 2025/26 Handbook, whose Rule 10 situations end at 10.25; **of the other Situations cited in this document, only 27.5, 27.6, 27.7, 27.9, 27.10, 27.11 and 67.5 have been re-checked against the 2026/27 edition — all seven are unchanged there, and still answer with "Rule 27.8" against the Rule Book's 27.7; the remainder have not been re-checked**, and the rest of the IIHF layer here is 2025/26 · [IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27, Revision 1 (published 25/07/26)](https://englandicehockey.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/08/IHUK-In-House-Rules-2026-2027.pdf) — **the current British document**: its Introduction, which adopts the IIHF Rule Book *"Version 1.1, published July 2025"* (the 2025/26 book), applies it *"in England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland (the Home Countries)"* alongside the BUIHA, calls the In-House Rules *"the overriding interpretation of how the IIHF Rule Book is to be applied domestically"*, and names no Situation Handbook; **Rule 9.12** (neck laceration protectors and their enforcement, *"mandatory for all players in all IIHF categories and IHUK/EIH/SIH competitions"*); Rule 37 and Rule 38 (no video review, no coach's challenge); the Rule 69.1 amendment; Rule 84 (overtime is not universal); and the section of rules applying specifically to BUIHA games · [IHUK In-House Rules 2025-26, Revision 1](https://englandicehockey.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/IHUK-In-House-Rules-25-26-R1.pdf) — superseded, and read alongside the current edition only to establish what changed. Full amendment list and the rest of the British layer: [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md)* ⚠️ The Handbook is linked above as the versioned PDF because the IIHF's officiating hub page (`iihf.com/en/statichub/officiating`), where it used to be reached, no longer resolves — it answers HTTP 200 with the IIHF's own error page (re-checked 29 August 2026). The Handbook itself is unaffected.

*Technique and terminology: [USA Hockey Goaltending – Goalie Terms That Really Count (Mitch Korn)](https://www.usahockeygoaltending.com/news_article/show/524949-goalie-terms-that-really-count) — source for challenging, square, front/back door, telescope, T-push, shuffle, half butterfly, paddle down, cushioning, five- and six-hole, Y-theory, flow, and cutting down the angle — *"The three steps are (1) being out … (2) being square … (3) being set (stationary if possible)"* (note: Korn numbers those steps but does not rank them, and his shuffle definition specifies no skate edge) · [BVHS Goalie Coaches Tactics and Skill Development Resource Guide, updated May 2026 (PDF)](https://bvhs.ca/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/BVHS-Goalie-Tactics-Manual-2026-2027-Season.pdf) — quoted verbatim for the shuffle on flats, the RVH off-post anchor at 45 degrees, the "close gap" school on screens, the breakaway checklist ("chest and hips, not their stick or eyes"), the refusal to sort goalies by style ("not to create identical goalies or force athletes into one specific style"), sacrificing depth only against a back-door or breakaway-deke threat, and — in its *How to Warm-up* chapter, read 2 September 2026 — the warm-up claim quoted in *The load goaltending puts on your hips* (*"The intense butterfly drops and side-to-side pushes require loose muscles. Jumping straight into live shots without a sweat drastically increases the risk of pulled groin or hip flexor muscles"*) together with the eight items of its *Goalie Pre-Practice Checklist*. ⚠️ **That warm-up claim is a coaching manual's, not a study's, and the manual attaches no figure to it** — searched in the full text, it contains no injury rate, no incidence figure and no reference to the adductors, and none has been supplied here. The measured finding it is set beside — proximal adductor injuries as the largest injury category and the largest source of days missed among NHL goaltenders (Keshen and colleagues, 2025, *American Journal of Sports Medicine*) — is [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md#groin-and-adductor-strain--the-classic-hockey-injury)'s, and its numbers, its population and its caveats stay there · [USA Hockey Goaltending – In the Crease: Controlling Rebounds (Joe Bertagna)](https://www.usahockeygoaltending.com/news_article/show/524951-in-the-crease-controlling-rebounds-physical-and-mental-skills) — source for the rebound priority order and the soft stick · [USA Hockey – Five Tips for Adult League Goalies](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/1035363-five-tips-for-adult-league-goalies) — hands out front, playing within the crease, the shuffle, the "cone of shame" · [IIHF Coaching – Butterfly Slide](https://www.iihf.com/en/coaching/19519/butterfly-slide) · [Butterfly style — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butterfly_style) — VH definition, butterfly recovery, and how the terms profly, hybrid and stand-up are used. ⚠️ **Used for terminology only.** Its sentence calling the profly *"the style most commonly used and taught"* cites a 2004 NHL.com coaching Q&A that does not contain the claim or the word "profly", so no statement about which style dominates rests on it here · [Goaltender — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goaltender)*

*Post play and modern practice: [InGoal Magazine – How Sweden Invented the RVH](https://ingoalmag.com/2018/06/29/how-sweden-invented-the-rvh/) — Linda Blomquist and Daniel Henriksson, Luleå, 2010, and the three problems with VH · [InGoal Magazine – Why Tuukka Rask Uses Shin on Post for Reverse VH](https://ingoalmag.com/2020/12/30/pro-tips-why-tuukka-rask-uses-shin-on-post-for-reverse-vh/) — Bob Essensa on seal points and getting stuck · [InGoal Magazine – Brady Robinson: When to Prioritize Depth on Short-Side Shots](https://ingoalmag.com/2021/09/04/prioritizing-depth-over-angle/) · [InGoal Magazine – The Modern Science of Goalie Tracking and Object Interception](https://ingoalmag.com/2023/06/08/the-modern-understanding-of-tracking/) — tracking as object interception, smooth pursuits and saccades*

*Coaching and the mental side: [USA Hockey Goaltending – In the Crease: Spotting Confidence Problems (Mitch Korn)](https://www.usahockeygoaltending.com/news_article/show/524945-in-the-crease-spotting-confidence-problems-with-goaltenders) · [USA Hockey – Coaching the Crease (Kevin Reiter, Jeff Blashill)](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/466357-coaching-the-crease) — "set, then shoot", and goaltending as much a mental position as a technical one · [USA Hockey – Tips on Coaching Goalies (Corey Schwab, Kevin Reiter)](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/311167-tips-on-coaching-goalies) — why dropping automatically fails for smaller goalies, and Schwab on wanting young goalies *"to learn how to react and not just drop and hope they stop the puck"**

*Communication: [On-Ice Communication](../foundation/on_ice_communication.md) and its sources, including [The Coaches Site – Goalie Breakout Calls (Kelvin Cech)](https://members.thecoachessite.com/article/goalie-breakout-calls-1)*

*Helmets and face masks — the four books read in the primary text on 1 September 2026: [NHL Official Rules 2025-2026 (PDF)](https://media.d3.nhle.com/image/private/t_document/prd/slwjuaqwmuvj5bkplixo.pdf) **Rule 9.6** — the lost-mask stoppage and its possession condition, the minor for removing the mask *"in order to secure a stoppage of play"*, and the awarded goal where it is done *"when the opposing team is on a breakaway, during the course of a penalty shot or shootout attempt"* — read with **Rule 25.4**, which lists the awarded-goal infractions during a penalty shot and **does not repeat the mask clause**, and **Rule 22.1** (a misconduct is ten minutes) · [IIHF Official Rule Book 2025/26 (PDF)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/contentimages/3_the_iihf/2025-26_iihf_rulebook_22122025-v1.pdf) **Rule 9.6**, the same two limbs with the breakaway qualified as one meeting the penalty-shot criteria *"except for a foul from behind"*, **identical in the 2026/27 edition**, and **Rule 25.4**, which does carry the clause — *"or removes their face mask to force an interruption"* · [USA Hockey Official Playing Rules 2025-29 (PDF)](https://cdn2.sportngin.com/attachments/document/945a-3442848/2025-29_USAH_Playing_Rules.pdf) **Rule 304(e)** (the immediate stoppage in all classifications, the goalkeeper not ruled off before the face-off, and the delay-of-game minor reaching *"a goalkeeper or skater"*), **Rule 610(e)**, and **Rule 404(a)** for the length of a misconduct, plus the sled-hockey goalkeeper equipment note in the Disabled Hockey section · [Hockey Canada Playing Rules 2026-2028 (PDF)](https://cdn.hockeycanada.ca/hockey-canada/Hockey-Programs/Officiating/Downloads/2026-28-hc-rulebook-e.pdf) **Rule 3.6(d)** — the goaltender stoppage, its imminent-scoring-chance exception, its equipment list reaching the blocker, trapper and skate blade, and the delay-of-game routing for deliberate removal — with **Rule 4.11(a)(iii)** and **Interpretation 11 to Rules 10.6(a) and 3.6**. ⚠️ **Two negative statements in that passage were established by search, not assumed.** That neither the NHL nor the IIHF writes a last-two-minutes or overtime limb rests on reading every occurrence of *"helmet"*, *"face mask"* and *"facemask"* in both books: in the NHL book the goalkeeper provision is Rule 9.6 alone, the rest being Rule 46.6 (helmets before a fight), Rule 51 (roughing, for removing an **opponent's** helmet) and the reference tables; in the IIHF book, Rules 9.6, 24.5, 25.4, 42, 46.12 and 51. That USA Hockey publishes no interpretation of 610(e) rests on reading all eleven Casebook Situations printed under Rule 610, none of which mentions a helmet or a facemask. **The lettering used for 610(e) is the printed body's**, per the convention flagged earlier in this document; the Casebook prints the same clause under the same letter. ⚠️ **A fifth book was searched for the same stoppage and writes none:** [CARHA Hockey Official Rule Book, printed 2020 (PDF)](https://carhahockey.ca/wp-content/uploads/2024/01/RuleBk-2020-interactive-1.pdf), read 2 September 2026 — its **Rule 24(f)** stops play *"[w]hen a goalkeeper loses their helmet"*, its **Rule 24(d)** requires that *"[g]oalkeepers must still wear a full face mask"* — the single occurrence of the word *mask* in the book — and its remaining face-protection clauses (**23(d)**, removal for identification; **24(d)**'s Notes, on CSA certification and insurance) are about what is worn, not about what a shot does to it. The phrases "hard shot", "dazed", "struck in the head", "head by the puck" and "hit on the face" return nothing from it, and it has no disabled-hockey section for a clause to sit in, as USA Hockey's does. ⚠️ **What every one of the five books does have is a general injured-player stoppage that reaches a goalkeeper, and the text above now says so.** **CARHA Rule 20(d)** — *"When a player is injured so that they cannot continue play or go to their bench, the play shall be stopped immediately"* — with **Note 1** adding the widest discretion of the five, *"Where there is suspicion that a player has sustained an injury, the Referee may stop the play immediately"* (no *serious*, no *obvious*); it reaches a goalkeeper on the book's own definition of **Players** — *"Except where special rules apply, the goalkeeper is to be considered as one of the players"* — corroborated by Rule 20(a)'s *"a player, other than a goalkeeper"* and Note 2's *"excluding the goalkeeper"*, both carve-outs that only make sense if the word otherwise includes one. **NHL Rule 8.1** and **IIHF Rule 8.1** stop play for a player *"injured so that he cannot continue play or go to his bench"* and allow an immediate stoppage where *"it is obvious that a player has sustained a serious injury"*, and each book's **Rule 8.2** presupposes the result — *"When play has been stopped by the Referee or Linesperson due to an injured goalkeeper"*. **USA Hockey Rule 206(a)** is the same, its *"A player other than a goalkeeper, whose injury appears serious enough to warrant the stoppage of play"* being a carve-out from a consequence rather than from the stoppage. **Hockey Canada Rule 2.4(b)** likewise, with **Note 1** at *"suspicion that a player has sustained serious injury"* and **Note 2** excluding the goaltender only from leaving the ice. ⚠️ **None of these is written about a mask**, and that is the whole point: each asks an official to judge that a player is hurt, which is what a goalkeeper behind a cage supplies least evidence for — so the distinction that matters is the **trigger**, not whether a stoppage exists. An earlier reading of this passage recorded only the two Notes, called them discretionary and concluded that the books wrote no stoppage; that was too narrow on both counts, because the operative clauses of 8.1, 206(a), 2.4(b) and 20(d) are mandatory once their threshold is met. **CARHA governs CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only.***

*Caveats and known gaps: (1) The trapezoid's width at the goal line is given differently in **NHL** Rule 27.8 (six feet from each post, stale pre-2014 wording) and NHL Rule 1.8 (eight feet from each post, the markings actually painted). The dimensions used here follow Rule 1.8. **The IIHF book has no equivalent defect** — its Rules 1.8 and 27.7 both give 6.80 m / 8.60 m. **A separate numbering trap replaces it for UK readers**, and it is flagged in the text: the IIHF numbers the restricted area **27.7** and the centre-red-line rule **27.6**, one below the NHL's 27.8 and 27.7, while IIHF 27.8 is merely a pointer to Table 13. **The IIHF's own two publications disagree, in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions alike**: the Rule Book and its goalkeeper-penalties table use 27.7, and the Situation Handbook still answers with 27.8 throughout Situations 27.6–27.11. The Rule Book's numbering is used here. **A second, purely clerical trap sits on top of it.** The IIHF renumbered its Appendix IV tables for 2026/27, merging three of them, so the goalkeeper-penalties table is **Table 13** in the 2025/26 book and **Table 11** in the 2026/27 one, and the goaltender-interference situations table is **Table 16** and **Table 14** respectively. The rows quoted in this document were read in both editions and are identical; the numbering used here is the 2025/26 book's, which is the edition the rest of this document's IIHF layer follows. (2) The interaction between Rule 63.2(vii) and Rule 67.3(ii) on freezing the puck inside the crease is my reading; the NHL publishes no interpretation of it. **The IIHF narrows the gap in the rule text itself** — its 67.3(I) reads "checked / pressured" where the NHL's reads only "checked" — though it does not repeat the word in 63.2(VII), so the tension is reduced rather than removed. Hockey Canada (Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a)) and USA Hockey (Rule 610(b) with its Note, and Casebook Rule 610, Situation 3) both publish official interpretations of the same question under their own rulebooks, and both adopt the "under pressure" / "act of playing goal" test the text describes; those are quoted where the point is made. (3) The goalie's "shot" call **is** documented in a published coaching source — Sportmentary's goalie communication guide, quoted in full where the call appears above and already cited in [On-Ice Communication](../foundation/on_ice_communication.md). The sourced meaning is "I have the shooter", not a warning that a shot is coming. (4) The claim that goaltending is the most psychologically demanding position in team sport is widely repeated, with no study behind it, and it is flagged as opinion in the text. (5) Whether deliberately blocking a rimmed puck with your body counts as "playing the puck" is **still my reading under the NHL book**, which does not define the term and publishes no interpretation. **It is no longer an open question under the IIHF book, which is the one that governs British play:** IIHF Situation Handbook Situation 27.7 puts the case of a goalkeeper who "drops down on the knees and blocks the pass using the leg pads" outside the designated area and answers that the goalkeeper "plays" the puck and takes the minor. The conservative reading in the text is therefore the published answer under IIHF rules and an inference under NHL rules, and it is labelled that way where it appears. (6) Rule 10.2 sets the maximum paddle length for a goalkeeper granted a height exemption at the same 26 inches as the ordinary limit, which makes the exemption inoperative as written; this appears to be stale wording and is flagged where it appears. (7) The characterisation of RVH as near-universal at professional level comes from goaltending-coaching sources, not from a survey. (8) No time limit on a penalty shot or a shootout attempt exists in NHL Rule 24 or Rule 84.4, **nor in IIHF Rule 24, whose only timing provision (24.7) excludes the shot from playing time rather than capping it** — ignore the "twenty seconds" figure that is commonly quoted, which appears in none of them. (9) **The EIHL Casebook has not been read**, so nothing here describes Elite League interpretation. The IHUK In-House Rules make no goalkeeper-specific amendment to the playing rules beyond the neck-protector enforcement and the removal of video review and coach's challenges — verified by reading the 2026-27 In-House Rules document in full, as the superseded 2025-26 edition had been. The one other goalkeeper-specific line in the book is administrative and sits in Appendix 1's overtime procedures, where *"Goalkeepers may go to their bench during the rest period; penalised players must remain in the penalty box."* **The one provision worth naming as *not* an exception** is In-House Rule 10.3, new for 2026-27, which bars skating with a broken stick and requires a player to drop it immediately: it is headed *"Broken Stick: Player"* and the goalkeeper's broken-stick rule is IIHF 10.4, which the In-House Rules leave alone — so on the wording the goalkeeper's play-on permission survives, and if your league reads it otherwise, ask before you rely on it. So every other IIHF rule quoted here applies unamended in EIH and SIHA competition **so far as the In-House Rules go — which is not the whole British picture.** England Ice Hockey's *Rules & Regulations* is a second document, and its Rule 22.3 suspends the Goalkeepers' Restricted Area, so silence in the In-House Rules is not by itself evidence that a rule is unamended in Britain. The Rules & Regulations have been read for the trapezoid and the neck protector, not end to end. Revisions and Rule Bulletins are issued in-season; check for a later one before relying on a detail. (10) **Nothing in the sources listed above counts goals by cause, ranks goaltending errors by frequency, or measures how often a call gets answered.** Where this document says a mistake is common — the reflex butterfly, screening your own goalie, an unanswered 'screen' call — that is coaching observation, and it is labelled as such where it appears. **Note the limit on that statement:** none of the goaltending sources above is held as searchable text, so it could not be re-checked by search in either direction. The counts of goals by cause this document can point to are not in goaltending sources at all: [Game Management](../systems/game_management.md) counts the tip, deflection and rebound share of goals scored with an extra attacker on the ice, and [Offensive Zone Play](../systems/offensive_zone_play.md) counts the share of all goals arriving within three seconds of a preceding shot, which is a proxy for the rebound. Both read the NHL play-by-play feed, and that feed carries **no marker for a screen** — so the net-front cause this document leans on hardest is precisely the one that cannot be counted from it. (11) **That NHL and IIHF Rule 63.2(iii) reaches a goalkeeper is an inference from Rule 5.1's definition of "player", not a statement either book makes about goalkeepers.** Its sibling limb 63.2(ii) *is* stated of goalkeepers outright, in both books' goalkeeper tables (NHL Reference Table 11, eighth row; IIHF Table 13, naming Rule 63.2(II)); no table and no situation held here does the same for (iii). The inference is the one the books' own drafting invites — Rule 63.2(i) names goalkeepers in, Rule 63.2(v) names them out, and Rule 63.2(iii) does neither — but it is an inference, and it is labelled as one where it appears. **Hockey Canada Rule 10.1(v), which is written for goaltenders in terms, carries an ambiguity of its own that no source on disk resolves**: the limb has no "deliberately" where four of its siblings do, while Rule 10.1's opening sentence supplies the word for the rule as a whole.*
